NMTA Final Exam Study Book

On the exam examined material Background not examined directly, but needed or tested in the mock Framework to remember learn it as a list or structure (all frameworks)

Week 1 Introduction and Fundamentals I

What the field is: one definition, three branches

On the exam

The lecture gives a working definition you should be able to write from memory: new media technology and analytics studies how digital media and data analytics reshape business, information systems, and marketing. The definition has a cause and three things it affects. The cause is two forces together: digital media (feeds, livestreams, AI content, extended reality) and data analytics (the methods that turn platform logs into decisions). Those forces change three fields, and the course calls them the three branches.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The three branches of new media technology and analytics (3 branches)

  • Business: how firms create and capture value (platform business models; pricing, channels, monetisation).
  • Information systems: the technologies and data infrastructures (platforms and recommendation; data pipelines; AI and AIGC).
  • Marketing: how organisations reach, understand and measure people (consumers, engagement, targeting; campaign measurement).

How to recall it: value, infrastructure, people. "Each field is an entry point, not a fence."

The business branch asks how firms create and capture value. It is made concrete as platform business models, and pricing, channels and monetisation. A typical business question is "how does a livestream platform make money, and who keeps the margin?" The information systems branch covers the technologies and data infrastructures the field is built on: platforms and recommendation, data pipelines, and AI and AI-generated content (AIGC). A typical question is "what does the recommendation system log, and how does that data reach an analyst?" The marketing branch is about how organisations reach, understand, and measure people: consumers, engagement, targeting, and campaign measurement. A typical question is "did this campaign bring buyers, or only views?"

The lecture's debrief makes one point: "each field is an entry point, not a fence." Your background picks your first comfortable week. A business student starts from the cases and the four pillars, an information systems student from data types, pipelines and the method weeks, and a marketing student from the Week 2 frameworks and every campaign case. The course spends the term connecting all three, and that connection is the definition.

Exam tip

If a question gives you a scenario and asks which branch it belongs to, look at the verb. "Capture value, price, monetise" points to business. "Store, log, recommend, build a pipeline" points to information systems. "Reach, target, engage, measure a campaign" points to marketing. Real problems usually touch all three, so name the main branch and say why.

Four technologies, two tracks, and the data each one leaves

On the exam

Four technologies are rewriting marketing, each with a short tag: social media is "scaled attention", livestreaming is "shows with checkout", AI and AIGC is "the invisible curator", and XR is "the next interface". For each one the lecture gives three things: what it is, a China track and an international track of example platforms, and the data it generates. Learn them as a grid, because the exam can ask about any cell.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Four technologies rewriting marketing, each with its tag (4 technologies, each with 2 tracks)

  • Social media: "scaled attention". Users make the content; feeds rank by engagement.
  • Livestreaming: "shows with checkout". Real-time video selling with in-stream checkout.
  • AI and AIGC: "the invisible curator". Feeds decide what you see; AI also makes the content.
  • XR: "the next interface". AR, VR and MR; try-on, virtual stores, gaze and dwell.

How to recall it: for every technology know three cells: what it is, its China track and international track of platforms, and the data it generates.

Social media ("attention at scale"): users make the content, feeds rank it by engagement, and there are 5.7 billion users (2026). The China track is the WeChat super-app, Weibo and RED; the international track is Instagram, TikTok and YouTube. A social platform generates four layers of data: profiles (who the user is), content and engagement (every action, timestamped), transactions (orders and refunds), and network ties (follows and mentions).

Framework to remember

What data a social platform generates (4 layers)

  • Profiles: who the user is.
  • Content and engagement: every action, timestamped.
  • Transactions: orders and refunds.
  • Network ties: follows and mentions.

How to recall it: who they are, what they do, what they buy, whom they know.

Livestreaming ("shows with a checkout button") is real-time video selling with in-stream checkout: you watch a host and buy without leaving the stream. The China track (Taobao Live, Douyin, Kuaishou) is worth "about ¥5 trillion a year"; the international track is TikTok Shop, Amazon Live and Shopee Live. The data trail logs traffic and engagement second by second, commerce per product per minute, and host performance as conversion and gross merchandise value (GMV) per hour. GMV is the total value of the orders placed.

Framework to remember

The livestreaming data trail (3 things logged per stream)

  • Traffic and engagement: logged second by second.
  • Commerce: logged per product, per minute.
  • Host performance: conversion, and gross merchandise value (GMV) per hour.

How to recall it: the crowd, the cart, the host; each at its own time grain.

AI and AIGC plays two roles: as a curator it decides what you see in the feed (curation), and as a producer it makes the content (production). The China track is ByteDance feeds, ERNIE and Doubao, and Kling; the international track is TikTok For You, ChatGPT and Sora. The lecture lists what this changes for analytics: generated content floods the data stream, so authenticity becomes an analytic problem, and detecting generated content is a classification problem (Week 4).

Framework to remember

AI and AIGC: curator and producer (2 roles, 3 consequences for analytics)

  • Curation: feeds decide what you see.
  • Production: AI makes the content.
  • Consequences: generated content floods the data stream; authenticity becomes an analytic problem; detection is a classification problem (Week 4).

XR, or extended reality, covers augmented, virtual and mixed reality (AR, VR, MR). Marketing uses include AR try-on and virtual stores, and the new data are gaze and dwell: where you look and how long you stay. The China track includes virtual influencers (Liu Yexi), AR try-on and metaverse pop-ups; the international track is Sephora and IKEA Place, Vision Pro and Quest, and the virtual influencer Lil Miquela. The table gives the tracks for all four.

TechnologyChina trackInternational trackData it generates
Social mediaWeChat, Weibo, REDInstagram, TikTok, YouTubeprofiles; content and engagement; transactions; network ties
LivestreamingTaobao Live, Douyin, KuaishouTikTok Shop, Amazon Live, Shopee Livetraffic second by second; commerce per product per minute; host conversion and GMV per hour
AI and AIGCByteDance feeds; ERNIE, Doubao; KlingTikTok For You; ChatGPT, Soraa stream mixed with generated content, so authenticity must be detected
XRLiu Yexi; AR try-on; metaverse pop-upsSephora, IKEA Place; Vision Pro, Quest; Lil Miquelagaze and dwell

Why this is "no longer a niche topic": 5.7 billion social media users worldwide, WeChat with 1.4 billion monthly users, and Douyin with 600+ million daily users (a 2020 figure). The four technologies are also not separate markets. In one purchase, a feed surfaces the product (discover), you join the livestream (watch), you check reviews, some of which are AI-written (check), and you use AR try-on and one-tap checkout (try). One purchase leaves data from all four technologies.

Framework to remember

One purchase, four technologies (4 steps, in order)

  1. Discover: a feed surfaces the product (social media).
  2. Watch: you join the livestream (livestreaming).
  3. Check: you check reviews, and some are AI-written (AI and AIGC).
  4. Try: AR try-on, then one-tap checkout (XR).

How to recall it: the four steps follow the four technologies in the same order as the list above.

The five data types and the platform shift

On the exam

Under the four technologies lies a common core of five data types. Behavioural events are the event log: every view, click and like with a time stamp. Text means comments, reviews and danmaku (bullet comments that fly across the video). Networks are follows, mentions and shares. Transactions are orders and refunds. Profiles describe who the user is. Every method week works on one or more of these five, so when you read a case, a good first move is to name which of the five the case data belongs to.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The common core: five data types (5 types)

  • Behavioural events: the event log (views, clicks, likes with time stamps).
  • Text: comments, reviews, danmaku.
  • Networks: follows, mentions, shares.
  • Transactions: orders and refunds.
  • Profiles: who the user is.

How to recall it: my own mnemonic, not a course term: "BTNTP", or think "what they do, say, know, buy, are".

The lecture then asks you to spot the platform shift: think of an app you use daily that did not exist five years ago, decide which shift explains it (mobile, feeds, or creators), and say what it logs that its predecessor could not. The debrief gives a test, which the course calls "the tell", for each shift. The mobile shift shows when sessions last minutes and happen anywhere, and the log carries location and motion. The feeds shift shows when you watch what the algorithm picks, not whom you subscribed to. The creators shift shows when the platform itself produces nothing you consume; all the content comes from users and creators.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Three platform shifts and their tells (3 shifts, plus one decision rule)

ShiftThe tell (the criterion)
Mobilesessions last minutes and happen anywhere; the log carries location and motion
Feedsyou watch what the algorithm picks, not whom you subscribed to
Creatorsthe platform itself produces nothing you consume

The removal test: most new apps carry all three shifts; the primary one is the one whose removal kills the app.

The debrief gives a decision rule. Most apps born in the last five years carry all three shifts, so "all three" is not an answer. The primary shift is the one whose removal kills the app; that is the shift that explains it. As my own illustration: remove the creators from a short-video app and nothing is left to watch; remove the algorithmic feed and it becomes a list of subscriptions. Argue which removal is fatal, then say what new data that shift logs.

Marketing analytics: the ladder and the process

On the exam

Marketing analytics turns footprints into decisions. The footprints are the data (profiles, engagement, transactions, text). The decisions come from four kinds of question, the analytics ladder: describe, diagnose, predict, prescribe. Each rung asks a harder question about the same data. Each rung is explained in detail in the analytics ladder section below. The rest of the week follows a roadmap: the data, the ladder, the metrics, and the tools.

The process has five steps: question, data, prepare, model, decide. The order matters. You start with a business question, not with a dataset, because the question decides which data you need. Then you collect the data, prepare it (see data quality and preprocessing below), model it with a method from Weeks 3 to 8, and finally decide. An analysis that stops at "model" has not done its job.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The analytics process (5 steps, in order)

  1. Question: start from a business question, not from a dataset.
  2. Data: collect the data the question needs.
  3. Prepare: check quality and preprocess (aggregate, discretize, binarize).
  4. Model: apply a method from Weeks 3 to 8.
  5. Decide: turn the result into a decision; stopping at "model" is not enough.

How to recall it: the question comes first and the decision comes last; the data sits between them.

From screen to dataset: objects, attributes and values

On the exam

The lecture's claim is that "a live page is a table". Every box on a livestream page (room, category, followers, popularity, heat index, hours live, rating) is a column waiting to be filled. The running example is six streamers with seven attributes, and "every concept fits in this table".

RoomCategoryFollowersPopularityHeat indexHours liveRating
1Film & TV125KHigh9085
2Gaming100KHigh8045
3Lifestyle70KLow5023
4Lifestyle120KHigh4555
5Gaming95KMid6014
6Music60KLow−502

Two words carry the vocabulary. A dataset is objects (rows) plus attributes (columns). An object is one row, here one streamer. Other books call it a record, case, sample or observation; they all mean the row. An attribute is one column, such as followers or category. Other books call it a variable, field or feature; they all mean the column. To find the object in any table, ask "what does one row describe?"

Two more words complete the vocabulary. A value is the number (or label) assigned to an attribute for one object: the cell where a row meets a column. A scale is the rule that maps a property of the world to a value. The course examples: hours live can be measured in hours or in minutes (one attribute, two measurement rules); room ID is an integer with no minimum and no maximum; followers is an integer with a minimum of 0 and a maximum equal to all platform users. The platform gives you numbers but not the rule behind them, and the rule decides which arithmetic makes sense.

Trap

Do not confuse the object with a value or with a grouping column. In a table with one row per video and columns for creator, length and shares, the object is one video. The share count in a cell is a value, and "creator category" is an attribute you might group by. The mock exam asked exactly this shape of question.

Four measurement scales and the operations ladder

On the exam

There are four measurement scales ("know your numbers"). Each adds one property to the one before it. Nominal values are names: they only tell you whether two things are the same or different (room ID, gender, category). Ordinal values are ranks: they have an order, but the gaps between them have no fixed size (popularity High, Mid, Low; tier Master, Elite, Warrior). Interval values have equal gaps but no true zero (the heat index). Ratio values have equal gaps and a true zero, where zero means "none of it" (followers).

Core framework: learn this by heart

Four measurement scales (4 scales, in order: each adds one property)

  1. Nominal: names; same or different only (room ID, gender, category).
  2. Ordinal: ranks; order but no fixed gaps (popularity, tier).
  3. Interval: equal gaps, no true zero (heat).
  4. Ratio: equal gaps and a true zero (followers, GMV, orders, views).

How to recall it: my own mnemonic, not a course term: "NOIR" (Nominal, Ordinal, Interval, Ratio), in the order of growing power.

The lecture labels every column of an extended streamer table, and you should know these labels because they show the reasoning. Room, gender and category are nominal. Popularity, recommended (Front page, Trending, Not listed) and tier are ordinal. Last live (a date), heat and rating are interval. Followers, resolution and hours live are ratio. The 1 to 5 rating is labelled interval: the course treats each step between stars as equal in size. A date is interval because gaps between dates are meaningful but there is no "zero date". Resolution is ratio if you read it as a count of pixels.

RoomGenderLast liveCategoryFollowersPopularityHeatRecommendedResolutionTierHours liveRating
1Female03-05Film & TV125KHigh90℃Front page4KMaster85
2Female03-01Gaming100KHigh80℃Front page720pElite45
3Female03-05Lifestyle70KLow50℃Trending4KWarrior23
4Female02-27Lifestyle120KHigh45℃Front page1080pElite55
5Male02-21Gaming95KMid60℃Trending1080pWarrior14
6Female02-23Music60KLow−5℃Not listed1080pWarrior02
ScaleNominalIntervalNominalRatioOrdinalIntervalOrdinalRatioOrdinalRatioInterval

Each scale carries different information. Nominal carries labels only (room ID: 1, or a nickname), so you may count and take the mode; "a mean of room IDs is nonsense". Ordinal carries order but not gaps, so you may use the median and percentiles. Interval carries gaps but not ratios: 90℃ − 80℃ is the same gap as 60℃ − 50℃, but 0℃ is not "no heat", and a heat of −5 is possible. So you cannot say a room at 90 is "twice as hot" as a room at 45. Ratio carries both gaps and ratios: 120K followers really is 2 × 60K followers, zero means none, and there are no negatives. Money and counts, such as GMV, orders and views, are ratio data.

The lecture puts this into one table, the operations ladder ("what you may compute"). Each scale inherits everything above it: ratio can do all four operations, nominal only the first.

Framework to remember

The operations ladder: what you may compute (4 scales × 4 operations, in order)

ScaleDistinct (= ≠)Order (< >)Add / subtractMultiply / divide
Nominalyesnonono
Ordinalyesyesnono
Intervalyesyesyesno
Ratioyesyesyesyes

How to recall it: a staircase. "Each scale inherits everything above it; ratio data can do all four; nominal only the first."

A second, separate split concerns how many values are possible. A discrete attribute takes finite or countable values, usually stored as integers: room ID, or the number of words in a comment. A binary attribute is the special discrete case with only two values, 0 and 1. A continuous attribute takes real values with finite precision, stored as floats: the heat value in degrees. Discrete versus continuous is about how many values are possible. Nominal to ratio is about what the values mean. Every attribute has one answer to each question, and the two answers are independent. Room ID is discrete and nominal; followers is discrete and ratio; heat is continuous and interval.

Framework to remember

Discrete and continuous attributes (2 types, plus binary as a special case)

  • Discrete: finite or countable values, stored as integers (room ID; words in a comment).
  • Binary: the special discrete case with only 0 and 1.
  • Continuous: real values with finite precision, stored as floats (heat value in ℃).

How to recall it: this split is independent of the four scales. Ask two questions of every attribute: how many values, and what they mean.

Trap

Storage type does not decide the scale. Room IDs are stored as integers, but they are nominal, so the computer will happily give you a "mean room ID" that means nothing. Always ask what the number means before you compute with it. To justify a "twice as many" claim, point to the true zero: 80 shares is twice 40 shares because zero shares means no shares (the mock exam tested this).

One summary per scale, and the superstar problem

On the exam

Because each scale allows different operations, each scale has its own summary. For nominal data you use the mode, the most frequent value. In the six streamers, the categories tie: Lifestyle and Gaming appear 2 of 6 times each, so there are two modes. For ordinal data you use the median, the middle of the sorted ranks, and percentiles. The course's percentile example: a streamer who received 85 gifts ranks at the 95th percentile of 100 streamers, which means 95% received fewer. For interval and ratio data you may also use the mean and the standard deviation, because gaps are meaningful, so adding and averaging make sense.

Framework to remember

One summary per scale (3 levels)

  • Nominal: mode: the most frequent value (Lifestyle and Gaming tie, 2 of 6 each).
  • Ordinal: median and percentiles: the middle of the sorted ranks, robust to extremes; 85 gifts ranks 95th of 100, so 95% received fewer.
  • Interval and ratio: mean and standard deviation: allowed because gaps are meaningful.

How to recall it: "one superstar doubles the mean", so for skewed creator data report the median.

Remember the lecture's warning: "one superstar doubles the mean". Creator data is heavily skewed: a few accounts are huge and most are small. The mean adds everything and divides by the count, so a single huge value pulls it up. The median only looks at the middle position, so it is "robust to extremes".

Worked example (my own illustration): one superstar joins five small creators

Five small creators have 10K, 12K, 15K, 18K and 20K followers. The mean is (10 + 12 + 15 + 18 + 20) / 5 = 75 / 5 = 15K, and the median (the third value) is also 15K. Now one superstar with 110K followers joins. The total becomes 185K, and the mean is 185 / 6 = 30.8K, about double. The median of six values is the average of the third and fourth sorted values, (15 + 18) / 2 = 16.5K, which barely moved.

Business reading: if an agency tells a brand "our average creator has 30.8K followers", the brand will expect a typical creator far bigger than it will get. Five of the six creators have 20K or fewer. For skewed creator data, report the median for the typical case, and use the mean only when you really need the total divided by the count.

Entropy: how much information is in an answer

On the exam

Entropy measures how much you learn from an answer. The course's principle is "information = uncertainty: the harder to guess, the more an answer tells". If you can almost always guess the answer before you hear it, hearing it tells you little. If every answer is equally likely, hearing it tells you a lot.

H(U) = −Σ pi log2 pi

pi is the probability (here, the share of rows) of each value the attribute can take. You multiply each share by its log base 2, add the results, and put a minus sign in front so the answer is positive (logs of numbers below 1 are negative). H is measured in bits. A value with probability 1 adds nothing, because log2 1 = 0. H is largest when all values are equally likely.

A useful shortcut: −p log2 p is the same as p × log2(1/p). The values you need are log2 6 = 2.585, log2 3 = 1.585, and log2(6/5) = 0.263.

Worked example: three questions about the six streamers

The table has six rooms. Gender is Female for rooms 1, 2, 3, 4 and 6, and Male for room 5. Category is Film & TV, Gaming, Lifestyle, Lifestyle, Gaming, Music.

Which room? Six different rooms, each with probability 1/6. H = 6 × (1/6 × log2 6) = log2 6 = 2.585, which the lecture rounds to 2.58.

Gender? The split is 5/6 Female and 1/6 Male. The Female term is 5/6 × log2(6/5) = 0.833 × 0.263 = 0.219. The Male term is 1/6 × log2 6 = 0.167 × 2.585 = 0.431. H = 0.219 + 0.431 = 0.650, given as 0.65.

Which category? Gaming 2/6, Lifestyle 2/6, Film & TV 1/6, Music 1/6. Each 2/6 term is 1/3 × log2 3 = 0.528, and there are two, giving 1.057. Each 1/6 term is 0.431, and there are two, giving 0.862. H = 1.057 + 0.862 = 1.918, given as 1.92.

Reading: the room number has the largest H, so it is the hardest to guess and carries the most information. Gender has the smallest H because you would guess "Female" and be right five times out of six. Category sits in between. Note that entropy depends only on the shares, never on the labels: renaming "Gaming" to anything else does not change H.

Trap

"Most information" here does not mean "most useful for a business decision". The room number has the highest entropy because every row is unique, but an ID tells you nothing about why a stream sells. Entropy measures how unpredictable an attribute is, not whether it explains an outcome. In Week 4, decision trees use the reduction of entropy to choose splits, which is a different question.

The chi-square test: are two attributes linked?

On the exam

The chi-square (χ2) test asks whether two categorical (nominal) attributes are related. The course example asks: is a streamer's category related to gender, among 420 streamers? The logic has three steps. First, assume there is no link. If category and gender were unrelated, every category would have the same female share as the whole sample, and that assumption tells you the count you would expect in each cell. Second, measure how far the observed counts are from the expected counts. Third, compare that distance with a threshold, the critical value.

Framework to remember

The chi-square test (3 steps, in order)

  1. Expected counts: assume no link; e = row total × column total / n for every cell.
  2. Gaps: for every cell compute (f − e)2 / e, then add them all to get χ2.
  3. Compare: with df = (rows − 1) × (columns − 1), compare χ2 with the critical value. Above it: evidence of a link. Below it: no evidence of a link.

How to recall it: expect, measure the gap, compare. Below the critical value is "no evidence", never "proven unrelated".

e = row total × column total / n

χ2 = Σ (f − e)2 / e

f is the observed count in a cell, e is the expected count if there were no link, and n is the total (420). Squaring makes all gaps positive. Dividing by e scales each gap to its cell: a gap of 5 matters more when you expected 30 than when you expected 300. Degrees of freedom (df) = (rows − 1) × (columns − 1).

Worked example: gender by category, 420 streamers

GenderFilm & TVGamingLifestyleMusicTotal
Female (observed)68755779279
Female (expected)66806073279
Male (observed)32453331141
Male (expected)34403037141
Total10012090110420

Step 1, expected counts. For Female and Film & TV: 279 × 100 / 420 = 66.4, rounded to 66. For Female and Gaming: 279 × 120 / 420 = 79.7, rounded to 80. The other cells follow the same rule, and the lecture rounds every expected count to a whole number.

Step 2, the gaps. For each cell compute (f − e)2 / e:

Cellfef − e(f − e)2(f − e)2/e
Female, Film & TV6866240.0606
Female, Gaming7580−5250.3125
Female, Lifestyle5760−390.1500
Female, Music79736360.4932
Male, Film & TV3234−240.1176
Male, Gaming45405250.6250
Male, Lifestyle3330390.3000
Male, Music3137−6360.9730

Step 3, add and compare. The sum is χ2 = 3.0319, about 3.03. The table has 2 rows and 4 columns, so df = (2 − 1) × (4 − 1) = 3. At significance level α = 0.1 with df = 3, the critical value is 6.25. Since 3.03 < 6.25, the conclusion is: no evidence of a link.

A small check for the careful reader: if you keep the unrounded expected counts (66.43, 79.71 and so on), χ2 comes out at about 2.76 instead of 3.03. The verdict is the same, because both are well below 6.25. On the exam, follow whatever rounding the paper uses.

Business reading: in this sample, gender does not help you predict which category a streamer works in. A platform should not, for example, assume Gaming is a male category when it recruits or targets streamers.

Trap

"No evidence of a link" is not the same as "proven unrelated". The test only says this table, at this sample size, does not show enough difference between observed and expected counts. A larger sample could reach a different verdict. Also, the decision rule points one way: a χ2 above the critical value is evidence of a link; below it is not.

Rank correlation: do two orderings move together?

On the exam

Chi-square is for two nominal attributes. For two ordinal attributes you want to know whether the two rankings move together. Because ordinal gaps are not meaningful, you "correlate the ranks, not the raw values". The tool is Spearman's rank correlation.

rs = 1 − 6 Σ di2 / ( n (n2 − 1) )

For each object i, di = xi − yi is the gap between its rank on the first attribute and its rank on the second. You square each gap, add them up, multiply by 6, and divide by n(n2 − 1), where n is the number of objects. Then subtract that fraction from 1.

Framework to remember

Reading Spearman's rs (3 anchor values, from +1 to −1)

  • +1: the two rankings move together.
  • 0: no relation.
  • −1: the rankings move in opposite directions.

How to recall it: the sign gives the direction, the distance from 0 gives the strength.

Read the result on a scale from −1 to +1. At +1 the two rankings move together perfectly: every di is 0, the fraction is 0, and rs = 1. At 0 there is no relation: knowing one ranking tells you nothing about the other. At −1 the rankings move in opposite directions: the top on one attribute is the bottom on the other. As my own illustration of −1, take three streamers ranked 1, 2, 3 on one attribute and 3, 2, 1 on the other. The gaps are −2, 0, 2, so Σd2 = 8, and rs = 1 − 6 × 8 / (3 × 8) = 1 − 2 = −1. In Week 1 the course shows only "the question shape", and the mechanics return in Weeks 4 and 5, but be ready to plug ranks into the formula if the paper prints it.

Beyond tables: the data shapes of new media

On the exam

Not all new media data arrives as a simple table. The course names five shapes and the week that handles each one. Record data is the table of this week: one object per row, fixed columns. Baskets are sets of items bought or viewed together (Week 3). Comments as words become a word count matrix (Week 7). Networks are nodes and the ties between them (Week 8). Time series are the same measure repeated over time (Weeks 5 and 9).

Framework to remember

The data shapes of new media (5 shapes, each with its week)

  • Record: tables, one object per row (Week 1).
  • Baskets: items bought or viewed together (Week 3).
  • Comments as words: a word count matrix (Week 7).
  • Networks: nodes and ties (Week 8).
  • Time series: the same measure over time (Weeks 5 and 9).

The course shows comments as word counts: each row is one streamer, each column is one word, and each cell counts how often the word appeared in that streamer's comments.

Streamerfungiftsponsorsurvivalplatformriptakeout
13050261
20702101
30100122

Streamer 1's comments contain "sponsor" 5 times and "rip" 6 times; streamer 2's contain "gift" 7 times. The vocabulary still holds: each row is one object and each column one attribute; the table is just wide and has many zeros. "Week 7 reads this matrix."

Data quality: platform data is not neutral

On the exam

Platform data is not neutral. It contains error, gaps and missing values; bots and water armies (paid accounts that post in bulk) inflate the numbers; and data ages, so you must check relevance and documentation. The course sorts six problems by the side where they arise: three on the collection side and three on the application side.

ProblemSideWhat it looks like
Measurement errorcollectionthe recorded value differs from the actual value
Missing valuescollectiona user refuses to give an age: drop, estimate, or ignore
Noisecollectionthe random part of the error
Timelinessapplicationdata ages after collection
Relevanceapplicationdata must hold what the question needs
Documentationapplicationa dataset should ship with a description

Core framework: learn this by heart

Six data-quality problems on two sides (2 sides × 3 problems)

Collection side (when data is recorded)Application side (when data is used)
Measurement error: recorded value differs from actual valueTimeliness: data ages after collection
Missing values: e.g. age refusedRelevance: data must hold what the question needs
Noise: the random part of the errorDocumentation: a dataset should ship with a description

How to recall it: my own mnemonic, not a course term: collection is "E-M-N" (error, missing, noise); application is "T-R-D" (time, relevance, description).

Collection problems happen when the data is recorded. Application problems happen when you use good data for a question it was not built for: a clean dataset from last year may describe a platform that has since changed its algorithm, and a clean dataset of views cannot answer a question about refunds.

For missing values, such as a user who refuses to give an age, the course names three options, and each has a cost. You can drop the row, which loses data and can bias the sample if the people who refuse are different from the rest. You can estimate the value (for example, fill in a typical age), which keeps the row but adds a guess. Or you can ignore the gap, leaving the cell empty and using only the attributes you have for that row, which keeps everything but means some calculations run on fewer values. When a case shows a surprising number, such as a sudden jump in likes, first ask whether bots or a water army could explain it.

Framework to remember

Three options for a missing value (3 options)

  • Drop: remove the row; you lose data.
  • Estimate: fill in a likely value; you add a guess.
  • Ignore: leave the gap and work with what is there; some results use fewer values.

Preprocessing: aggregate, discretize, binarize

On the exam

Preprocessing shapes raw data before modelling. The course names three operations.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Preprocessing: shaping data before modelling (3 operations)

  • Aggregate: single events → per-stream totals.
  • Discretize: a continuous score → a grade (High / Mid / Low).
  • Binarize: categories → 0/1 columns (category → integer → bits).

How to recall it: my own mnemonic, not a course term: "A-D-B": add up, divide into grades, break into bits.

Aggregate: roll single events up into per-stream totals. The raw log has one row per click or order; aggregation turns it into one row per stream (total viewers, total orders, total GMV). This decides what one object means, so it is a modelling choice: totals per stream and totals per creator answer different questions.

Discretize: turn a continuous value into a grade, such as a popularity value into High, Mid or Low. There are three ways to set the cut points. Equal width splits the range into intervals of the same size. Equal frequency splits so that each grade holds the same number of objects. K-means places the boundaries where the data naturally separates, in the gaps between groups. Discretizing loses detail, but the result is easier to read and less sensitive to small changes.

Framework to remember

Three ways to discretize (3 methods)

  • Equal width: every interval covers the same range of values.
  • Equal frequency: every grade holds the same number of objects.
  • K-means: boundaries sit in the natural gaps between groups.

How to recall it: same size, same count, or natural gaps. The choice changes who lands in which grade.

Worked example (my own illustration): three grades for the heat index

The six heat values are 90, 80, 50, 45, 60 and −5. Equal width: the range is 90 − (−5) = 95, so each of three bins is 95 / 3 = 31.67 wide. Low is −5 up to 26.67, Mid is 26.67 up to 58.33, High is 58.33 to 90. Low gets only −5; Mid gets 45 and 50; High gets 60, 80 and 90. Equal frequency: sort the values (−5, 45, 50, 60, 80, 90) and put two in each grade: Low is −5 and 45, Mid is 50 and 60, High is 80 and 90. Notice that room 5 (heat 60) is High under equal width but Mid under equal frequency. A k-means cut would look for natural gaps, and the obvious one is the large gap between −5 and 45.

The lesson: one extreme value (−5) stretched the range, so equal width left a Low grade with a single streamer. Equal width is the easiest to explain but is distorted most by skewed data, which platform data usually is. The method you choose changes who lands in which grade.

Binarize: turn categories into 0/1 columns so that methods expecting numbers can use them. The route is "category → integer → bits". First give each category an integer (Film & TV 0, Gaming 1, Lifestyle 2, Music 3). Then write each integer in binary across the columns x1, x2, x3.

CategoryIntegerx1x2x3
Film & TV0000
Gaming1001
Lifestyle2010
Music3011

Read the bits as the binary number: Music is 011, which is 3. With four categories only two bits are really needed, so x1 is 0 in every row of this table. The other common way to binarize uses one 0/1 column per category; both give a method numbers to work with. Whichever you use, remember the integers are codes for a nominal attribute: Music (3) is not "three times" Gaming (1).

Similarity and distance between streamers

On the exam

Similarity and distance are two views of the same idea, scaled in opposite directions. A similarity of 1 means identical; higher means more alike. A distance of 0 means identical; higher means more different. The course places four streamers on two attributes, a popularity score (x) and a tier (y), and reports that d(S2, S3) = 1.41 is the closest pair. No formula is printed with this example, but the number 1.41 only comes out of the Euclidean (straight-line) distance, which the course notes also use.

d(p, q) = √( Σk (pk − qk)2 )

For each attribute k, take the difference between the two objects, square it, add over all attributes, and take the square root. With two attributes this is Pythagoras: the length of the straight line between two points on a plot.

Worked example: the closest pair of streamers

StreamerPopularity score (x)Tier (y)
S102
S220
S331
S451

For S2 and S3: the x gap is 3 − 2 = 1 and the y gap is 1 − 0 = 1. d = √(12 + 12) = √2 = 1.41. For S1 and S2: gaps of 2 and 2, so d = √8 = 2.83 (the notes give this pair too). Checking all six pairs: S1–S3 = √10 = 3.16, S1–S4 = √26 = 5.10, S2–S4 = √10 = 3.16, and S3–S4 = √4 = 2.00. The smallest is 1.41, so S2 and S3 are the most alike pair.

You can see why the formula matters. With city-block (Manhattan) distance, which adds the absolute gaps without squaring, S2–S3 would be 1 + 1 = 2 and S3–S4 would also be 2, a tie. The result 1.41 confirms the course uses the Euclidean distance. Clustering in Week 6 "runs on these" distances: it groups streamers whose distances are small.

Trap

Euclidean distance adds the columns as it finds them, so a column in big units dominates. If followers (in thousands) sits next to a 1 to 5 rating, the distance is really just the follower difference. Put the columns on comparable scales before you compute distances; Week 6 standardizes for exactly this reason.

The analytics ladder: describe, diagnose, predict, prescribe

On the exam

Each rung of the ladder has a definition and an example. Learn the question each rung asks, because exam questions often give you a manager's request and ask which rung it is.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The analytics ladder (4 rungs, in order)

  1. Describe: what happened? Summarize what the data says happened (12 streams, ¥2.1M GMV, 3.4% conversion).
  2. Diagnose: why did it happen? Break the description apart to find the driver (views fell only on paid streams: the creative changed).
  3. Predict: what will happen? Use patterns in past data to score future cases (classification: buy?; regression: how much?).
  4. Prescribe: what should we do? Turn predictions into actions and compare options (shift 20% of budget to retargeting the flagged 8%).

How to recall it: the first two rungs look backward, the last two look forward. "Why" signals diagnose; "should" signals prescribe.

Describe asks "what happened?" It summarizes what the data says happened, with dashboards, totals and trends. The course example: 12 streams, ¥2.1M GMV, 3.4% conversion. Nothing here explains anything; it just reports.

Diagnose asks "why did it happen?" It breaks the description apart to find the driver. The course example: views fell only on paid streams, so the creative changed. Breaking total views into paid and unpaid streams located the problem.

Predict asks "what will happen?" It uses patterns in past data to score future cases. The course examples are classification (will this viewer buy?) and regression (how much will they spend?), taught in Weeks 4 and 5. Predicting needs past cases where you already know the outcome.

Prescribe asks "what should we do?" It turns predictions into actions and compares options. The course example: shift 20% of budget to retargeting the flagged 8%. Here a prediction (which 8% of viewers are flagged as likely buyers) is turned into a budget decision.

The important break is between the second and third rungs: describe and diagnose look backward at data you hold, while predict and prescribe look forward and need a model. Each rung builds on the one below it.

Trap

The word "why" signals diagnose, and "should" or "which option" signals prescribe. A request like "which streamer should get next month's budget?" is prescribe, even though it sounds simple. A request like "which viewers will buy next month?" is predict, not prescribe, because it scores cases without choosing an action.

The metric taxonomy and the toolkit

On the exam

Marketing metrics fall into three families, from engagement to retention. Engagement metrics measure attention: views, likes, comments, shares. Conversion metrics measure whether attention turns into money: click-through rate (CTR), GMV, and refunds. Retention metrics measure whether customers come back: repeat purchase and churn (customers who leave). The families follow the customer from watching to buying to returning. A campaign can be strong in one family and weak in another: high engagement with low conversion means people watch but do not buy.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The metric taxonomy: engagement to retention (3 families, in order)

  1. Engagement: views, likes, comments, shares.
  2. Conversion: click-through rate (CTR), GMV, refunds.
  3. Retention: repeat purchase, churn.

How to recall it: watch, buy, come back. The key formula: conversion rate = orders / viewers.

conversion rate = orders / viewers

The numerator is the outcome you want (orders); the denominator is the chances to produce it (viewers). Most rate metrics have this shape.

The hard part is the denominator. Does a viewer who joined twice count once or twice? Two teams that report different conversion rates for the same stream usually disagree about viewers, not orders, so check that both streams define viewers the same way before you compare them.

The course toolkit is Python every week (pandas for tables, sklearn for models, mlxtend for association rules, nltk for text) and Stata in Week 5 only (regress and logit).

Discussion: one question for the six streamers

On the exam

The lecture asks you to write one question about the six streamers, decide which rung of the ladder it sits on, and choose the column you would start with. The debrief sorts typical answers:

RungYour question asksExample for the six streamers
describewhat happenedwhich streamer sold the most last month
diagnosewhy it happenedwhy one streamer's conversion dropped
predictwhat will happenwhich viewers will buy next month
prescribewhat to dowhich streamer gets next month's budget

The debrief makes two points. First, start from the column whose data you already have, usually orders or viewers; a question that needs data you do not hold cannot be answered. Second, you climb the ladder over time: "a describe question today becomes next term's predict question once the log grows". In an exam answer, name the rung, name the starting column, and say what data you would need to climb one rung higher.

The short version

The field studies how digital media and analytics reshape business (creating and capturing value), information systems (technologies and data infrastructures) and marketing (reaching, understanding and measuring people). Four technologies (social media, livestreaming, AI and AIGC, XR) each have a China and an international track, and together they produce five data types: behavioural events, text, networks, transactions and profiles. An app's primary platform shift is the one whose removal would kill it. Analysis follows question, data, prepare, model, decide, and climbs the ladder from describe to diagnose to predict to prescribe. In a table, a row is an object and a column is an attribute. The scale of an attribute (nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio) decides what you may compute: only ratio data has a true zero, so only ratio data supports "twice as much". Use the mode for nominal, the median and percentiles for ordinal, and the mean and standard deviation for interval and ratio, but prefer the median when one superstar can double the mean. Entropy is highest when values are hardest to guess (2.58 for room, 0.65 for gender, 1.92 for category). Chi-square compares observed with expected counts; 3.03 below 6.25 means no evidence of a link, not proof of no link. Spearman reads +1 as rankings moving together, 0 as no relation and −1 as opposite. Clean and shape data before modelling (aggregate, discretize, binarize), measure closeness with Euclidean distance (S2 and S3 at 1.41), and always check the denominator in conversion rate = orders / viewers.

Practice

P1.1 A fictional short-video app gives each post a "buzz score" that can be negative, and the app says a score of 0 is just a reference point, not "no buzz". Post A scored 30 last week and 60 this week. Which statement is correct? (a) The buzz doubled, because 60 = 2 × 30. (b) The buzz rose by 30 points, but you cannot say it doubled, because the scale has no true zero. (c) Nothing can be said, because the score is nominal. (d) The median of the two scores is the only valid summary.

Show the answer

(b). A score that can be negative and whose zero is only a reference point is an interval scale, like the heat index in the streamer table. Interval data allows adding and subtracting, so "rose by 30 points" is valid. It does not allow multiplying or dividing, so "doubled" is not valid. (a) would need a ratio scale with a true zero. (c) is wrong because the scores have order and meaningful gaps. (d) is wrong because interval data allows the mean as well as the median.

P1.2 The manager of a fictional livestream agency asks: "Our refund rate jumped last month. Is it coming from one product line or from all of them?" Which rung of the analytics ladder is this, and which column would you start with? (a) Describe; start with followers. (b) Diagnose; start with refunds broken down by product. (c) Predict; start with next month's viewers. (d) Prescribe; start with the advertising budget.

Show the answer

(b). The manager already knows what happened (refunds jumped), so this is not describe. The question asks where the change comes from, which means breaking the description apart to find the driver; that is diagnose, like the course example "views fell only on paid streams". The natural starting column is the data you already hold: refunds (and orders) per product line. It is not predict or prescribe, because it neither scores future cases nor chooses an action.

P1.3 A fictional electronics brand studies 200 viewers of its livestream. 120 watched on a phone and 80 on a PC. 50 bought and 150 did not. Observed counts: phone and bought 36, phone and did not buy 84, PC and bought 14, PC and did not buy 66. Compute the expected counts and χ2 = Σ(f − e)2/e. The critical value printed on the paper is 2.71 at α = 0.1 with df = 1. What do you conclude?

Show the answer

Expected counts use e = row total × column total / 200. Phone and bought: 120 × 50 / 200 = 30. Phone and not: 120 × 150 / 200 = 90. PC and bought: 80 × 50 / 200 = 20. PC and not: 80 × 150 / 200 = 60. Each gap is 6 in size (36 − 30 = 6, 84 − 90 = −6, 14 − 20 = −6, 66 − 60 = 6), so each squared gap is 36. χ2 = 36/30 + 36/90 + 36/20 + 36/60 = 1.2 + 0.4 + 1.8 + 0.6 = 4.0. The degrees of freedom are (2 − 1) × (2 − 1) = 1. Since 4.0 > 2.71, there is evidence of a link between device and buying at α = 0.1: phone viewers bought more often (36 of 120, 30%) than PC viewers (14 of 80, 17.5%). This is evidence of an association, not proof that the device causes buying; for example, the people who watch on phones may simply be different customers.

P1.4 A fictional brand ranks five creators by audience fit (1 = best) and, separately, by last month's sales (1 = highest). Creator A: fit 1, sales 2. B: fit 2, sales 1. C: fit 3, sales 3. D: fit 4, sales 5. E: fit 5, sales 4. Compute Spearman's rs = 1 − 6Σdi2 / (n(n2 − 1)) and interpret it.

Show the answer

The rank gaps d = fit rank − sales rank are A: −1, B: 1, C: 0, D: −1, E: 1. The squares are 1, 1, 0, 1, 1, so Σd2 = 4. With n = 5, n(n2 − 1) = 5 × 24 = 120. rs = 1 − 6 × 4 / 120 = 1 − 24/120 = 1 − 0.2 = 0.8. This is close to +1, so the two rankings move together strongly: creators with better audience fit tended to rank higher in sales. With only five creators, treat this as a pattern worth checking, not a law, and remember that a rank correlation says nothing about cause.

P1.5 A fictional MCN pitches ten creators to a snack brand and says "our creators average ¥50,000 GMV per month". You learn that nine creators each made ¥20,000 and one made ¥320,000. In three to five sentences, check the claim, say which summary the brand should use, and name one data-quality question to ask before signing.

Show the answer

The claim is arithmetically true: total GMV is 9 × 20,000 + 320,000 = 500,000, and 500,000 / 10 = ¥50,000. But the median is ¥20,000, because nine of the ten creators made exactly that. One superstar more than doubled the mean, so the mean describes no typical creator on the list. The brand should ask for the median (and the full list), since it is likely to sign one of the typical creators. Before signing, it should also ask whether the GMV is net of refunds and whether any orders or engagement could be inflated by bots or water armies, because platform data is not neutral.

Week 2 Fundamentals II: marketing frameworks and data preparation

Porter's five forces, platform edition

On the exam

The five forces model, from Porter (1980), asks a simple question: who else wants your customer's money, and who can squeeze your profit? It looks at an industry, not at one firm. In the middle sits industry rivalry, the fight among firms that already sell the same thing. Four other forces push into that middle box from outside. When the forces are strong, profit leaks away from every firm in the industry. When they are weak, firms keep more of what they earn. You use the model to judge how attractive a market is and to see where the pressure on your margin comes from.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Porter's five forces, platform edition (5 forces)

  • New entrants: "a channel opens in a day", so the entry barrier is almost zero.
  • Substitutes: "short video, stream and search ads split one budget".
  • Supplier power: "top hosts name their price".
  • Buyer power: "one swipe to the next shop".
  • Industry rivalry: "coupon wars in one category feed".

How to recall it: rivalry sits in the middle; entrants and substitutes threaten from outside the industry; suppliers and buyers squeeze from the two ends of the chain. On platforms, all five are stronger.

Each force has a platform example, and you should be able to explain each one in your own words.

ForceCourse exampleWhat it means in plain words
New entrantsa channel opens in a dayAnyone can open a shop or a stream account almost at once, so the barrier to entry is very low. New rivals keep arriving and take a share of the profit.
Substitutesshort video, stream and search ads split one budgetA substitute is a different product that does the same job. A brand has one marketing budget, and short video, livestreams and search ads all compete for that same money.
Supplier powertop hosts name their priceThe key input in livestream selling is the host. Top hosts are scarce, so they can set their own fee, and the brand's margin shrinks.
Buyer powerone swipe to the next shopSwitching costs the buyer almost nothing. A buyer who can compare rivals in one swipe has strong power over price.
Industry rivalrycoupon wars in one category feedRivals sit side by side in the same feed and fight with coupons, which pushes prices and margins down for everyone.

On platforms almost every force is stronger than in a classic shop. That raises the next question: how can a firm still earn above-average profit? Porter's generic strategies, in the next section, are the answer.

Exam tip

In a multiple-choice question, match the story to the actor. If the pressure comes from the people who sell you an input (hosts, factories), it is supplier power. If it comes from the people who buy from you and can leave easily, it is buyer power. If it is a different kind of product doing the same job, it is a substitute, not a rival.

Cost leadership, differentiation and focus

On the exam

Porter's answer to the five forces is to pick one way to win. There are two broad strategies, cost leadership and differentiation, and a third, focus, which applies either of them to a narrow target.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Porter's generic strategies: cost leadership, differentiation, focus (2 × 2 grid)

How you win ↓ / How wide your target →Whole marketOne niche
Lower costCost leadership: total cost below every rivalCost focus: serve the niche cheaper
UniquenessDifferentiation: seen as unique, different where buyers lookDifferentiation focus: serve the niche better

How to recall it: two questions, "how wide?" and "cheaper or better?". Focus is not a third way to win; it is cost or uniqueness applied to a narrow target.

Cost leadership

Cost leadership means your total cost is below every rival's, so your profit is above the industry average. The key line is "win the margin game, not the price tag". A cost leader does not have to be the cheapest seller. It can sell at the normal market price and keep a bigger margin, because each unit costs it less to make and deliver. A firm that only cuts its price without cutting its cost is not a cost leader; it is just giving away margin.

There are five routes to a lower cost: a simplified product (fewer features, fewer parts), improved design (a design that is cheaper to make), material saving (using less or cheaper material), lower labor cost, and production innovation and automation. The course example is the factory-direct livestream, which uses several routes at once: a simplified product, material saving and lower labor cost. The same idea comes back in the product part of the course as "a factory-direct stream with the tiers cut", meaning the layers of middlemen between the factory and the buyer are removed.

Framework to remember

Five routes to cost leadership (5 items)

  • Simplified product: fewer features and parts.
  • Improved design: a design that is cheaper to make.
  • Material saving: less material, or cheaper material.
  • Lower labor cost: fewer or cheaper working hours per unit.
  • Production innovation and automation: new processes and machines that cut unit cost.

How to recall it: the factory-direct livestream uses three routes at once (simplified product, material saving, lower labor cost). The goal is the margin, not the price tag.

Differentiation

Differentiation means buyers see your product as unique, so they will pay for it or choose it over cheaper rivals. The course phrase is "different where buyers look". Being different only counts if buyers notice it and care about it. A unique feature that buyers ignore just adds cost. The course examples are dermatologist hosts (a skin doctor presenting the product gives expert trust) and exclusive shades (colours no rival sells). Both lead to the same result: "where buyers look, meaningfully different".

Differentiation does not fit every market. It fits when there are many valued ways to differ, buyers' needs differ, few rivals are already differentiating, the firm has a quality or technology reputation, and the firm has strong marketing. Read this as a checklist. If buyers all want the same thing and every rival already claims to be special, differentiation will be expensive and weak.

Framework to remember

When differentiation fits (5 conditions)

  • Many valued ways to differ: buyers care about several features, so there is room to be special.
  • Needs differ: buyers do not all want the same thing.
  • Few rivals differentiate: the "special" slots are not yet taken.
  • Quality or tech reputation: buyers believe the firm can deliver the difference.
  • Strong marketing: the firm can make buyers notice the difference.

How to recall it: three are about the market (ways to differ, needs differ, few rivals differ) and two are about the firm (reputation, marketing).

Focus

Focus means you do not try to serve the whole market. The course defines it as concentrating on one buyer group, one product use or one region, and winning it completely ("own a niche completely"). Inside that narrow target you still have to choose how you win, and that gives two kinds of focus. Cost focus means you serve the niche cheaper than anyone else. Differentiation focus means you serve the niche better than anyone else. So the generic strategies are really a choice on two axes: how wide your target is (whole market or a niche), and how you win (lower cost or uniqueness). The in-class question was exactly this choice: "a niche you know well: would you serve it cheaper or better? Pick a side."

Framework to remember

Focus: three targets, two ways to win (3 targets × 2 ways)

  • One buyer group: e.g. people with sensitive skin.
  • One product use: e.g. sunscreen only.
  • One region: e.g. one city or province.

How to recall it: pick the target (who, what use, where), then pick the way to win it: cost focus (serve the niche cheaper) or differentiation focus (serve it better).

The course also lists four forms of focus: product line, customer, region and low share. The first three match the three targets above: you can focus on one line of products (one product use), on one type of customer (one buyer group), or on one place (one region). The fourth, low share, is given only as two words. The reasonable reading is a firm that accepts a small share of the total market on purpose, in exchange for being strong in its part of it.

Framework to remember

Four forms of focus (4 items)

  • Product line: focus on one line of products.
  • Customer: focus on one type of customer.
  • Region: focus on one place.
  • Low share: accept a small share of the whole market to be strong in one part of it.

How to recall it: the first three are the three focus targets (product use, buyer group, region) under other names; low share is the price you pay for focus.

The new media fit is that "a niche audience is one hashtag away". On a platform, a narrow group of buyers can find you through one tag, so a niche is cheaper to reach than it was in mass media. The course example is a streamer who reviews only sunscreen for sensitive skin. That streamer serves one product use for one buyer group and wins by going deeper than general beauty hosts, which makes it differentiation focus.

Trap

Do not confuse cost leadership with low price. The definition is about cost below every rival and profit above the average. A seller that wins on price while its cost is the same as everyone else's is losing the margin game, which is exactly what "win the margin game, not the price tag" warns against.

Segmentation: from mass market to a segment of one

On the exam

The next block is STP: segmentation, targeting and positioning. Its one-line meaning in the course glossary is "who you serve, which word you own". It runs in that order. You first cut the market, then choose which pieces to serve, then decide how you want those buyers to see you.

Core framework: learn this by heart

STP: segment, target, position (3 steps, in this order)

  1. Segment: cut the market into groups whose needs differ (using geographic, demographic, psychographic or behavioral variables).
  2. Target: choose which groups to serve (undifferentiated, differentiated or concentrated).
  3. Position: own one clear idea in the buyer's mind ("which word you own").

How to recall it: "who you serve, which word you own". Rule: STP first, then the 4Ps execute, never the other way round.

Segmentation is defined as cutting a market into sub-markets whose needs differ, where each sub-market is a group of buyers who want much the same thing. The two halves of the definition matter. Between segments, needs must differ, or there is no reason to treat them differently. Inside a segment, buyers must be similar, or one offer cannot serve them all.

There are four levels, from widest to narrowest. In the mass market there is one offer for everyone, like a classic soda sold to everyone. At the segment level the firm serves a few large groups, like skincare for teens versus skincare for mothers. A niche is narrow, clearly defined and served deep, like sunscreen only for sensitive skin. Custom means one buyer and one offer, like print-on-demand merchandise where one buyer prints one shirt. As you move right, the group gets smaller and the fit to each buyer gets closer; data and platforms make small groups cheap to find and serve.

Framework to remember

The segmentation spectrum: mass, segment, niche, custom (4 levels, from widest to narrowest)

  1. Mass: one offer for everyone (a classic soda).
  2. Segment: a few large groups (skincare for teens versus for mothers).
  3. Niche: narrow, defined, served deep (sunscreen only for sensitive skin).
  4. Custom: one buyer, one offer (print-on-demand: one buyer prints one shirt).

How to recall it: "from mass market to a segment of one". Each step right makes the group smaller and the fit closer.

Disruption enters from below

On the exam

Christensen (1997) explains disruption in three steps. First, overshoot: the incumbents (the established firms) keep improving their products until they offer more than most buyers need, and they charge for it. Second, low end: new entrants serve the low end of the market cheaply, with a simpler product that is good enough for those buyers. The incumbents tend to leave this end alone, because it looks unattractive to them. Third, climb: the entrants improve step by step and move up into the incumbents' own segments.

Framework to remember

Christensen's disruption (3 steps, in order)

  1. Overshoot: incumbents overshoot what most buyers need.
  2. Low end: new entrants serve the low end cheaply.
  3. Climb: then they climb into the incumbents' segments.

How to recall it: "disruption enters from below". Case: factory-direct livestreams undercut mid-market beauty, then climb.

The course case is factory-direct livestreams, which undercut mid-market beauty brands and then climb. This connects to two ideas you have already seen. The factory-direct stream is a cost leader (see cost leadership above), and the low end is a segment the mid-market firms overlooked. The lesson for segmentation is that a segment the leaders ignore is not always a small one forever. It can be the door through which a future rival enters.

Four variable families for cutting a market

On the exam

To cut a market, you need variables that separate buyers whose needs differ. They fall into four families.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Four segmentation variable families (4 items)

  • Geographic: where the buyer is (city tier, climate, logistics reach).
  • Demographic: facts about the person (age, income, life stage).
  • Psychographic: how the buyer thinks and lives (lifestyle and values).
  • Behavioral: what the buyer does (usage occasion, loyalty, benefit sought).

How to recall it: where they are, who they are, how they think, what they do. Platforms record the last one directly, so "segment by behavior".

Variable familyWhat it captures
Geographiccity tier, climate, logistics reach
Demographicage, income, life stage
Psychographiclifestyle and values (ingredient purists versus trend chasers)
Behavioralusage occasion, loyalty, benefit sought

Geographic variables are about where the buyer is. Demographic variables are facts about the person you could write on a form. Psychographic variables are about how the buyer thinks and lives: two buyers of the same age and income can be an "ingredient purist" who reads every label and a "trend chaser" who buys whatever is new. Behavioral variables are about what the buyer does. Platforms record behavior directly, in every click, watch and order, which is why the course's STP rule begins "segment by behavior" (see the herbal tea example below). My own illustration: "buyers who only purchase during late-night streams" is behavioral (usage occasion), while "buyers in third-tier cities" is geographic.

Three targeting strategies

On the exam

Once the market is cut, targeting decides which segments to serve and how. There are three ways.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Three targeting strategies (3 items)

  • Undifferentiated: one product and one mix for the whole market (commodity traffic goods).
  • Differentiated: a different mix, sometimes a different product, per segment (separate streams for students and for professionals).
  • Concentrated: all strength behind one or a few segments, for a high share of a small market.

How to recall it: one mix for all, one mix per segment, or everything on one segment. Concentrated is the targeting version of focus.

Undifferentiated targeting uses one product and one marketing mix for the whole market. It ignores the differences between segments. The course example is commodity traffic goods: cheap everyday items that buyers treat as the same whatever the label. It is the cheapest way to serve a market, but it only works when buyers really do want the same thing.

Differentiated targeting serves several segments, each with a different mix and sometimes a different product. The course example is separate streams for students and for professionals. It fits buyers better but costs more, because each segment needs its own effort.

Concentrated targeting puts all the firm's strength behind one or a few segments, aiming for a high share of a small market. This is the natural choice for a small firm, and it is the targeting version of the focus strategy.

Coverage: five product-market patterns

On the exam

Picture a grid. The rows are products, the columns are segments, and a filled cell means "we sell this product to this segment". The five patterns describe which cells a firm fills.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Five coverage patterns on the product-segment grid (5 items)

  • Single-segment concentration: one product for one segment (one cell).
  • Selective specialization: a few unrelated attractive cells (scattered cells).
  • Market specialization: many products for one segment (one full column).
  • Product specialization: one product for many segments (one full row).
  • Full coverage: every product for every segment (every cell).

How to recall it: rows are products, columns are segments. The word before "specialization" is the thing you hold fixed: one market, or one product.

PatternDefinitionWhat the grid looks like
Single-segment concentrationone product for one segmentone filled cell
Selective specializationa few unrelated attractive cellsscattered cells, no common row or column
Market specializationmany products for one segmentone full column
Product specializationone product for many segmentsone full row
Full coverageevery product for every segmentevery cell filled

To remember market versus product specialization, read the word that comes before "specialization": in market specialization you specialize in one market (segment) and sell it many products; in product specialization you specialize in one product and sell it to many segments. Selective specialization picks cells because each one is attractive on its own, not because they share a product or a segment, so it spreads risk but gives no shared strength.

The course's note on Lumen Beauty says it sits in single-segment concentration today, and in a year it should read as product specialization. In words: Lumen should first win one segment with its skincare line, and then carry that same line to more segments, rather than adding new product types for its first segment.

Choosing a targeting strategy: the six factors

On the exam

Which of the three targeting strategies should a firm pick? Six factors decide: firm strength, product homogeneity, market homogeneity, the product lifecycle (PLC) stage, rival (competitor) strategy and rival count. "Homogeneity" means sameness. High product homogeneity means buyers see all versions of the product as alike; high market homogeneity means buyers want much the same thing.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Six factors for choosing a targeting strategy (decision table: 6 factors × 3 strategies)

StrategyFirm strengthProduct homogeneityMarket homogeneityPLC stageRival strategyRival count
Undifferentiatedstronghighhighintro and growthanyfew
Differentiatedstronglowlowmaturitydifferentiatedmany
Concentratedlimitedlowlowdeclineanymany
Lumen: concentratedlimitedlowlowintrodifferentiatedmany

How to recall it: two about the firm and product (strength, product homogeneity), one about buyers (market homogeneity), one about time (PLC stage), two about rivals (strategy, count). Firm strength acts as a gate: "limited" leaves only concentrated.

The logic of each row makes sense once you say it aloud. Undifferentiated works when a strong firm sells something everyone sees as the same, to buyers who want the same thing, with few rivals around: one offer is enough and is the cheapest. Differentiated needs a strong firm, because serving many segments with separate mixes is expensive, and it suits a mature market where buyers vary and rivals are already differentiating. Concentrated is for a firm with limited strength facing varied buyers and many rivals: it cannot win everywhere, so it picks one place to win.

Worked example: why Lumen Beauty is concentrated

Read Lumen's row against the three strategy rows, one factor at a time. Firm strength is limited: Lumen is a startup with a junior team. Only the concentrated row has "limited"; both other strategies need a strong firm. This one factor already rules out undifferentiated and differentiated.

Product and market homogeneity are both low: skincare buyers differ in skin type and in what they want. Low homogeneity rules out undifferentiated again, and fits either differentiated or concentrated. Rival count is many, which also fits differentiated or concentrated.

Now the two factors that do not match the concentrated row. The PLC stage is intro, which in the table belongs to undifferentiated. Rival strategy is differentiated, which belongs to the differentiated row. So Lumen's row is not a perfect copy of any row. The decision still lands on concentrated, because the factors are weighed together, and firm strength works like a gate: a firm without the resources to run several mixes cannot choose differentiated targeting, however well the other factors fit.

The business reading: Lumen should put all its streams, budget and messages behind one segment it can win, and only spread to more segments when it is stronger. That matches the coverage note above (single-segment concentration today, product specialization in a year).

Exam tip

When a question describes a firm, list the six factors in a line and mark each one. Start with firm strength and homogeneity; they usually decide. Then say which factors point the other way and why they do not change the answer. Examiners reward the reasoning, not just the label.

Five ways to position the brand

On the exam

Positioning is giving the firm and its product a distinct standing in the buyer's mind inside the target market. Think of the buyer's mind as having a few "slots" for each category, such as "the gentle one" or "the cheap one". Positioning is the fight for a slot. There are five moves.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Five positioning strategies (5 items)

  • First place: be first to claim the slot (the first sunscreen-only channel).
  • Consolidate: defend and deepen the lead.
  • Excavate: find an unowned attribute and take it (clean beauty for sensitive skin).
  • Share: coexist with the leader's slot (the we-try-harder number two).
  • Reposition: change what the rival's slot means.

How to recall it: two moves for a leader (first place, consolidate), one for a gap (excavate), two for facing a leader (share beside it, or reposition it).

StrategyThe moveWhen you use it
First placebe first to claim the slot (the first sunscreen-only channel)The slot is empty and you can get there before anyone else.
Consolidatedefend and deepen the leadYou already own the slot and want to keep it.
Excavatefind an unowned attribute and take it (clean beauty for sensitive skin)The main slots are taken, but some attribute buyers care about has no owner.
Sharecoexist with the leader's slot (the we-try-harder number two)The leader is too strong to beat, so you stand openly beside it.
Repositionchange what the rival's slot meansYou make the rival's position look weaker or different, so buyers rethink it.

The difference between first place and excavate is a common question. First place is about timing: you are first into a slot. Excavate is about digging: the obvious slots are full, so you find a new attribute nobody has claimed. The difference between share and reposition is about the leader. In share you accept the leader's slot and live next to it; in reposition you attack what the leader's slot means. A good self-test from class: pick a brand you follow and name which of the five strategies it is running right now.

STP in the feed: the herbal tea example, and STP before the 4Ps

On the exam

The STP block ends with a three-step rule: segment by behavior, target one, position sharp. Its example is a Douyin herbal tea, and you should be able to walk through it in three sentences.

Framework to remember

STP in the feed: segment by behavior, target one, position sharp (3 steps, in order)

  1. Segment by behavior: late-night workers buying calming tea.
  2. Target one: serve them alone.
  3. Position sharp: "the three-in-the-morning tea".

How to recall it: this is STP in its platform form. Then comes the second rule: STP first, then the 4Ps execute.

Worked example: the three-in-the-morning tea

Segment: the brand cuts the market by behavior and finds late-night workers who buy calming tea. This is a behavioral variable (a usage occasion), the kind the platform records directly.

Target: it serves this group alone. That is concentrated targeting, and on the coverage grid it is single-segment concentration.

Position: it claims a sharp slot in the buyer's mind, "the three-in-the-morning tea". The name says exactly who it is for and when, which is what "which word you own" means in the definition of STP.

The second rule is the one you must not reverse: STP first, then the 4Ps execute. The 4Ps (product, price, place, promotion) are the levers a firm controls. They only make sense once you know who you serve and what you want them to think. For the herbal tea, the promotion (late-night streams) and the product (a calming tea) follow from the STP choice; they do not come before it. The practice set asks this as a true-or-false statement, and the answer is false whenever a question says the mix is set first and STP is used afterwards to fit it.

The rest of the marketing toolkit: value, satisfaction, brand, product and price

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

The middle of the week works through customer value and the marketing mix one lever at a time. These topics are not on the exam checklist themselves, but two of these ideas are defined in the examined list of key terms (PLC and DREK), and the mock exam asked about brand equity, pricing bases and customer delivered value. So you should know them well enough to answer a multiple-choice question or a short answer.

Customer delivered value

customer delivered value = total customer value − total customer cost

Total value has four parts: product, service, personnel and image. Total cost has four parts: money, time, energy and psychic cost (the worry of regret or of a fake). Price is only one of the eight levers. A livestream raises personnel and image value and cuts search time, so delivered value can rise even when the price is the same.

Framework to remember

Customer delivered value: four value parts minus four cost parts (2 × 4)

Total customer value (what the buyer gets)Total customer cost (what the buyer gives up)
Product: the item itselfMoney: the price paid
Service: delivery, help, after-salesTime: searching, waiting, checkout
Personnel: the people who serve you (the host)Energy: effort to find, compare and buy
Image: what the brand says about youPsychic: worry, risk of regret or of a fake

How to recall it: delivered value = value − cost. Money is only one of four costs, so a price cut is only one of eight levers.

The mock exam's B1 used this idea: a creator whose buyers must leave the video, face six checkout screens and wait a day for delivery wants to cut the price. The price cut touches only the money cost, while the real problem is time and energy. A shorter checkout fixes the actual cost, and you would check it with a measure such as the share of buyers who complete checkout, or the time from purchase to access, before and after the change. The question called the 4Cs "the customer-oriented marketing framework" (see the 4Cs below): the fix improves convenience and cost to the customer.

Satisfaction

Satisfaction is performance compared with expectations: above them the buyer is delighted, at them satisfied, below them lost. You can read it as a ratio, performance divided by expectations: above 1 means highly satisfied, about 1 means satisfied, below 1 means dissatisfied. The lesson is "keep the satisfaction ratio above 1 at all times". Expectations come from three sources: the buyer's own experience, other people's experience, and your promises. The promise is the only source of expectations you control, so a host who overpromises converts today and gets refunds next week. Satisfaction is a profit variable: quality leaders earn more, and satisfied buyers become loyal buyers.

Brand equity and DREK

Brand equity is the added value a product or service carries because of its brand: an intangible asset with a psychological side (what buyers think) and a financial side (what it earns). The course reads it with DREK (Kotler & Keller, 2016): differentiation, relevance, esteem and knowledge. Differentiation plus relevance gives brand strength; esteem plus knowledge gives brand stature. Strength looks forward (does the brand stand out, and does it matter to me?); stature looks back (is it respected, and do people know it?). The mock exam's A3 described customers seeing a service as more distinctive and relevant: that is strength.

Core framework: learn this by heart

DREK: strength = differentiation + relevance, stature = esteem + knowledge (4 pillars, 2 × 2 brand quadrants)

Low stature (esteem + knowledge)High stature (esteem + knowledge)
High strength (differentiation + relevance)Niche potential: stands out, not yet widely known or respected (IKEA, Dolce&Gabbana)Leaders: high on both (Disney, Nike, Lego)
Low strength (differentiation + relevance)New or unfocused: low on both (HSBC, Air France)Declining: still known and respected, but no longer different or relevant (Kmart, Xerox, Gillette)

How to recall it: D and R are the first pair (strength), E and K the second pair (stature). "Distinctive and relevant" means strength; "respected and well known" means stature.

Read the grid as a path. A new brand starts low on both. If it builds strength first, it has niche potential; as esteem and knowledge follow, it becomes a leader. A leader that stops being different and relevant keeps its stature for a while, which is the declining quadrant: people still know the name, but it no longer pulls them.
