Two more brand ideas from the course: a strong brand can carry trust to new offers (a serum brand's cleanser "presells"), and a top key opinion leader (KOL) is a person-shaped brand.

The product lifecycle (PLC)

The product lifecycle (PLC) has four stages, and "each stage [has] its own playbook": the stage sets the customer, the rival count, the cost and the marketing job. In introduction, sales are low, profit is negative and the goal is awareness and trial. In growth, sales rise fast, rivals enter and the goal is maximum share. In maturity, sales peak, profit starts to erode and the goal is to defend share and maximize profit. In decline, sales fall and the firm cuts spending and milks the product (harvest it), keeps a niche, or exits. The lifecycle is "a tendency, not a destiny". You need the stage names for the six-factor table above.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The product lifecycle: introduction, growth, maturity, decline (4 stages, in order)

IntroductionGrowthMaturityDecline
Saleslowrise fastpeakdecline
Cost per customerhighaveragelowlow
Profitlossrisinghighdeclining
Customersinnovatorsearly adoptersmiddle majoritylaggards
Rivalsfewgrowingstable, then shrinkingshrinking
Marketing goalawareness and trialmaximum sharedefend share, maximize profitcut spending and milk

How to recall it: the marketing goal row is the one to know cold: try it, grab share, defend and profit, milk it. In the six-factor table: intro and growth point to undifferentiated, maturity to differentiated, decline to concentrated.

Two new media twists. Platform lifecycles nest inside product lifecycles: a product can be mature on one platform and in introduction on the next. And the customer row is the adoption curve (innovators to laggards), which the social network week measures as who adopts through whom. The course also names six kinds of "new" product: all new, re-engineered, improved, repositioned, rebranded, and cost reduced (the factory-direct stream with the tiers cut).

Pricing: three bases

Pricing is "the only P that brings money in", and it has three bases. The cost base adds a markup to the unit cost; it gives the floor but says nothing about buyers. The demand (value) base asks what the target segment's income will bear; the course example is the same tea selling at three prices in three streams. The competition base sets the price against rivals' prices, either to meet them or to avoid the fight. The mock exam's A4, a seller who adjusts its price every month to stay near three rivals, is the competition base.

Framework to remember

Three pricing bases: cost, demand, competition (3 items)

  • Cost: cost plus a markup; the floor logic.
  • Demand (value): what the target segment will bear.
  • Competition: anchor on the rival, to meet or to avoid the fight.

How to recall it: look at yourself (cost), at the buyer (demand), at the rival (competition).

Within the cost base there are three cost-plus formulas. The difference is where the percentage sits: on the cost, or inside the price.

Worked example: pricing a notebook computer by cost and by value

A notebook computer maker has a standard model that costs 2,400 yuan. The factory price adds 5% profit on cost and covers a 20% tax on the factory price: 2,400 × (1 + 5%) / (1 − 20%) = 2,520 / 0.8 = 3,150. The retail price adds a 12% margin on the retail price: 3,150 / (1 − 12%) ≈ 3,580.

Price by cost for a new model: if the new model's target cost is 2,600, the factory price moves in proportion, 3,150 × (2,600 / 2,400) ≈ 3,410, and the retail price is 3,410 / (1 − 12%) ≈ 3,880.

Price by quality score (a value approach): both models are scored on six weighted criteria (CPU 30, memory 20, graphics 15, durability 15, battery 10, looks 10; each scored 0 to 4). The weighted score is each score times its weight, summed, then divided by 100: 3.48 for the standard model and 3.85 for the new one. The quality coefficient is 3.85 / 3.48 ≈ 111%, so the factory price is 3,150 × 111% ≈ 3,500 and the retail price is 3,500 / (1 − 12%) ≈ 3,980. The class question: would you pay the 111 percent, and where does the scoring break?

Price changes are risky in both directions. A cut can raise quality doubts, fail to win share and invite retaliation (and permanent coupons are a hidden cut). A raise can be direct (change the sticker) or indirect (a smaller pack, a weaker bundle, fewer freebies). Channels and promotion complete the mix: the new media channel is "collapsed", from five tiers to one tap (brand, platform, consumer), and promotion runs from advertising to short-term pushes such as flash prices and countdown coupons on a stream.

The 4Ps, the 4Cs and the AISAS loop

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

The marketing part of the week ends by pulling the mix together into frameworks, and three of them are on the examined key-terms list.

The 4Ps are the firm's controllable levers: product, price, place and promotion. The course uses the Zara video from the start of class: product (make what sells), place (restock twice a week) and promotion (almost no advertising). The key word is controllable: these are the things a firm can move and then measure ("levers exist to be moved and measured").

Core framework: learn this by heart

The 4Ps: the firm's controllable levers (4 items)

  • Product: what you sell (Zara: make what sells).
  • Price: what you charge; the only P that brings money in.
  • Place: where and how buyers get it (Zara: restock twice a week).
  • Promotion: how you tell buyers about it (Zara: almost no advertising).

How to recall it: the seller's view of the mix. The 4Ps execute only after STP has decided who you serve.

The 4Cs (Lauterborn, 1990) are the same mix seen through the customer's eyes. Each P has a matching C.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The 4Cs mapped to the 4Ps (4 pairs)

4P (seller's view)4C (customer's view)The shift
ProductCustomer solutionthey want the look, not the lipstick
PriceCost to the customermoney plus time plus effort plus risk
PlaceConveniencebuy without leaving the video
PromotionCommunicationtwo-way by default, in comments

How to recall it: keep the P order and swap each lever for what the buyer experiences: solution, cost, convenience, communication.

Notice that "cost to the customer" is the cost side of customer delivered value: it is more than the price. In class the 4Cs became an audit tool: take a stream you watch, score it on all four Cs, and find the weakest one. The Lumen Beauty case asked the same thing ("weakest 4C, and the first change?").

AISAS stands for attention, interest, search, action, share. The course draws it as a loop, not a funnel: the share step of one buyer (a post, a review, a repost) becomes the attention step of the next buyer ("share feeds the next person's attention").

Core framework: learn this by heart

AISAS: attention, interest, search, action, share (5 steps, in order, as a loop)

  1. Attention: the buyer notices the product (often from someone else's share).
  2. Interest: the buyer wants to know more.
  3. Search: the buyer looks it up, reads reviews, compares.
  4. Action: the buyer buys.
  5. Share: the buyer posts, reviews or reposts, which feeds the next person's attention.

How to recall it: "the journey is a loop, not a funnel". The last step restarts the first for someone else.

Three forces keep the loop turning, and each one is easy to see on a livestream.

Framework to remember

Three forces that drive the AISAS loop (3 items)

  • Social proof: "ten thousand bought this".
  • Scarcity: "only fifty left".
  • Parasocial trust: "I know this streamer" (a one-sided feeling of knowing the host).

How to recall it: others bought it, it is running out, and someone I trust says so.

The course adds the data-driven loop the rest of the course plugs into: question, data, method, decision, measure. Inside it sits the analytics ladder from Week 1 (describe, diagnose, predict, prescribe; see the ladder). The end-to-end example: which items to restock before Friday's stream? Describe sales, diagnose the drivers, predict demand, prescribe the list. Weeks 3 to 8 each supply one method for this loop.

Four shapes of new media data

On the exam

Recall from Week 1 (see the data shapes of new media): new media data arrives in four shapes, and each shape has its own place in the course. Tables are rows and columns, such as orders, logs and advertising reports; this is what Weeks 2 to 6 use. Nested JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) is a text format where data sits inside data: one record for a post can hold a list of comments, and each comment can hold its own author and time. You must flatten it into a table before most methods can use it. Text and video are handled in Week 7, and networks (who follows whom, who reposts whom) are handled in Week 8. When a question describes a data source, name its shape first; the shape tells you which method can read it.

Framework to remember

Four shapes of new media data (4 items)

  • Tables: orders, logs, ad reports (Weeks 2 to 6).
  • Nested JSON: data inside data, such as a post holding its comments; flatten before use.
  • Text and video: reviews, comments, clips (Week 7).
  • Networks: follows and reposts (Week 8).

How to recall it: name the shape first; the shape picks the method.

Variable types and three traps

On the exam

The key question is "what kind of number is it?" It matters because the kind of variable decides which summaries are valid. A number stored in a computer is not always a quantity.

Among numeric variables, continuous variables can take any value on a scale, like GMV in yuan or average watch time in minutes. Discrete variables are counts, like units sold, likes and comments. The test is "means make sense?" For both continuous and discrete variables an average is meaningful: Lumen's mean of about 2,502 comments per session is a fair summary, even though no single session can have a fraction of a comment. Among categorical variables, nominal variables are labels with no order, like a session ID or a product name. Ordinal variables are labels with an order, like ranks or a 1-to-5 rating.

Framework to remember

What kind of number is it? Two pairs of variable types (2 × 2)

FamilyTypeMeaning and example
Numeric (test: do means make sense?)Continuousany value on a scale: GMV, watch minutes
Discretecounts: units sold, likes, comments
Categorical (labels and ranks)Nominallabels with no order: session ID, product name
Ordinallabels with an order: ranks, a 1-to-5 rating

How to recall it: "continuous vs discrete: means make sense?"; "nominal vs ordinal: labels and ranks".

A class poll asked whether satisfaction scored 1 to 5 is numeric or categorical. The course's answer (practice set 2) is that it is ordinal: the levels are ordered, but the step from 4 to 5 is not a fixed amount of satisfaction. So "average satisfaction rose from 4.0 to 4.5" is a weaker claim than "average order value rose from 100 to 150 yuan", where every yuan is the same size. For a rating, the median or the share of 4s and 5s is safer.

There are three traps to watch for.

Framework to remember

Three variable traps (3 items)

  • Identifiers (IDs): stored as numbers, but they are names; an average ID means nothing.
  • Small-base ratios: a rate on a tiny denominator looks big and says little; report the base.
  • Bots: fake or paid accounts inflate views, likes and comments, and so distort rates.

How to recall it: a number that is really a name, a rate with too little behind it, and counts made by machines.

Identifiers (IDs): a session or product code may be stored as a number, but it is a name. If sessions S01 to S12 were stored as 1 to 12, their average (6.5) is easy to compute and means nothing. Any report that shows an average ID has a bug.

Small-base ratios: a rate computed on a small denominator can look impressive and carry almost no information. One viewer and one order is a 100% conversion. The course notebook shows a milder version with Lumen's own data. It computes conversion as units sold divided by peak viewers. Session S03 had 844 peak viewers and sold 139 units, a conversion of 139 / 844 = 16.5%. Session S01 had 1,419 peak viewers and sold 218 units, a conversion of 218 / 1,419 = 15.4%. S03 wins on the rate but sold fewer units. Always report the denominator beside the rate.

Bots: automated or paid accounts inflate views, likes and comments. These counts often sit in the denominator or numerator of engagement rates, so bots distort the rates, and they do not announce themselves.

From export to a dataset you trust: the cleaning order

On the exam

A platform export is not evidence yet. Turning it into a dataset you trust takes five steps, in a fixed order.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The cleaning order: tidy, duplicates, missing values, units and coding, outliers (5 steps, in order)

  1. Tidy: one row per observation, one column per variable.
  2. Duplicates: dedupe before judging missingness (order rule 1: copies double-count gaps).
  3. Missing values: drop the row or flag it, never silently fill.
  4. Units and coding: fix units before judging outliers (order rule 2: wrong units look like outliers).
  5. Outliers: keep it or flag it, never silently delete.

How to recall it: the lab's short form is "dupes, missing, units, outliers", after making the table tidy, and "log every fix". Two "never silently" rules: never silently fill, never silently delete.

StepWhat you do
Tidyone row per observation, one column per variable
Duplicatesdedupe before judging missingness
Missing valuesdrop the row or flag it, never silently fill
Units and codingfix units before judging outliers
Outlierskeep it or flag it, never silently delete

Tidy data means one row per observation and one column per variable. An observation is one thing you measured, such as one stream, or one product in one week. If a variable such as "week" is hidden in the column names (gmv_w1, gmv_w2 and so on), the table is not tidy, because you cannot group or filter by week.

Duplicates before missing values. A duplicated row copies everything in it, including its gaps. If you count missing values first, the same gap is counted twice and the problem looks bigger than it is. So remove duplicates, then count what is missing.

Missing values: drop or flag, never silently fill. You may drop the row from an analysis that needs the value, or keep it and flag it. What you must not do is quietly put in a plausible number, such as the average of the other rows. That creates data nobody can tell apart from real data. Practice set 2 shows why it is so dangerous: filling a gap with the mean leaves the mean unchanged, so the headline number gives no sign that anything was invented.

Units and coding before outliers. A value recorded in the wrong unit looks exactly like an extreme value. If you judge outliers first, you may delete a correct observation that was only mislabelled. Fix units first, then see what is still extreme.

Outliers: keep or flag, never silently delete. The course glossary defines an outlier as "an extreme but real value". Errors get fixed; real extremes stay in the data and get a flag in the report. Deleting a real event to make a chart look neat hides the most interesting thing that happened.

Behind every step sits one habit: log every fix, so that any number can be traced back to the export it came from.

Exam tip

The in-class question was: "A fresh export lands on your desk. Which check do you run first, and why that one?" A strong answer follows the order: first look at the shape and check it is tidy (how many rows, and does that match what you expect, such as 13 rows for 12 streams?), then look for duplicates, because every later count (missing values, outliers, means) is wrong while copies remain.

Trap

The two order rules are the ones examiners test: dedupe before judging missingness, and fix units before judging outliers. If a question asks why the order matters, give the reason for each: duplicates double-count gaps, and wrong units look like outliers.

The lab demo: open, clean, reshape

On the exam

The lab runs the checklist on Lumen's two exports using pandas, the Python library for tables. Lumen Beauty is a mid-size skincare brand new to running its own Douyin streams: 12 streams, 8 stock keeping units (SKUs; an SKU is one product listing), and a junior team. Its two exports are livestream_sessions.csv (12 streams × 11 fields) and livestream_weekly_sales.csv (8 SKUs × 4 weeks). No code writing is examined, but you should be able to say in words what each line does and what its output means.

First look: open the file

Line of codeWhat it does in words
import pandas as pdLoads the pandas library and gives it the short name pd.
df = pd.read_csv('livestream_sessions.csv')Reads the session export into a table (pandas calls it a data frame) named df.
w = pd.read_csv('livestream_weekly_sales.csv')Reads the weekly sales export into a second table named w.
df.shape  # (13, 11)Reports the size of df: 13 rows and 11 columns.
df.head()Shows the first five rows so you can see what the data looks like.

The shape is the first clue. Lumen ran 12 streams, but the table has 13 rows. Something is wrong before any analysis has started, and that is why you look before you count.

Clean before you count

Line of codeWhat it does in words
df[df.duplicated(keep=False)]Shows every row that has an exact copy. "keep=False" marks all copies, not only the second one, so you can see both side by side.
df = df.drop_duplicates()  # (12, 11)Removes the extra copy. The table now has 12 rows, one per stream.
df['avg_watch_min'].isna().sum()  # 1Counts the missing values in the average watch time column: there is one.

The lab's reminder line: "Order: dupes, missing, units, outliers · log every fix".

Worked example: cleaning Lumen's session export (with the course notebook's details)

Step 1, duplicates. The duplicate check shows session S07 twice, with every field identical. The notebook calls it a re-export artifact: the platform wrote the same row twice. Dropping it takes the table from 13 rows to 12, which now matches the 12 streams.

Step 2, missing values. After deduplication, one cell in avg_watch_min is missing; the notebook shows it belongs to session S05. The notebook neither fills it nor deletes S05 everywhere. It keeps two tables: one with all 12 sessions, used for totals such as GMV and units sold, which are complete; and one with the 11 sessions that have a watch time, used only for watch-time questions. That is "drop or flag, never silently fill" in practice.

Step 3, units. In the notebook, session S09 shows an average watch time of 252 for a stream of 150 minutes. An average viewer cannot watch longer than the stream lasted, so 252 cannot be minutes. It is seconds: 252 / 60 = 4.2 minutes, which sits inside the range of the other sessions. Left as 252, it would look like an outlier and might be deleted, which is why units come before outliers.

Step 4, outliers. Session S11 drew 9,500 comments, far above every other session (the next highest is 2,788). The notebook checks the cause: that night ran a giveaway, a real event. So S11 is kept and flagged. Its pull shows in the summaries: the mean is about 2,502 comments per session while the median is 2,074. The median is the better description of a typical stream.

Reshape: wide to long

The weekly sales export is wide: one row per SKU, with a separate column for each week's GMV (gmv_w1 to gmv_w4). This breaks the tidy rule, because the week is a variable but it lives in the column names. Analysis wants long: one row per SKU per week, with one column for the week and one for the GMV.

Part of the codeWhat it does in words
long = w.melt(...)"Melts" the wide table w into a long table called long.
id_vars=['sku', 'product_name']These identifier columns stay as they are and label every new row.
value_vars=[f'gmv_w{i}' for i in range(1,5)]Builds the list of the four columns to collapse: gmv_w1, gmv_w2, gmv_w3, gmv_w4.
var_name='week'The old column names become the entries of a new column called week.
value_name='gmv'The numbers from those columns go into a new column called gmv.
long.groupby('week')['gmv'].sum()Groups the rows by week and adds up the GMV in each group.

Worked example: from 8 rows to 32 rows to four weekly totals

The wide table has 8 rows, one per SKU. After the melt, each SKU appears once per week, so the long table has 8 × 4 = 32 rows and four columns: sku, product_name, week and gmv. The weekly sales file also has a price column. Because the code names the four GMV columns in value_vars, price is left out. Practice set 2 notes the common slip: if you forget to name the value columns, price is melted as if it were a fifth week, giving 8 × 5 = 40 rows.

Grouping by week and summing gives these totals, in yuan: week 1, 41,352; week 2, 49,128; week 3, 57,287; week 4, 71,617. Add them: 41,352 + 49,128 + 57,287 + 71,617 = 219,384, which is Lumen's store total for month one (of which 182,907 yuan, 83%, came from streams). The weekly increases are 7,776, then 8,159, then 14,330, so growth sped up in the last week. From week 1 to week 4, GMV grew by 71,617 / 41,352 ≈ 1.73, about 73%.

The business reading: Lumen's sales are rising every week, and fastest at the end of the month. That is a reason to look closer, not a final answer. GMV is gross merchandise value: total order value before refunds. A month of rising GMV can still hide returns, and it says nothing yet about whether the streams covered their cost.

The lab ends with a hands-on round: teams of two complete three blank "TRY IT" cells in the notebook, one per concept, and the answers stay private. Know the steps they practise: open and look, clean in order, reshape wide to long.

Trap

GMV is measured before refunds. When a question compares GMV across weeks or sessions, remember that a host who overpromises can raise GMV today and lose it to refunds next week. GMV is a sales volume figure, not profit and not net revenue.

Week 2 in ten terms

On the exam

These ten terms are the week's glossary. Learn each line well enough to explain it in a sentence and to use it in a case. Five of them (PLC, 4Ps, 4Cs, AISAS, DREK) are explained in the background sections above (the marketing toolkit and the 4Ps, 4Cs and AISAS).

TermOne-line meaningIn plain words
STPsegment, target, position: who you serve, which word you ownCut the market, pick your buyers, own a clear idea in their minds.
Five forcesentrants, substitutes, supplier power, buyer power, rivalryThe five pressures that decide how much profit an industry keeps.
PLCthe product lifecycle; each stage its own playbookIntroduction, growth, maturity, decline; the stage sets the marketing job.
4Psthe firm's controllable levers: product, price, place, promotionWhat the seller can change.
4Csthe mix through customer eyes: solution, cost, convenience, communicationThe same four levers, judged by what the buyer gets and gives up.
AISASattention, interest, search, action, share; the journey loopsOne buyer's share becomes the next buyer's attention.
DREKdifferentiation, relevance, esteem, knowledge: the equity readingStrength is differentiation plus relevance; stature is esteem plus knowledge.
Tidy dataone row per observation, one column per variableThe table shape every later method expects.
Outlieran extreme but real value; keep it or flag it, never silently deleteErrors are fixed; real extremes stay, with a flag.
GMVgross merchandise value: total order value, before refundsThe big sales number, before returns are taken out.

The week closes with four takeaways: STP, then the 4Ps; the looping journey; clean before you count; and the project starts today. The first three are the spine of this chapter.

The short version

Platforms make every one of Porter's five forces stronger, so a firm needs a clear way to win: lower total cost than every rival (cost leadership, which is a margin game, not a price game), being different where buyers look (differentiation), or owning a niche, either cheaper or better (cost focus or differentiation focus). STP comes before the 4Ps: cut the market with geographic, demographic, psychographic or behavioral variables, pick undifferentiated, differentiated or concentrated targeting using the six factors (a firm with limited strength like Lumen ends up concentrated), and claim a slot with one of the five positioning moves. On the data side, ask what kind of number each column is, watch for IDs, small-base ratios and bots, and clean in order: tidy, duplicates, missing values, units, outliers, never silently filling or deleting. Lumen's export went from 13 rows to 12 after removing a duplicate, had one missing watch time, and after a wide-to-long melt gave weekly GMV of 41,352, 49,128, 57,287 and 71,617.

Practice

P2.1 (Multiple choice) A fictional snack brand, Crunchway, sells mainly through livestreams. This season the four most-watched food hosts on the platform all double their fee per stream, and Crunchway has no other hosts with similar reach. Which of the five forces does this describe? (a) Threat of new entrants. (b) Supplier power. (c) Buyer power. (d) Threat of substitutes.

Show the answer

(b) Supplier power. The hosts supply a key input to Crunchway's selling, and because there are few of them with this reach they can set their price; the course example is "top hosts name their price". It is not buyer power, because the pressure comes from the people Crunchway buys from, not the people who buy from Crunchway. It is not a substitute, because no different product is replacing the snack.

P2.2 (Multiple choice) A fictional oat-drink brand, Meadowfold, enters a category where the leader already owns "creamy" and a second brand owns "cheapest". Meadowfold notices that no brand claims "low sugar for people with diabetes", and builds all its streams around that claim. Which positioning strategy is this? (a) First place. (b) Consolidate. (c) Excavate. (d) Share.

Show the answer

(c) Excavate. Meadowfold finds an attribute that buyers care about but nobody owns, and takes it, which is the definition of excavate. It is not first place, because the main slots were already taken and the move is about finding an unowned attribute, not about being first into an open slot. It is not share, because Meadowfold is not placing itself beside the leader's "creamy" slot.

P2.3 (Short answer) Brightpine is a fictional three-person studio that sells hand-painted phone cases. Its buyers have very different tastes, there are hundreds of rival sellers on the platform, the product is new, and most rivals already run different styles for different buyer groups. Using the six factors, choose a targeting strategy for Brightpine and explain your choice in three to five sentences. Then name the coverage pattern it starts in.

Show the answer

Brightpine should use concentrated targeting. Its firm strength is limited (three people), which rules out both undifferentiated and differentiated targeting, since the six-factor table gives both of those to strong firms. Product and market homogeneity are low (hand-painted designs, varied tastes), which also rules out undifferentiated, and the many rivals fit concentrated. The introduction stage and the differentiated rivals point elsewhere, just as in Lumen's row, but a small firm cannot afford separate mixes for many segments, so it should put everything behind one segment it can win. It starts in single-segment concentration: one product for one segment.

P2.4 (Case reading) Mooncrest, a fictional candle brand, ran 20 livestreams. Its session export has 21 rows. Before any cleaning, a missing-value count shows 3 gaps in avg_watch_min. Exhibit: session S14 appears twice with identical values, and both copies have no watch time; session S06 shows avg_watch_min = 390 for a 120-minute stream; session S18 drew 12,000 likes on a night with a celebrity guest, against fewer than 3,000 for every other session. (a) In what order should the analyst handle these issues, and how many missing values remain after the first step? (b) What should be done with S06 and with S18? (c) A colleague suggests filling the missing watch times with the average of the other sessions. What do you say?

Show the answer

(a) Follow the cleaning order: duplicates, then missing values, then units, then outliers. Removing the duplicate S14 takes the table from 21 to 20 rows, one per stream. The two S14 copies counted the same gap twice, so after deduplication 3 − 1 = 2 missing watch times remain. This is exactly why you dedupe before judging missingness. (b) S06 is a unit error, not an outlier: an average viewer cannot watch 390 minutes of a 120-minute stream, so the value is in seconds, and 390 / 60 = 6.5 minutes. Fix it and log the fix before looking for outliers. S18 is a real event (the celebrity guest), so it is an outlier in the course's sense ("an extreme but real value"): keep it and flag it in the report, and give the median beside the mean so the reader sees a typical session. (c) Do not fill silently. Filling with the mean invents viewer behavior and leaves the mean unchanged, so no reader could tell that values were made up. Either drop those two sessions from watch-time questions only (keeping them for totals such as GMV) or keep them and flag the gap.

P2.5 (Calculation) Fernleaf Tea, a fictional brand, exports a wide table with columns sku, product_name, price, gmv_w1, gmv_w2, gmv_w3. The three rows are: T1 with GMV 1,200, 1,500, 1,800; T2 with 800, 900, 1,100; T3 with 500, 400, 700 (in yuan, weeks 1 to 3). (a) An analyst melts the table, keeping sku and product_name as identifiers and naming the three GMV columns as the value columns. How many rows and which columns does the long table have? (b) Compute the weekly GMV totals that a grouped sum by week would print, and the growth from week 1 to week 3. (c) What goes wrong if the analyst forgets to name the value columns?

Show the answer

(a) Each of the 3 SKUs appears once per week, so the long table has 3 × 3 = 9 rows and four columns: sku, product_name, week and gmv. Price is left out because it is not named as a value column. (b) Week 1: 1,200 + 800 + 500 = 2,500. Week 2: 1,500 + 900 + 400 = 2,800. Week 3: 1,800 + 1,100 + 700 = 3,600. Growth from week 1 to week 3 is 3,600 / 2,500 = 1.44, a rise of 44%. Note that T3 fell in week 2 (500 to 400) before rising, which the total hides. (c) Without a named list, every non-identifier column is melted, so price is treated as if it were another week. The table would then have 3 × 4 = 12 rows, and a grouped sum would print a meaningless "price" total next to the real weeks.

Week 3 Association and rules

From co-occurrence to baskets

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

The week is built in three parts. Keep them in mind, because every later section belongs to one of them.

The lecture opens by setting co-occurrence against correlation. Correlation needs two variables on a scale, such as watch time and spend, and gives you r between −1 and 1. Co-occurrence needs no scale at all. It only asks which items land in the same basket, and you get the answer by counting transactions. The course uses the textbook definition of association analysis: given a set of transactions, find rules that predict the occurrence of an item from the occurrences of other items in the same transaction. The left side of a rule is called the antecedent and the right side the consequent, as in {bread flour} → {instant yeast}.

The questions association analysis answers are everyday ones: which products are bought together, which streams are watched together, what a buyer of a first personal computer buys next, and which pages get clicked in sequence.

A basket is the set of distinct items in one order or one session. "Distinct" matters. If a customer buys three print packs and two frames, the basket simply contains "print pack" and "frame". In a basket table you write 1 for present and 0 for absent, never the quantity, so that order is the row 1, 1, 0 if the third column is stickers. Support counts orders, not units. The mock exam tested exactly this point (A5: the answer is 1, 1, 0, not 3, 2, 0), and the practice solutions warn that adding up a quantity column is a common slip. Each row of a basket table is one basket, and market-basket analysis reads all rows at once.

Framework to remember

Reading a basket table (3 rules)

  • One row = one basket: one order, one session or one viewer's list.
  • 1 = present, 0 = absent: record presence, never quantity (three print packs is still a 1).
  • Count baskets, not units: support is the share of rows that contain the itemset.

The lecture names four families of rules. Rules by value are about specific items (game streamer 1 → game streamer 2). Rules by category are about whole categories (Gaming → Film). Rules by attribute value are about properties of a session (watched: yes → liked: yes). Rules by time link periods (year one: Gaming → year two: Lifestyle). A rule by time only works if your data keeps each person's items separate for each period. If you merge January and February into one list per viewer, you can no longer ask whether January travel viewing goes with February language viewing. That is the reasoning behind mock question A6: the right input is each viewer's categories recorded separately for January and February, because it keeps both the viewer link and the time distinction.

The lecture also lists four business uses, which explain why retailers and platforms care.

One line sets the tone for the whole week: association is not causation. A rule says two things appear together; it does not say one causes the other.

Finally, here is the small table that the examined material computes on. Five viewers of one live channel, and a dot means the viewer watched that category. Keep this table in front of you for the next three sections.

ViewerGamingComedyMusicFilmLifestyleFood
1●●
2●●●●
3●●●●
4●●●●
5●●●●
Count444312

A rule is a claim. To decide whether a claim is worth acting on, the course measures it with three numbers, each answering one question. The next sections define all three on numbers you can check by hand from this table.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Three numbers decide whether a rule is worth acting on: support, confidence, lift (3 measures, learned in this order)

  1. Support: how often does the whole pattern show up? (share of all baskets)
  2. Confidence: given the left side, how often the right? (share of the X baskets that also hold Y)
  3. Lift: more likely than the base rate? (confidence compared with chance)

How to recall it: common? reliable? better than chance? Lift is the number to decide on.

Itemsets and support

On the exam

An itemset is any set of items you want to count across baskets, for example {Comedy, Gaming, Music}. Three ideas define how common an itemset is, and they build on each other.

The first is the support count, written σ (the Greek letter sigma). It is the number of transactions that contain the whole itemset. "Contain" means the basket has every item of the itemset; it may have other items too. The second is the support, written s. It is the support count divided by the total number of transactions, N. So σ is a count of baskets and s is a share between 0 and 1. The third is the frequent itemset: an itemset whose support is at least minsup, the minimum support. The lecture says plainly that minsup is the threshold you set. The data does not tell you what it should be.

Framework to remember

Support count, support, frequent itemset: σ, s, minsup (3 ideas, each built on the one before)

  1. Support count σ: the number of transactions that contain the itemset (a count).
  2. Support s: σ over all N transactions, s = σ / N (a share between 0 and 1).
  3. Frequent itemset: s ≥ minsup, where minsup is the threshold you set.

How to recall it: count, then share, then compare with your threshold. Example: σ({Comedy, Gaming, Music}) = 2, so s = 2/5 = .40.

s(I) = σ(I) / N     and     I is frequent if s(I) ≥ minsup

σ(I) is how many baskets hold every item of I. N is how many baskets there are in total. Support is therefore "what share of all baskets show this whole pattern".

Worked example: the support of {Comedy, Gaming, Music}

Go down the table row by row and ask: does this viewer have all three dots? Viewer 1 has Gaming and Comedy but no Music, so no. Viewer 2 has Gaming and Music but no Comedy, so no. Viewer 3 has Comedy and Music but no Gaming, so no. Viewer 4 has all three, yes. Viewer 5 has all three, yes. So σ({Comedy, Gaming, Music}) = 2.

There are N = 5 viewers, so s = 2/5 = .40. In words: two of the five viewers watched all three categories. If you had set minsup at .40 or lower, this itemset would be frequent. At minsup .60 it would not.

Support has no direction. It describes a set, and a set has no left or right side. {Comedy, Gaming, Music} and {Music, Gaming, Comedy} are the same set with the same support. Direction only arrives with rules, in the next section.

A rule and its two strength numbers

On the exam

An association rule is written X → Y. X and Y are itemsets, and they must be disjoint, which means they share no item. The rule claims that Y tends to appear when X appears. You cannot write {Comedy, Music} → {Music}, because Music would sit on both sides and the rule would say nothing.

A rule gets two strength numbers. Both use the same top number: the count of baskets that contain everything in the rule, σ(X ∪ Y). The symbol ∪ means "union", so X ∪ Y is all the items of X and Y together. The two numbers differ only in what they divide by.

support: s(X → Y) = σ(X ∪ Y) / N

confidence: c(X → Y) = σ(X ∪ Y) / σ(X)

Support divides by all baskets, so it asks how common the whole pattern is. Confidence divides only by the baskets that contain X, so it asks: among the cases where the rule applies, how often is it right? Support tells you whether the rule is worth attention. Confidence tells you whether the arrow can be trusted.

Framework to remember

A rule's two strength numbers (2 numbers, same top, different bottom)

NumberFormulaDivide byHow to read it
Support sσ(X ∪ Y) / Nall baskets"x% of all viewers watch both"
Confidence cσ(X ∪ Y) / σ(X)baskets that contain X"after X, x% also take Y"

How to recall it: the reading follows the denominator. Support has no direction; confidence changes when you flip the arrow.

Worked example: {Comedy, Music} → {Film}

Step 1, find the baskets that contain X = {Comedy, Music}. Viewer 3 has both, viewer 4 has both, viewer 5 has both. Viewers 1 and 2 do not. So σ(X) = 3.

Step 2, among those three, find the ones that also contain Film. Viewer 3 has Film, viewer 4 has Film, viewer 5 does not. So σ(X ∪ Y) = σ({Comedy, Music, Film}) = 2.

Step 3, support = 2/5 = .40: two of five viewers watch all three. Confidence = 2/3 ≈ .67: two of the three Comedy-Music viewers also watch Film.

Worked example: reading the two numbers for {Gaming} → {Comedy}

Gaming viewers are 1, 2, 4 and 5, so σ({Gaming}) = 4. Of these, viewers 1, 4 and 5 also watch Comedy; viewer 2 does not. So σ({Gaming, Comedy}) = 3.

Support = 3/5 = .60, read as "60% of all viewers watch both Gaming and Comedy". Confidence = 3/4 = .75, read as "after Gaming, 75% also watch Comedy". Notice how the reading follows the denominator: support talks about all viewers, confidence talks about Gaming viewers only.

Because confidence divides by σ(X), it changes when you flip the arrow. For {Comedy} → {Gaming} the top number is still 3, but Comedy viewers are 1, 3, 4 and 5, so the bottom is also 4 and confidence happens to be .75 again. That is a coincidence of this small table, not a rule. In general the two directions give different confidences, while support stays the same in both directions. The practice set shows this on real data: bread flour → bread improver has confidence .470, but bread improver → bread flour has .689.

Exam tip

When a question gives you counts, write down three numbers before anything else: N, σ(X) and σ(X ∪ Y). Support and confidence each take one division from there. If a question asks you to "read" a number, say what the denominator is: "of all viewers" for support, "of the viewers who already watch X" for confidence.

Two thresholds and the four readings

On the exam

Why is one threshold not enough? You need two, and both are yours. minsup answers "how common must the pattern be?" minconf, the minimum confidence, answers "how reliable must the rule be?" The lecture's line: both come from the decision, not the data. A shop that wants to redesign its whole home page needs patterns that touch many customers, so it sets a high minsup. A shop that only wants a small "you may also like" note under one niche product can accept a lower minsup but wants the note to be right often, so it cares more about minconf. The numbers in the data stay the same; the thresholds change with what you plan to do.

Framework to remember

Two thresholds, both yours: minsup and minconf (2 thresholds)

  • minsup: how common must the pattern be? (keep rules with s ≥ minsup)
  • minconf: how reliable must the rule be? (keep rules with c ≥ minconf)

How to recall it: "Both come from the decision, not the data." Typical starting point: minsup .5, minconf .5.

Putting the two numbers together gives a four-way reading. Learn it as a grid: support high or low, confidence high or low.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Reading support and confidence together (2 x 2 grid)

SupportConfidenceReadingWhat it means in practice
highhighact on itCommon and reliable: the pattern touches many baskets and the arrow is usually right.
highlowweak ruleMany baskets show the pattern, but knowing X does not tell you much about Y.
lowhighseductive but thinThe arrow looks reliable, but it rests on very few baskets.
lowlowignoreRare and unreliable.

How to recall it: both high, act; both low, ignore; the two mixed cells are the traps. The low-support, high-confidence cell is the one the course warns about.

The cell to remember is the seductive trap. Take the rule {streamer 1} → {streamer 99}. Streamer 1 has only one fan, and that fan also watches streamer 99. So every streamer 1 viewer watches streamer 99, and confidence is 1.00. But the support is one viewer in 100,000. The lecture's verdict: "Perfect confidence, no business value." Confidence divides by σ(X), and when σ(X) is tiny, one or two baskets can push confidence all the way to 1. That is why you never read confidence without support next to it. (The three-rule comparison later in this chapter prints this same rule with s .02 and lift 1.02, which does not match "one viewer in 100,000". The lesson is identical either way: high confidence, thin support, no signal. In an exam, use whatever numbers the question gives.)

The base rate trap

On the exam

So far it looks as if support and confidence together are enough: demand a pattern that is common and an arrow that is reliable. The next example shows that even this can fool you. This is the most important idea of the week: the trap inside support and confidence.

Worked example: one platform, 5,000 viewers

A platform has N = 5,000 viewers. 3,000 watch Gaming, 3,750 watch Comedy, and 2,000 watch both. Score the rule {Gaming} → {Comedy}.

Support = 2,000 / 5,000 = .40. That is a big share of the platform. Confidence = 2,000 / 3,000 = .667. Two out of three Gaming viewers watch Comedy. With both numbers looking healthy, you might pin a Comedy recommendation under every Gaming stream.

Now the catch: 75% of all viewers watch Comedy anyway. 3,750 / 5,000 = .75. This is the base rate, P(Comedy) = .75. A random viewer watches Comedy with probability .75. A Gaming viewer watches Comedy with probability .667. So knowing that someone watches Gaming makes Comedy less likely, not more. The rule's confidence sits below the base rate.

Now score the opposite rule, {Gaming} → {not Comedy}. Gaming viewers who do not watch Comedy number 3,000 − 2,000 = 1,000. Support = 1,000 / 5,000 = .20. Confidence = 1,000 / 3,000 = .333. The course says this rule "fits the data better". Why, when its numbers are smaller? Because only 1,250 of 5,000 viewers (.25) skip Comedy in general, and among Gaming viewers the share is .333, which is higher than .25. As my own check: .333 / .25 ≈ 1.33, a value above 1 in the lift measure of the next section.

RuleSupportConfidenceReading
{Gaming} → {Comedy}.40.667below the base rate of .75
{Gaming} → {not Comedy}.20.333fits the data better

Trap

"A rule with high confidence can still be worse than guessing the base rate." Confidence has no idea how common the right-hand side is. Any rule that points at a very popular item will look confident, simply because that item is in most baskets. Before you believe a confident rule, ask: what share of all baskets contain Y anyway? If confidence is not above that share, X adds nothing.

Lift: the number that compares against chance

On the exam

The base rate trap shows what is missing: a comparison against chance. Lift supplies it. It divides the confidence of the rule by the base rate of the right-hand side.

lift(X → Y) = P(Y | X) / P(Y) = c(X → Y) / s(Y)

P(Y | X) is the probability of Y given X, which is exactly the confidence. P(Y) is the probability of Y in any basket, which is the support of Y alone, the base rate. Lift asks: how many times more likely is Y when X is present, compared with a random basket?

Worked example: lift of {Gaming} → {Comedy}

Confidence = .667 and s(Comedy) = .75, so lift = .667 / .75 = .89. The reading: below 1, Gaming viewers watch Comedy less than a random viewer does.

You can get the same answer straight from the counts, which is handy when an exam gives counts rather than shares: lift = (σ(X ∪ Y) × N) / (σ(X) × σ(Y)) = (2,000 × 5,000) / (3,000 × 3,750) = 10,000,000 / 11,250,000 ≈ .89.

Lift has three readings. A lift below 1 means the items substitute: having X makes Y less likely than usual, so people tend to choose one or the other. A lift equal to 1 means the items are independent: X tells you nothing about Y. A lift above 1 means they go together more often than chance would predict. A lift below 1 is still a finding. The practice solutions make this point with a tea shop: if two teas have lift .55, do not bundle them, but you can show them side by side as alternatives.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The three lift readings (3 zones around 1)

  • Lift < 1, they substitute: X makes Y less likely than a random basket (Gaming → Comedy, .89).
  • Lift = 1, independent: X tells you nothing about Y; this is the chance level.
  • Lift > 1, they go together: Y is more likely when X is present (matcha → bamboo whisk, 7.80).

How to recall it: 1 is chance. Below it, "one or the other"; above it, "both together".

One more property is useful. If you write lift out as P(X and Y) / (P(X) × P(Y)), you can see that swapping X and Y gives the same value. Lift is symmetric. It scores how strongly two sides go together, not which way the arrow points. Confidence is the number that differs between the two directions, so you rank by lift to find the association and then read confidence to decide which way to point a recommendation.

Exam tip

If the paper gives you only support and confidence, you can still find s(Y) when you know the base-rate count. If it gives you lift and confidence, you can recover the base rate: s(Y) = c / lift. Practise moving between the three numbers in every direction.

Trust this rule? Three verdicts

On the exam

The course ends the metrics part with a class vote: three rules side by side, each with all three numbers, and the question "Trust this rule?" Vote first, then read the verdicts. This is the most exam-like material in the week, because a Part C question can hand you a table like this and ask you to decide.

RulescliftVerdict
R1 {Gaming} → {Comedy}.40.67.89Kill
R2 {streamer 1} → {streamer 99}.021.001.02Ignore
R3 matcha_powder → bamboo_whisk.089.8107.80Keep, and act on it

R1, kill. R1 has the highest support of the three and a confidence that looks strong. But lift .89 says the two categories appear together slightly less often than independence would give. The strong confidence came from Comedy being a common item, not from a link between Gaming and Comedy. Acting on it would push Comedy at people who are, if anything, a little less interested than average.

R2, ignore. Confidence is perfect, but it is built on one rare viewer. Support .02 is thin, and lift 1.02 is almost exactly 1, which is the independence value. The pairing carries no signal to act on. This is the "seductive but thin" cell of the support-confidence grid, now confirmed by lift.

R3, keep and act. R3 has the lowest support of the three, .089, about one order in eleven. The course calls that thin but real. Confidence is high at .810, and lift 7.80 says matcha powder and the bamboo whisk travel together almost eight times more often than chance. That combination is what you want.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Trust this rule? The three verdicts: kill, ignore, keep and act (3 verdicts)

  • R1 {Gaming} → {Comedy}, kill (s .40, c .67, lift .89): confidence came from Comedy being common, not from a link.
  • R2 {streamer 1} → {streamer 99}, ignore (s .02, c 1.00, lift 1.02): perfect confidence on one rare viewer, no signal.
  • R3 matcha_powder → bamboo_whisk, keep and act (s .089, c .810, lift 7.80): support thin but real, confidence high, lift far above chance.

How to recall it: each rule wins on a different number: R1 on support, R2 on confidence, R3 on lift. Only the lift winner survives.

The lesson is that no single number decides. R1 wins on support and loses on lift. R2 wins on confidence and is empty. R3 is modest on support but wins where it matters. Read all three, and let lift tell you whether there is any association at all.

Real baskets: the bakery and the doilies decoy

On the exam

Now move to real-looking data: 2,800 orders from one Taobao bakery (the BakeBox case, which is fictional with synthetic data), mined at min_support 0.05. With 2,800 orders, 0.05 means an itemset must appear in at least 0.05 × 2,800 = 140 orders. Five rules come out.

RuleSupportConfidenceLift
bread_flour → instant_yeast.098.6584.43
cake_mold → parchment_roll.101.6743.98
matcha_powder → bamboo_whisk.089.8107.80
piping_bag → nozzle_set.095.6164.39
flour + yeast → bread_improver.069.7036.96

The course's comment: "Every rule here clears lift 1.5. The strongest pairs a niche powder with its tool." Why is matcha → bamboo whisk the strongest? Look at the columns. Its support (.089) is not the highest; cake_mold → parchment_roll has .101. But it has the highest confidence (.810) and by far the highest lift (7.80). Matcha powder is a niche product, and almost nobody needs a bamboo whisk unless they bought matcha. So when matcha is in the basket, the whisk is very likely, and when matcha is not, the whisk is rare. That gap between "with X" and "without X" is exactly what lift measures. Flour, yeast and parchment are bought by many bakers for many recipes, so their base rates are higher and their lifts lower.

You can check the table yourself. Lift = confidence / s(Y), so s(Y) = confidence / lift. For bread_flour → instant_yeast, .658 / 4.43 ≈ .149, and the course notebook lists instant_yeast with support .149. For cake_mold → parchment_roll, .674 / 3.98 ≈ .169, and the notebook lists parchment_roll at .169. For matcha → whisk, .810 / 7.80 ≈ .104: only about one order in ten contains a bamboo whisk, which is why the confidence of .810 is so far above chance. The last row also has a two-item antecedent, flour + yeast; this is the "serious bread baker" rule, and its lift of 6.96 is the second highest.

The decoy: high support is not association

Now add one item that does not fit: doilies, the paper mats placed under cakes. They are the most-bought item in the shop, with support .280, more than one order in four (784 of the 2,800 orders). If you sorted items by popularity, doilies would be first, and you might think they should anchor bundles. Compute the lift of doilies with each of the other 24 items. Every value lies between 0.850 and 1.073, inside a band of ±0.25 around 1.

Picture a chart with a lift axis from 0 to 8 and a shaded band around 1, labelled as the chance level. All 24 doilies points sit in a tight cluster inside that band. The five real rules from the table above sit far to the right, at about 4, 4.4 and 4.4, 7 and 7.8. In the course's words, doilies "ride along with everything, predict nothing".

Framework to remember

The decoy: high support is not association (4 signs)

  • Very high support: doilies, support .280, the most-bought item.
  • Lift near 1 with everything: against all 24 items, 0.850 to 1.073.
  • Inside the ±0.25 band around 1: every value sits at the chance level, far from the real rules (about 4 to 7.8).
  • Rides along with everything, predicts nothing: popular, not associated; do not use it as a bundle anchor.

How to recall it: co-occurring is not associating. Sort a rules table by lift, never by support.

Why does this happen? A popular item is in many baskets, so it will co-occur with almost every other item. That raises the confidence of any rule that points at doilies. But lift divides by the base rate of doilies, .280, which is also high. The two effects cancel, and lift lands near 1. Co-occurring is not the same as associating. This is the practical reason a rules table is sorted by lift, not by support or confidence. The case's recommendation follows from it: stop featuring doilies as an anchor product, because support .280 with lift inside 0.850 to 1.073 means nobody buys them because of anything else.

Trap

"Everybody buys it" is not a reason to bundle it. A best-seller has high support and therefore high confidence in any rule pointing at it, but its lift sits near 1. If an exam shows an item with high support and lift close to 1 across the board, call it a decoy: it is popular, not associated.

Finding rules is a search problem

On the exam

Restate the goal as a filter. Keep rules with s ≥ minsup, which are common enough, and with c ≥ minconf, which are reliable enough. Both thresholds are yours and come from the decision, not the data. A typical starting point is minsup .5 and minconf .5. The simplest way to find every rule that passes is brute force: score every possible rule, and keep the ones that pass. The question is: how many rules is that?

The count for a catalogue of d items can be derived exactly, in three steps.

Step 1: R = Σk C(d, k) · Σj C(d − k, j)

First pick the antecedent: choose k items out of d, which can be done in C(d, k) ways ("d choose k"). Then pick the consequent: choose j items out of the d − k that are left, in C(d − k, j) ways. The consequent must come from the leftover items because the two sides are disjoint. Add over every possible k and j, with neither side empty.

Step 2: R = Σk C(d, k) · (2d−k − 1)

The inner sum counts every non-empty subset of the d − k remaining items. A set of size d − k has 2d−k subsets in total, and one of them is empty, so there are 2d−k − 1 choices for the consequent. Here k runs from 1 to d − 1, since the antecedent cannot be empty and cannot use up every item.

Step 3: R = 3d − 2d+1 + 1

The binomial theorem collapses the sum. Split it into Σ C(d, k) 2d−k minus Σ C(d, k). The full sum of C(d, k) 2d−k over k = 0 to d is (1 + 2)d = 3d; drop the end terms k = 0 (which is 2d) and k = d (which is 1) to get 3d − 2d − 1. The full sum of C(d, k) is 2d; drop the same two end terms, each equal to 1, to get 2d − 2. Subtract: (3d − 2d − 1) − (2d − 2) = 3d − 2d+1 + 1.

Framework to remember

Counting the rules, exactly (3 steps, in order)

  1. Pick both sides: R = Σk C(d, k) · Σj C(d − k, j); the antecedent is k of d items, then the consequent is j of the rest.
  2. Any non-empty rest: R = Σk C(d, k) · (2d−k − 1); the consequent is any non-empty subset of the remaining items.
  3. Collapse: R = 3d − 2d+1 + 1; the binomial theorem collapses the sum.

How to recall it: check: d = 6 gives 729 − 128 + 1 = 602. The series for d = 3 to 8 is 12, 50, 180, 602, 1,932, 6,050: each extra item roughly triples the count.

The check: d = 6 gives 36 − 27 + 1 = 729 − 128 + 1 = 602 rules. Six live categories already produce 602 possible rules: "six items, 602 rules".

If the binomial step feels heavy, here is my own way to see the same formula, which is not part of the lecture. For each item, a rule puts it in one of three places: on the left, on the right, or not in the rule at all. That gives 3d ways. Some of these leave the left side empty: then each item has only two places (right or out), so 2d ways. The same number leave the right side empty. Subtract both, 3d − 2 × 2d. But the case where both sides are empty was subtracted twice, so add 1 back. The result is 3d − 2d+1 + 1. You can also check the smallest case by hand. With d = 3 items A, B, C, a one-item antecedent (3 choices) can take any non-empty subset of the other two (3 choices), giving 9 rules, and a two-item antecedent (3 choices) can only take the last item, giving 3 rules. Total 12, which matches the formula.

Items d3d2d+1Rules R = 3d − 2d+1 + 1
3271612
4813250
524364180
6729128602
72,1872561,932
86,5615126,050

Drawn as a bar chart, the bars for d = 3, 4 and 5 are barely visible next to the bar for d = 8. Each extra item roughly triples the number of rules. The bakery has 25 products, and real catalogues hold thousands, so brute force is hopeless.

The answer is to split the search into two steps. Step 1 is frequent itemset generation: find every itemset that clears minsup. The cost explodes with d, because there are 2d possible itemsets. Step 2 is rule generation: split each frequent itemset into strong rules that clear minconf. This is cheap once step 1 is done. The reason is that every rule built from one itemset has the same support, the support of that itemset. If the itemset is frequent, all its rules already pass minsup, and only confidence is left to check. Confidence uses counts you already have from step 1, so no new pass over the data is needed. The lecture's closing line: "Next: Apriori prunes step one."

Framework to remember

The two steps of the search (2 steps, in order)

  1. Frequent itemset generation: find every itemset clearing minsup. The cost explodes with d (2d itemsets).
  2. Rule generation: split each frequent itemset into strong rules that clear minconf. Cheap once step 1 is done.

How to recall it: first the sets (minsup), then the arrows (minconf). Step 1 is the expensive one, so that is the step Apriori prunes.

Exam tip

The formula is printed on the paper, so the marks are in using it and explaining it. Be ready to compute R for a given d, to say in one sentence what each step does (choose the left side, then any non-empty subset of the rest for the right side, then simplify), and to explain why the size of R is the reason an algorithm is needed.

The Apriori principle and candidate pruning

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

This section is not on the exam list, but the mock exam's Case 1 (C1.1, 8 marks) asks you to apply it, and it finishes the story of the search problem. Learn the method step by step so you can answer such a question if it appears.

Picture the itemset lattice: a diagram of every possible itemset, from the empty set { } at the top, through the single items (A, B, C), the pairs (AB, AC, BC), down to the full set (ABC). Every node is a candidate itemset. With d items there are 2d nodes, the empty set included. For six categories that is 26 = 64 nodes.

The cost of brute force is roughly O(N · M · w): M = 2d candidate itemsets to score, N transactions to compare each candidate against, and w items per transaction to match. For six categories in a small shop that is 64 × 2,800 orders × 3 items on a typical ticket ≈ 537,600 comparisons. Real catalogues hold thousands of items and N is in the millions. The exponential term is M, so that is the term to attack.

The Apriori principle (Agrawal and Srikant, 1994) lets you skip most of the lattice. It says: if an itemset is frequent, every one of its subsets is frequent too. Turned around: if an itemset is infrequent, every superset of it is infrequent, so you can prune them all and never count them.

The reason is simple: adding an item can never add baskets. A basket that holds Gaming, Comedy and Music also holds Gaming and Comedy, so the count of the bigger set can only be the same or smaller. In the five-viewer table, count({Gaming}) = 4, count({Gaming, Comedy}) = 3 and count({Gaming, Comedy, Music}) = 2, so 4 ≥ 3 ≥ 2. This property is called anti-monotone support: a set never has higher support than any of its subsets. Once a count falls below the minimum, that itemset and every superset of it are set aside together. The bound is not always strict. {Film} and {Film, Music} both count 3, because every Film viewer also watches Music.

Framework to remember

The Apriori principle (1 principle, read in 2 directions, 3 named ideas)

  • Forward: if an itemset is frequent, every one of its subsets is frequent too.
  • Turned around: if an itemset is infrequent, every superset is infrequent. Prune them all, never count them.
  • Anti-monotone support: a set never has higher support than any of its subsets (adding an item can never add baskets).
  • Support-based pruning: the pruning step that uses this property.
  • No recounting needed: pruned sets are never counted against the data.

How to recall it: one bad subset condemns the set. But all good subsets do not save it.

A small picture of pruning on the five viewers with a minimum count of 3: Lifestyle appears only once, so it is infrequent. Then Lifestyle and its three supersets in the small lattice of Gaming, Comedy and Lifestyle ({Gaming, Lifestyle}, {Comedy, Lifestyle}, {Gaming, Comedy, Lifestyle}) are 4 candidates never counted. Frequent so far: Gaming, Comedy and {Gaming, Comedy}.

Apriori uses the principle level by level, in a loop called generate and test. Before anything else, a threshold given as a share must become a count, because the algorithm compares counts: minimum count = minimum support × number of baskets.

Baskets (N)Minimum supportMinimum countWhere it comes from
560%3the five viewers
2,8005%140the bakery lab file
2,80028%784the orders containing doilies, the most-bought item

Then the loop runs. Count the single items and keep the frequent ones. Build candidate pairs only from frequent singles ("join the survivors, then prune"). Then, before counting any candidate of size k, check that every subset of size k − 1 is frequent; if even one is not, discard the candidate without counting. Count the survivors against the data, keep the frequent ones, and move up one level. Stop when a level produces nothing frequent.

For an exam question like the mock's C1.1, you only need one level of this loop, done by hand. The routine below is my own write-up of the course method, so the step names are mine, but each step is what the answer key gives marks for.

Framework to remember

Applying Apriori pruning by hand (5 steps, in order)

  1. Convert: minimum count = minimum support × number of baskets.
  2. Mark the pairs: label each pair frequent (count ≥ minimum) or infrequent.
  3. Check each triple: list its three pairs (for ABC: AB, AC, BC).
  4. Discard or keep: if any pair is infrequent, discard the triple without counting and name that pair; if all pairs are frequent, keep it as a candidate.
  5. Count the survivors: a survivor is only a candidate; count it against the data before calling it frequent.

How to recall it: my own phrasing, not a course term: "one bad pair kills, three good pairs only nominate". Passing the pruning does not confirm the set is frequent.

Worked example: Apriori on the five viewers at minimum support 60%

Minimum count = 0.60 × 5 = 3 baskets. Level 1 counts: Gaming 4, Comedy 4, Music 4, Film 3, Food 2, Lifestyle 1. Gaming, Comedy, Music and Film survive. Food and Lifestyle fail, so no set containing either is ever built.

Level 2 builds pairs from the four survivors only: C(4, 2) = 6 candidates instead of C(6, 2) = 15. The counts are {Gaming, Comedy} 3, {Gaming, Film} 2, {Gaming, Music} 3, {Comedy, Film} 2, {Comedy, Music} 3, {Film, Music} 3. Four pairs are frequent: {Gaming, Comedy}, {Gaming, Music}, {Comedy, Music} and {Film, Music}.

Level 3 builds triples from the frequent pairs and prunes. {Gaming, Music, Film} contains the pair {Gaming, Film}, which counted only 2, so it is discarded without counting. {Comedy, Music, Film} contains {Comedy, Film}, also 2, so it is discarded too. {Gaming, Comedy, Music} has all three of its pairs frequent, so it survives pruning. Now it must be counted: viewers 4 and 5 only, a count of 2, below 3. It is not frequent, and the search stops.

Total scored: 6 singles + 6 pairs + 1 triple = 13 candidates, against 64 for brute force. The longest frequent itemset has kmax = 2 items, so the search needs at most kmax + 1 = 3 passes over the data.

Trap

Pruning only tells you which candidates cannot be frequent. It never proves that a survivor is frequent. Frequent subsets are a necessary condition, not a guarantee. {Gaming, Comedy, Music} had three frequent pairs and still failed when it was counted. If a colleague says "all its pairs are frequent, so the triple is a confirmed bundle", the answer is: it is only a candidate, and you must count it against the data before you know.

Worked example: the mock exam's ClipFoundry case (C1.1)

100 baskets, four items A, B, C, D, minimum support 20%. Pair counts: AB 32, AC 27, AD 12, BC 22, BD 25, CD 18.

Step 1: minimum count = 0.20 × 100 = 20 baskets. Step 2: frequent pairs are AB, AC, BC and BD; AD (12) and CD (18) are below 20. Steps 3 and 4: discard ABD because AD has only 12; discard ACD because AD has 12 and CD has 18; discard BCD because CD has 18. A larger itemset cannot occur more often than one of its subsets, so none of these can reach 20. ABC survives, because AB, AC and BC each appear in at least 20 baskets. Step 5: ABC still needs to be counted. Frequent pairs are a necessary condition for a frequent triple, not a guarantee that all three items occur together in at least 20 baskets, so the colleague's "confirmed bundle" claim is wrong until ABC is counted.

Two further ideas belong to rule generation, step 2 of the search. First, a frequent itemset of k items can be split into at most 2k − 2 rules (both sides non-empty), so a 3-itemset gives 6 rules and a 4-itemset gives 14 (ABC → D, ABD → C, ACD → B, BCD → A, six rules with two items on each side, and four with one item on the left). All of them already clear minsup, because they share the itemset's support; only confidence is left to check. Second, confidence has its own pruning, with no rescanning of the data. For rules from the same itemset, moving items from the left side to the right side can only lower confidence, because the top number stays the same, σ(ABCD), and the bottom number can only grow. So c(ABC → D) ≥ c(AB → CD) ≥ c(A → BCD), and if the first fails minconf, every rule below it in that branch fails too. For example, if BCD → A fails, then CD → AB and D → ABC fail with it. The counts are already known from step 1.

The cost of Apriori is one pass over the data per level, kmax + 1 passes in all, where kmax is the size of the longest frequent itemset. On the bakery's 2,800 orders at min_support 0.05, the course notebook's Apriori run returns 40 frequent itemsets, and the most frequent single itemset is the decoy itself, doilies at support 0.280. FP-growth (Han, Pei and Yin, 2000) is a faster algorithm that compresses the transactions into a tree and skips generate-and-test entirely, but it finds the same frequent itemsets and the same rules; in the course's words, it is "an engineering optimization of the same search, not a new idea about rules".

The short version

A basket records which items are present, not how many. Support is the share of all baskets that contain the whole pattern, and an itemset is frequent when its support reaches the minsup you chose. A rule X → Y has disjoint sides; its support divides the count of X and Y together by all baskets, and its confidence divides the same count by the baskets that contain X. You need both thresholds, minsup and minconf, and both come from the decision: high support with high confidence is worth acting on, while perfect confidence on one rare viewer is seductive but thin. Even then, confidence can fool you when Y is common anyway, as in the 5,000-viewer case where .667 sits below the base rate of .75. Lift fixes this by dividing confidence by the support of Y: below 1 the items substitute, at 1 they are independent, above 1 they go together. Sort by lift: R1 is killed at lift .89, R2 ignored at 1.02, and matcha → bamboo whisk kept at 7.80, while doilies at support .280 are a decoy with every lift between 0.850 and 1.073. Brute force must score R = 3d − 2d+1 + 1 rules (602 for six items), so the search is split into frequent itemset generation, which is the expensive step, and rule generation, which is cheap. Apriori cuts the first step: an infrequent set condemns every superset, but a candidate that survives pruning must still be counted.

Practice

P3.1 (Multiple choice) A fictional podcast app has 8,000 listeners. 2,000 listen to True Crime, 4,800 listen to News, and 1,200 listen to both. For the rule {True Crime} → {News}, which statement is correct? (a) Confidence is .60, so True Crime listeners are strongly drawn to News. (b) Support is .25, because a quarter of listeners hear True Crime. (c) Lift is 1.00, so True Crime tells you nothing about News. (d) Lift is .60, so the two shows substitute for each other.

Show the answer

(c). Support = 1,200 / 8,000 = .15, so (b) is wrong; .25 is the support of True Crime alone, not of the rule. Confidence = 1,200 / 2,000 = .60. The base rate of News is 4,800 / 8,000 = .60, so lift = .60 / .60 = 1.00. True Crime listeners take News at exactly the rate every listener does, so the two are independent. Option (a) falls into the base rate trap: a confidence of .60 only looks strong until you see that 60% of everyone listens to News. Option (d) confuses confidence with lift.

P3.2 (Multiple choice) A fictional stationery shop finds the rule {gold fountain pen} → {leather pen case} with confidence 1.00 and support .0004. Which reading from the course fits best? (a) Act on it. (b) Weak rule. (c) Seductive but thin. (d) Ignore.

Show the answer

(c). Confidence is perfect, but support is tiny: only about 4 orders in 10,000 contain both items. This is the low-support, high-confidence box, the same shape as the streamer 1 → streamer 99 trap. When σ(X) is very small, a handful of baskets can push confidence to 1.00, so the rule looks reliable without enough evidence behind it. Whether to use it depends on the minsup the decision needs, but the reading is "seductive but thin".

P3.3 (Short answer) A fictional gift shop sells wrapping paper in 35% of all orders, more than any other item. Its lift with each of the other products lies between 0.92 and 1.06. The manager wants to show wrapping paper as "frequently bought together" on every product page. In three to five sentences, explain whether the data supports this.

Show the answer

The data does not support it. Wrapping paper has high support, so it appears in many baskets with almost everything, and any rule pointing at it will show a fairly high confidence. But every lift lies close to 1, which is the value for independence: buying any particular product does not make a customer more likely to take wrapping paper than a random customer. Like the doilies in the BakeBox case, it rides along with everything and predicts nothing. A "frequently bought together" prompt should be built on rules with lift clearly above 1, where the first item really raises the chance of the second.

P3.4 (Calculation) A fictional phone-accessory brand sells 7 products and wants to score every possible rule by brute force. How many rules is that? It then adds one more product. How many rules now? In one sentence, say which step of the search becomes the problem.

Show the answer

With d = 7, R = 37 − 28 + 1 = 2,187 − 256 + 1 = 1,932 rules. With d = 8, R = 38 − 29 + 1 = 6,561 − 512 + 1 = 6,050 rules, more than three times as many from one extra product. The expensive step is frequent itemset generation, step 1, because the number of possible itemsets is 2d and doubles with every product; rule generation, step 2, is cheap once the frequent itemsets and their counts are known.

P3.5 (Case) A fictional craft store, Loomly, has 200 baskets and four products: yarn (P), knitting needles (Q), pattern books (R) and stitch markers (S). Minimum support is 15%. All four single items are frequent. Pair counts are: PQ 45, PR 38, PS 22, QR 41, QS 33, RS 29. Yarn appears in 90 baskets and needles in 80. (a) Convert the minimum support to a count and apply the Apriori principle to the triples PQR, PQS, PRS and QRS. (b) A colleague says PQR is "a confirmed bundle". Respond. (c) Compute support, confidence and lift of {yarn} → {needles} and say what the lift means.

Show the answer

(a) Minimum count = 0.15 × 200 = 30 baskets. Frequent pairs are PQ (45), PR (38), QR (41) and QS (33). PS (22) and RS (29) are below 30. Discard PQS because PS is infrequent. Discard PRS because PS and RS are infrequent. Discard QRS because RS is infrequent. A larger itemset can never appear in more baskets than any of its subsets, so these three triples cannot reach 30 and need no counting. Only PQR remains, because PQ, PR and QR all clear 30.

(b) PQR is only a candidate. Frequent pairs are a necessary condition for a frequent triple, not a guarantee: the three pairs could each appear in 30 or more baskets without all three items appearing together that often. The store must count PQR against the 200 baskets. If, for example, it appears in 26 baskets, it fails the minimum of 30 and is not frequent.

(c) Support = 45 / 200 = .225. Confidence = 45 / 90 = .50. The base rate of needles is 80 / 200 = .40, so lift = .50 / .40 = 1.25. Yarn buyers take needles 1.25 times as often as a random customer: the two go together, a little more than chance would predict. The association is moderate, so a placement test is reasonable, but it is far weaker than a pair like matcha and bamboo whisk at 7.80.

Week 4 Classification

Labels, features and the hidden test set

Background: not examined directly, but needed to understand the exam material

A label is the answer on a past case. Training a model on examples whose answers are already known is called supervised learning. When the label is a category, such as spam or not spam, the task is classification; when it is a number, it is regression (Week 5). In the TeaPop data the label is is_spam: 1 if the platform removed the comment as spam, 0 if it stayed up. Each comment also has 15 features, the counted signals the model may see: the account (age, followers, verified or not), the text as numbers (length, links, emoji, digits, contact and price keywords) and behaviour (posting hour, comments in the last hour, repeated identical texts). The model never reads the words, only these counts.

The step that matters most for the exam material is the train and test split. The model learns from 3,000 comments with the answers visible and is judged on the other 1,000, whose answers stay hidden during training. Grading a model on the comments it learned from grades its memory, not its skill, just as a student who saw the paper in advance proves nothing by scoring well. The split is stratified: both parts carry the same 31.2 percent spam share, so the hidden 1,000 holds 312 spam and 688 genuine comments. Every verdict number in this chapter comes from that hidden 1,000. One last warning: prediction is not causation. The filter uses patterns that went with spam in the past; it cannot say why spam arrives or whether a stricter filter would deter it.

The score is a probability; the verdict is a cutoff

On the exam

This idea holds the whole week together. A classification model does not say "spam" or "not spam". It gives each comment a spam probability, a number between 0 and 1. In the course example, one comment scores 0.97 (very likely spam) and another 0.02 (very likely genuine). Sorted by score, the comments form a ranking from safest to spammiest.

A verdict (keep or delete) appears only when someone chooses a threshold, a cutoff on the score. The course example uses 0.5: score 0.5 or higher, delete; below, keep. But 0.5 is a choice, not a law; at 0.9 or 0.1 the same model gives different verdicts. That is why the lecture says to keep the probability: stored scores let you recut the verdicts later, when the business situation changes, without retraining anything. The lecture's line is "The model proposes; the threshold disposes." The model proposes a probability; the person who chooses the threshold decides what happens, and owns that business decision.

Framework to remember

The score and the verdict (2 steps, in this order)

  1. The score: the model gives each comment a spam probability from 0 to 1 (0.97 very likely spam, 0.02 very likely genuine); scores rank comments from safest to spammiest.
  2. The verdict: a person chooses a threshold (for example, 0.5 or higher: delete; below: keep); only then does keep or delete exist.

How to recall it: "The model proposes; the threshold disposes." Keep the probability; the verdict can be recut later.

verdict = delete if score ≥ t; keep if score < t

The score is the model's spam probability for one comment. The threshold t is chosen by a person. A high t means the filter deletes only comments it is very sure about. A low t means it deletes anything that looks even a little suspicious.

Worked example: one comment, two verdicts (background)

The lecture scores one real comment from the data with a small three-signal model. The comment posted identical texts twice, posted 8 times in the last hour, and had no links. The model adds the signals with their weights: z = −6.09 + 2.12 × 2 + 0.28 × 8 + 2.47 × 0 = −6.09 + 4.24 + 2.23 + 0 = 0.38. It then turns z into a probability: p = 1 / (1 + e−0.38) = 0.594. At threshold 0.5, 0.594 is above the cutoff, so the comment is deleted. At threshold 0.9, 0.594 is below the cutoff, so the comment is kept. Same comment, same model; only the dial moved. Remember this picture: the model did not change its mind. The person who set the threshold did.

Two ways to be wrong: the four cells

On the exam

Once a verdict exists, you can compare it with the truth. Two truths (actually genuine, actually spam) times two verdicts (keep, delete) give four cells. The course puts the truth in the rows and the filter's action in the columns. Spam is the "positive" class, because spam is what the filter looks for; "positive" means "flagged as spam", not "good".

Filter keepsFilter deletes
Actually genuineTrue negative (TN): genuine, kept. The quiet majority.False positive (FP): genuine, deleted. A customer voice lost.
Actually spamFalse negative (FN): spam left up. Still visible.True positive (TP): spam caught. The filter worked.

Read each name in two parts. The second word is what the filter said: positive means it called the comment spam and deleted it, negative means it kept it. The first word says whether that was right.
