So a false positive is a real customer the filter wrongly deleted, and a false negative is spam the filter wrongly kept, still visible.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Two ways to be wrong: the four cells of the confusion matrix (2 x 2 grid)

Truth (row) / filter action (column)Filter keepsFilter deletes
Actually genuineTN: genuine, kept: the quiet majorityFP: genuine, deleted: a customer voice lost
Actually spamFN: spam left up: still visibleTP: spam caught: the filter worked

How to recall it: the second letter is what the filter said (P = deleted as spam, N = kept); the first letter says whether it was right (T) or wrong (F). The two F cells are the two errors.

The two wrong cells are where the cost lives, and they land on different people. A false positive is paid by the commenter, whose genuine question vanished, and by the brand, which now looks like it silences customers. A false negative is paid by the audience, who still see scam links, and by the brand, whose comment section looks rotten. Hence the section title "Two errors, one dial": two kinds of mistake, and a threshold that trades one for the other.

Exam tip

If a question uses a different setting, first decide which class is the "positive" one, the thing being flagged. Then fill the grid row by row: truth on the side, verdict on the top. Most mistakes on these questions come from mixing up FP and FN, so say each cell out loud in plain words, for example "genuine, deleted".

The confusion matrix, counted

On the exam

A confusion matrix is the two-by-two table of counts in the four cells. The lecture counts it for the depth-6 decision tree on the hidden 1,000, stating the facts in words. You should be able to build the table from them.

Worked example: building the matrix from four sentences

The facts, on the hidden 1,000: 312 comments were truly spam; the filter deleted 307, of which 298 were spam and 9 were genuine; 14 spam comments slipped through; 679 genuine comments stayed untouched.

Step 1. Spam that was deleted is spam caught: TP = 298.

Step 2. Genuine comments that were deleted are false alarms: FP = 9. Check: 298 + 9 = 307, the number the filter deleted.

Step 3. Spam that slipped through was kept by mistake: FN = 14. Check: 298 + 14 = 312, all the true spam.

Step 4. Genuine comments left alone: TN = 679. Check: 679 + 9 = 688, all the genuine comments, and 688 + 312 = 1,000.

Depth-6 tree, hidden 1,000Filter keepsFilter deletesRow total
Actually genuineTN = 679FP = 9688
Actually spamFN = 14TP = 298312
Column total693 kept307 deleted1,000

Business reading: of 1,000 new comments, the filter wrongly silenced 9 customers and wrongly left 14 spam comments visible. Those two numbers are the whole story of what this filter costs. Everything else in the next sections is arithmetic on these four counts.

Notice the totals. The row totals (688 and 312) are fixed by the world. The column totals (693 kept, 307 deleted) are set by the filter and its threshold. Precision and recall divide by these different totals.

The accuracy trap

On the exam

Accuracy is the share of all comments where the filter was right. It adds the two correct cells and divides by everything.

accuracy = (TP + TN) / (TP + FP + FN + TN)

The top counts every correct verdict: spam that was deleted plus genuine that was kept. The bottom is all the comments that were judged.

The lecture compares two filters. Filter A deletes nothing. It is right about every genuine comment and wrong about every spam comment: 688 of 1,000 right, accuracy 0.688, zero spam caught. Filter B, this week's tree, is right on 298 + 679 = 977 of 1,000, accuracy 0.977, and catches 298 of 312 spam.

The trap is that 0.688 sounds respectable: almost seven in ten, for doing nothing. The data is lopsided (genuine comments far outnumber spam), so a useless model that always says "genuine" scores well for free. This number is the majority baseline, the accuracy of always predicting the larger class. It is the floor every model must beat, not a target. And neither 0.977 nor 0.688 says which mistakes were made. The lecture's rule: "Never ask how accurate. Ask which mistakes, at what price."

Trap

Accuracy flatters on lopsided data. When most cases belong to one class, a model that ignores the rare class still scores high. Always compare accuracy with the majority baseline, and always report the two errors separately. If a question gives you a stream where 95 percent of cases are one class, the do-nothing model already scores 0.95, so a model at 0.96 has done very little.

Precision and recall: who feels each

On the exam

Precision and recall look at the two errors one at a time. Both put true positives on top; they differ in what they divide by.

Precision: how clean is the delete pile

precision = spam caught / all deleted = TP / (TP + FP)

The bottom is everything the filter deleted: the spam it caught plus the genuine comments it deleted by mistake. Precision asks: of the comments I removed, what share deserved it? The only error in this formula is FP, the false alarm.

For the tree: 298 / (298 + 9) = 298 / 307 = 0.971. Of every 100 deleted comments, about 97 were truly spam. The lecture names who feels precision: the user whose genuine comment vanished, and the teammate who re-checks the delete pile by hand.

Recall: how much spam got caught

recall = spam caught / all spam = TP / (TP + FN)

The bottom is all the spam that actually arrived: the spam caught plus the spam that slipped through. Recall asks: of all the spam out there, what share did I remove? The only error in this formula is FN, the miss.

For the tree: 298 / (298 + 14) = 298 / 312 = 0.955. So 14 spam comments stayed visible. The lecture names who feels recall: the audience that still sees scam links, and the brand whose comment section keeps rotting.

To keep them apart: precision divides by what the filter did (the delete column), recall by what the world delivered (the spam row). Precision audits the delete pile; recall audits the stream.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The two error measures: precision and recall (2 measures)

  • Precision: how clean is the delete pile. TP / (TP + FP) = spam caught / all deleted; tree: 298 / 307 = 0.971. Felt by the user whose genuine comment vanished, and the teammate who re-checks the delete pile by hand.
  • Recall: how much spam got caught. TP / (TP + FN) = spam caught / all spam; tree: 298 / 312 = 0.955. Felt by the audience that still sees scam links, and the brand whose comment section keeps rotting.

How to recall it: precision is hurt by false positives (customers deleted); recall is hurt by false negatives (spam left up).

Exam tip

When a question asks "who is hurt if precision falls?", think of the false positive: a genuine voice deleted, so the commenter and the person checking the delete pile. When it asks "who is hurt if recall falls?", think of the false negative: spam left up, so the audience and the brand's comment section.

You cannot maximise both: the dial and F1

On the exam

Precision and recall pull against each other, and the threshold moves them. Raise the dial (stricter, say 0.5 to 0.9) and the filter deletes only what it is very sure about: it deletes less and re-checks more, so precision climbs, but borderline spam survives and recall falls. Lower the dial (looser, say 0.5 to 0.1) and it deletes more and catches more, so recall climbs, but more genuine voices are lost and precision falls. One dial, two errors moving in opposite directions. The extremes make it plain: a threshold of 1.0 deletes nothing (no false alarms, zero recall), and 0 deletes everything (perfect recall, every customer silenced).

Framework to remember

You cannot maximise both: the dial (2 directions)

  • Stricter, raise the dial: delete less, re-check more; precision climbs; more spam survives, so recall falls.
  • Looser, lower the dial: delete more, catch more; recall climbs; more genuine voices lost, so precision falls.

How to recall it: one dial, two errors moving in opposite directions. F1, the harmonic mean of precision and recall (0.963 for the tree), ranks models quickly, but it never replaces the pricing question.

The F1 score

To rank models quickly with one number, the F1 score combines precision and recall using the harmonic mean, an average pulled down hard by whichever of the two is smaller.

F1 = 2 × precision × recall / (precision + recall)

Multiply precision by recall, double it, and divide by their sum. If both are equal, F1 equals that value. If they are far apart, F1 sits close to the smaller one. F1 ignores true negatives completely: it looks only at the spam problem.

Worked example: checking the tree's F1 of 0.963

Precision = 0.971 and recall = 0.955.

Top: 2 × 0.971 × 0.955 = 2 × 0.927305 = 1.85461.

Bottom: 0.971 + 0.955 = 1.926.

F1 = 1.85461 / 1.926 = 0.9629, which rounds to 0.963, the lecture's number. You can also do it straight from the counts, with no rounding: F1 = 2TP / (2TP + FP + FN) = 596 / (596 + 9 + 14) = 596 / 619 = 0.963.

Why not the ordinary average? Because a filter should not hide a terrible score behind a great one. In my own illustration, a filter with precision 1.00 and recall 0.10 misses nine spam in ten. The ordinary average is 0.55, which sounds half decent; F1 is 2 × 1.00 × 0.10 / 1.10 = 0.18, which tells the truth. The lecture adds a limit: F1 ranks models, but it never replaces the pricing question. It treats a missed spam and a lost customer as equally bad, which a real brand rarely does.

The dial in action

On the exam

The lecture shows one model at five thresholds on the hidden 1,000. The model is not named at this point, but the counts are those of the logistic regression filter (they reappear in the TeaPop posture table below); the tree caught 298 at 0.5, not 300.

ThresholdSpam caught, of 312Genuine lost, of 688
Keep everything (t = 1.0)0 (0%)0
t = 0.9267 (85.6%)8
t = 0.5300 (96.2%)9
t = 0.1302 (96.8%)37
Delete everything (t = 0)312 (100%)688 (all)

As the threshold drops, both columns can only rise: a lower cutoff deletes everything a higher one did, plus more. The spam percentages are recall (267 / 312 = 0.856, and so on); the genuine column counts false positives. Now read the steps. From 0.9 to 0.5 the filter catches 33 more spam for only 1 more genuine comment lost, a very good trade. From 0.5 to 0.1 it catches only 2 more spam for 28 more genuine lost, a poor trade for most brands. The errors do not move at a steady rate; the case calls this "the dial is asymmetric". The two end rows are the free filters, and neither is useful. The lecture's line: "Where the dial sits decides who pays: the audience, or the commenter."

My own arithmetic from the lecture's counts: precision at each middle setting

At t = 0.9 the filter deletes 267 + 8 = 275 comments, so precision = 267 / 275 = 0.971. At t = 0.5 it deletes 300 + 9 = 309, so precision = 300 / 309 = 0.971. At t = 0.1 it deletes 302 + 37 = 339, so precision = 302 / 339 = 0.891. Recall rises from 0.856 to 0.962 to 0.968 across the same steps. This shows the dial rule in real numbers: loosening the dial raises recall and, past a point, drags precision down.

Two errors, two prices

On the exam

You cannot choose a threshold until you know what each error costs. The lecture prices them in words. A miss (spam left up, a false negative) means the audience keeps seeing scam links, trust in the comment section erodes, and the campaign pays because its engagement starts to look fake. A false alarm (genuine deleted, a false positive) means a real customer is silenced and notices, creators and repeat buyers drift away, and the brand gets support tickets and public complaints.

Framework to remember

Two errors, two prices (2 errors, 3 consequences each)

Spam left up: a miss (FN)Genuine deleted: a false alarm (FP)
The audience keeps seeing scam linksA real customer is silenced, and notices
Trust in the comment section erodesCreators and repeat buyers drift away
The campaign pays: engagement reads as fakeSupport tickets and public complaints

How to recall it: "Price the two errors separately; then set the dial." The miss hurts the audience, then trust, then the campaign; the false alarm hurts the customer, then loyalty, then the support desk.

The lecture's rule: "Price the two errors separately; then set the dial." Do not pick 0.5 because it is the default, or whatever scores the best accuracy. First ask which error hurts this brand more, now. If misses are expensive, lower the threshold and accept more false alarms. If false alarms are expensive, raise it and accept more misses.

Prices change with the situation. A discussion question asks: a luxury launch post versus a flash-sale post, which fears a missed scam link more? The course gives no model answer, so reason it out: who reads these comments, what would a scam link do to them, and what would a deleted genuine comment do? A flash sale draws buyers ready to click and pay, an easy target for scam links that cost followers money; a luxury launch lives on image, where both visible spam and a silenced customer can hurt. Good answers name both errors, say who pays each, and then move the dial with a reason.

Every threshold on one picture

On the exam

A curve shows every threshold at once. The lecture plots the logistic regression filter on the same split: each threshold gives one point, and joining the points gives the curve. Textbooks call this a receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve; the lecture does not use that name, so use the course's words on the exam.

The vertical axis is the catch rate: spam deleted, as a share of all spam. It is the same as recall, also called the true positive rate. Higher is better. The horizontal axis is the false-alarm rate: genuine comments deleted, as a share of all genuine comments, also called the false positive rate. Lower is better. It is not 1 minus precision: it divides by all 688 genuine comments, not by the delete pile.

true positive rate = TP / (TP + FN)     false positive rate = FP / (FP + TN)

The first is the share of real spam the filter caught. The second is the share of real customers the filter wrongly deleted. Each threshold gives one pair of these numbers, and so one point.

The lecture gives four reading rules. Every threshold is one point. Up and left is better: more catch, fewer false alarms, so a good model's curve hugs the top-left corner. The dotted diagonal is a coin flip: a random guesser catches spam at the same rate it wrongly deletes customers, so its points lie on the line from bottom-left to top-right; a useful model sits well above it. And choosing a point is a pricing decision, not a technical one: the curve shows the options, not which to take.

Framework to remember

Reading the threshold curve (4 reading rules)

  • Every threshold is one point: catch rate (up) against false-alarm rate (across).
  • Up and left is better: more catch, fewer false alarms.
  • The dotted diagonal is a coin flip: a useful model sits well above it.
  • Choosing a point is a pricing decision: not a technical one.

How to recall it: point, corner, coin flip, price.

Worked example: placing the three labelled points

The lecture's chart is zoomed to the working region (catch rate from 0.80 to 1.00, false-alarm rate from 0 to about 0.15), so the diagonal is outside the visible window. The labelled points can be computed from the five-threshold table above.

At t = 0.9: catch rate = 267 / 312 = 0.856; false-alarm rate = 8 / 688 = 0.012.

At t = 0.5: catch rate = 300 / 312 = 0.962; false-alarm rate = 9 / 688 = 0.013.

At t = 0.1: catch rate = 302 / 312 = 0.968; false-alarm rate = 37 / 688 = 0.054.

Reading: from 0.9 to 0.5 the curve goes almost straight up (a big gain in catch for almost no extra false alarms). From 0.5 to 0.1 it goes almost flat to the right (more false alarms for almost no extra catch). The shape of the curve is the asymmetry of the dial, drawn.

Two thresholds make a review queue

On the exam

One threshold makes two piles. Two thresholds make three, and the middle pile goes to a human.

BandScoreWhat happens
Keepbelow 0.1Published straight through. Most comments live here.
Review0.1 to 0.9A human reads these. Slow but safe.
Delete0.9 or higherRemoved at once. A sample is audited weekly.

The logic is automation where the model is sure, human judgment where it is not. Very low scores are almost certainly genuine, so they publish. Very high scores are almost certainly spam, so they go, with a weekly sample audit to keep the delete pile honest. The uncertain middle, where both errors are most likely, goes to a person. The lecture's line: "One threshold makes a filter; two thresholds make a workflow."

Core framework: learn this by heart

Two dials make a review queue: keep, review, delete (3 bands, in order of score)

  1. Keep (score below 0.1): publish straight through; most comments live here.
  2. Review (score 0.1 to 0.9): a human reads these; slow but safe.
  3. Delete (score 0.9 or higher): removed at once; audit a sample weekly.

How to recall it: "One threshold makes a filter; two thresholds make a workflow": automation where the model is sure, human judgment where it is not.

Moving the boundaries changes the queue. A wider review band means more staff time and fewer machine errors. A narrower band hands comments back to the machine. Raising the lower boundary sends some comments straight to publish, risking more missed spam; lowering the upper boundary sends some straight to deletion, risking more genuine comments deleted. Without the true labels of the moved comments, you can say a risk may rise, but not by how much.

My own arithmetic: how big would TeaPop's review band be?

The five-threshold table counts what scores at 0.1 or above and at 0.9 or above, so you can find the middle band for the logistic regression filter on the hidden 1,000.

Score 0.1 or higher: 302 spam + 37 genuine = 339 comments. Score 0.9 or higher: 267 spam + 8 genuine = 275 comments. So the review band (0.1 up to 0.9) holds 339 − 275 = 64 comments: 302 − 267 = 35 spam and 37 − 8 = 29 genuine. The keep band holds 1,000 − 339 = 661 comments, of which 312 − 302 = 10 are spam that still slip through. The delete band holds 275 comments, 8 of them genuine.

Reading: a person reads 64 comments in 1,000 instead of 1,000. If the reviewers judge correctly, the 29 genuine comments in the band are saved and the 35 spam are removed, so compared with the single threshold at 0.9, the queue catches 35 more spam, and compared with the single threshold at 0.1, it saves 29 more customers. The price is human time on 64 comments, plus the 10 misses in the keep band and the 8 false alarms in the delete band, which the queue does not touch.

Which error would you price higher? The discussion questions

The first question (luxury launch versus flash sale) was covered under pricing above. The second asks: for TeaPop's first pass, auto-delete at 0.9, or send everything scoring 0.1 and up to a reviewer? On the hidden 1,000, auto-deleting at 0.9 needs no human time but leaves 312 − 267 = 45 spam up and deletes 8 customers. Sending everything from 0.1 up to a reviewer means reading 339 comments per 1,000, heavy for a team of four, but reviewers could remove up to 302 spam and rescue all 37 genuine comments in that pile. You gain on both errors and pay in staff time. The three-band design, auto-delete at 0.9 and review only the middle, sits between the two. The third question, who should own the dial, is a governance question that the exam does not test.

Three models: logistic regression, decision trees, random forests

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

The middle of the lecture introduces the three models behind the numbers. The TeaPop case compares all three and the mock exam asks you to read a tree and judge a split, so you need to know what each does and what it gives up. The lecture places them on one line, with interpretability (can you defend a decision?) at one end and predictive power (does it catch what simple rules miss?) at the other.

Framework to remember

The three candidate filters (3 models, from most readable to most powerful)

  1. Logistic regression: reads as a readable scorecard (full weights); accuracy 0.979, F1 0.966, 300 of 312 spam caught, 9 of 688 genuine lost.
  2. Decision tree (depth 6): reads as a flowchart (full flowchart); accuracy 0.977, F1 0.963, 298 of 312 caught, 9 of 688 lost.
  3. Random forest (200 trees): reads as votes only; accuracy 0.980, F1 0.968, 301 of 312 caught, 9 of 688 lost.

How to recall it: scorecard, flowchart, votes. "Accuracy is a tie; the choice rides on what you can defend." Always run the simple model first.

Logistic regression: the readable scorecard

Logistic regression adds up the signals, each with its own weight, into one score, then squeezes that score through an S-shaped curve into a probability between 0 and 1. Each signal has one readable weight, so you can say what drives the score: here repeated characters push hardest toward spam (+2.57), and being verified pulls toward genuine (−0.66). Its weakness is that it draws only smooth, simple boundaries and misses signals that matter only in combination. The lecture calls it the benchmark every fancier model must beat.

The full list of its strengths: it is fast on huge data, each signal gets a readable weight (repeated characters +2.57, contact keywords +0.98, verification −0.66), and you can say which signals drive the score. Its weaknesses: only smooth, simple boundaries; it misses interactions (signals that matter only together) that a tree would catch; and weights can mislead when signals move together. Its S-shaped curve takes fifteen signals in and gives one probability out: near 0 means almost certainly genuine, near 1 almost certainly spam, and keep or delete is a cutoff on that probability chosen by a person.

How a decision tree asks questions

A decision tree is a flowchart of yes/no questions. Each node tests one feature. The branches are the answers: yes goes one way, no the other. The leaves are the verdicts, keep or delete, each with a probability. To classify a comment, start at the top and follow its answers down to a leaf. This week's tree opens with "identical text posted 3 or more times?" and, on the yes branch, "posted before 21:30 or after?", going six questions deep. Reading a tree is careful following. In my own example, a tree asks "price keyword?" (no: keep; yes: next question), then "fewer than 10 followers?" (yes: delete; no: keep). A comment with a price keyword from a 250-follower account passes the first question, answers no at the second, and is kept. Follow only the path the case takes, and check each boundary exactly.

Framework to remember

A decision tree asks yes/no questions: nodes, branches, leaves (3 parts, read top to bottom)

  1. Nodes: each tests one feature, one question each.
  2. Branches: the answers; yes goes one way, no the other.
  3. Leaves: the verdicts, keep or delete, with a probability.

How to recall it: question, answer, verdict. A case meets only the questions on its own path.

Worked example: the mock exam's tree question

The mock exam asked exactly this. A small tree first asks whether a message contains a link. If no, it keeps the message. If yes, it asks whether the account is less than seven days old: yes leads to deletion, no leads to keeping. What happens to a message with a link from a ten-day-old account?

Step 1. Link? Yes, so go down the yes branch to the second question.

Step 2. Account less than seven days old? Ten days is not less than seven, so the answer is no.

Step 3. The no branch ends in a leaf: keep. The message follows the link branch and is kept at the age question.

The wrong options tempt you to stop at the first question ("it has a link, so delete"), to flip the boundary ("ten is below seven"), or to ask for a feature the tree never uses (followers). The tree uses only the questions on its own path.

Pure and mixed splits

The tree grows greedily: at each step it asks the single most informative question and never goes back. An informative question separates spam from genuine. A branch is pure when it holds only one label, and mixed when it still holds both. A question that leaves both branches as mixed as the starting node has separated nothing and is discarded. How informative a question is gets measured by the impurity (the amount of mixing) it removes. The process then repeats on each branch, and the tree grows until it is stopped.

Worked example: the root question, measured

On the 3,000 training comments (about 31 percent spam), the root question is "identical text posted 3 or more times?" The no branch holds 2,410 comments, 14.8 percent spam. The yes branch holds 590 comments, 98.1 percent spam. One question produced a nearly pure spam branch and a mostly genuine one. That is what a tree searches for: copy-paste posting is the loudest spam signal in this stream.

The mock exam tests this with a 50/50 node. A split whose two branches are still half spam and half genuine has done nothing. A split that sends all spam one way and all genuine the other makes two pure branches and is the best possible. Equal branch sizes do not matter; the mix inside each branch does.

Worked example: the mock exam's split question

A node holds 40 spam and 40 genuine messages. Split X gives two branches, each with 20 spam and 20 genuine. Split Y gives one branch with 40 spam and one with 40 genuine. Which split separates the labels better?

Split X: each branch is still 50 percent spam, exactly as mixed as the node it started from. It has separated nothing, even though its branches are equal in size.

Split Y: each branch holds only one label. Both branches are pure. Y is the better split, and it is the best split possible at this node.

Depth, overfitting and the forest

A tree that keeps asking questions until every leaf is pure memorises the training file, noise included, and does worse on new comments. This is overfitting: learning history too well and new comments too little. Stopped too early, a tree underfits and misses real patterns. In the lecture's sweep, test accuracy peaks at depth 5 (0.980), the shipped depth-6 tree scores 0.977, and by depth 12 training accuracy reaches 0.991 while test accuracy sinks to 0.960. Only the hidden test set reveals this. A random forest grows many trees (200 here), each on a random slice of comments with a random subset of signals, so their mistakes differ and the majority vote cancels much of the noise. The price is that the flowchart is gone: a forest answers with votes. On the hidden 1,000 the three models nearly tie (accuracy 0.979, 0.977 and 0.980), which is exactly the case report that follows. The lecture's advice: always run the simple model first.

The tree's strengths: it reads as a flowchart, the only model a committee can audit line by line; it handles mixed data without rescaling; and it scores 0.977 on new comments at depth 6. Its weaknesses: it is unstable (small changes in the data grow a different tree), its greedy growth is short-sighted, and it is rarely the most accurate model on its own. The fix is to grow many trees and let them vote.

Why averaging works: each tree errs on different comments, and the random slices and random signals guarantee it. A majority vote keeps only the shared signal, and individual noise votes itself out. The forest also gives a ranking of which signals carry the score: identical texts lead at 0.202, because copy-paste is the loudest spam signal. Treat that ranking as a briefing, not an audit; it is biased toward signals with many distinct values. The lecture's advice on the maths: you do not need it, you need to recognise the words when vendors use them.

When does the forest earn its keep? On this week's stream, all three models sit within 0.003 accuracy of each other, because clean, well-behaved signals leave few interactions to exploit. The forest pays off with messier signals and strong interactions, when the last 0.003 is worth losing the flowchart, and when nobody has to defend a single decision.

The TeaPop case: the consultant's report

On the exam

TeaPop is a milk-tea brand. Its one-week Kuaishou campaign is over: six videos, 4,000 comments, 31.2 percent removed as spam, and a social team of 4 people who cannot read everything. They want a filter that keeps customer voices and removes spam before the next campaign. The case's question: which comment does a person handle? A consultant tested three filters on 1,000 held-out comments (312 spam, 688 genuine, answers hidden during training).

FilterAccuracyF1Spam caughtGenuine deleted
Logistic regression0.9790.966300 of 3129 of 688
Decision tree (depth 6)0.9770.963298 of 3129 of 688
Random forest (200 trees)0.9800.968301 of 3129 of 688

The report's instruction: "Read both error columns. The accuracy column alone cannot tell these filters apart." The accuracies differ by at most 0.003, which is 3 verdicts in 1,000. The error columns show why: all three delete exactly 9 genuine comments, and they differ only in spam caught (298, 300, 301, so 14, 12 and 11 missed). The filters are essentially tied, and picking the forest "for the highest accuracy" is choosing on noise.

Worked example: rebuilding the report from the four cells

Each row of the report is a confusion matrix in disguise. For logistic regression: TP = 300, FN = 312 − 300 = 12, FP = 9, TN = 688 − 9 = 679. Accuracy = (300 + 679) / 1,000 = 0.979. Precision = 300 / 309 = 0.971. Recall = 300 / 312 = 0.962. F1 = 600 / (600 + 9 + 12) = 600 / 621 = 0.966.

For the forest: TP = 301, FN = 11, FP = 9, TN = 679. Accuracy = 980 / 1,000 = 0.980. F1 = 602 / (602 + 9 + 11) = 602 / 622 = 0.968.

All three filters have precision 0.971 because they each delete 9 genuine comments next to roughly 300 spam. The whole difference between them sits in 1 to 3 missed spam comments.

Since the numbers cannot choose, something else must. The case answers: interpretability (see what the report does not say).

Strict, balanced, sweep: one filter, three postures

On the exam

The report's second table shows the logistic regression filter at three thresholds. Each setting is a posture, a stance the brand takes toward the two errors.

SettingThresholdSpam caughtGenuine deleted
STRICT0.9267 of 3128 of 688
BALANCED0.5300 of 3129 of 688
SWEEP0.1302 of 31237 of 688

Read the names carefully. STRICT is strict about deleting: it needs a score of 0.9 before removing anything, so it protects customers and lets more spam through (45 missed). SWEEP sweeps the section clean: anything from 0.1 up goes, so it misses only 10 spam but deletes 37 customers. BALANCED sits at 0.5 (12 missed, 9 deleted). The case's line: "Strict misses more spam; sweep deletes more customers. The dial is a business decision, not a technical one." The model is the same in all three rows; only the threshold, and so the bill to each group, changes.

Core framework: learn this by heart

One filter, three postures: strict, balanced, sweep (3 settings, from highest to lowest threshold)

  1. STRICT (0.9): 267 of 312 spam caught, 8 of 688 genuine deleted: protects customers, misses 45 spam.
  2. BALANCED (0.5): 300 of 312 caught, 9 of 688 deleted: misses 12 spam.
  3. SWEEP (0.1): 302 of 312 caught, 37 of 688 deleted: misses only 10 spam, but deletes 37 customers.

How to recall it: "Strict misses more spam; sweep deletes more customers." Same logistic regression model in all three; the threshold is the dial between them.

Exam tip

When you defend a posture, always quote both error counts, for example "BALANCED: 12 spam missed and 9 customers deleted out of 1,000". An answer that defends a setting with accuracy alone misses the point of the case.

The number that flatters, and what the errors cost

On the exam

An intern added a line to the report: a filter that deletes nothing at all is already 68.8 percent accurate on this stream. True, and that is the problem. It catches 0 of 312 spam, so every scam link stays up, and deletes 0 of 688 genuine comments, so no customer is wronged. One error column is perfect, the other a disaster, and the accuracy hides which. The case's rule: "Accuracy flatters on lopsided streams. Report the two errors separately, or the number lies by omission." The number is not false; it leaves out the fact that matters.

The case then puts faces on the two errors. A missed spam is a scam link under the brand's own video: followers click, lose money, and blame TeaPop. Scam links are 9.0 percent of this stream, so this is not rare (by my arithmetic, 9.0 / 31.2 = 0.29, close to three in ten spam comments). A deleted genuine comment is a customer who asked about ingredients and watched her question vanish; she now believes the brand censors criticism, and some like her complain in public or never come back. The case's line: "Two errors, two different payers. The threshold chooses who pays."

My own arithmetic: accuracy across the three postures

Accuracy = (spam caught + genuine kept) / 1,000. STRICT: (267 + 680) / 1,000 = 0.947. BALANCED: (300 + 679) / 1,000 = 0.979. SWEEP: (302 + 651) / 1,000 = 0.953. Accuracy would always pick BALANCED. But a brand facing a scam wave may rationally prefer SWEEP, and a brand in a censorship row may rationally prefer STRICT. Accuracy gives every error the same price, so it cannot make that choice.

Your tasks for TeaPop: ship a setting, answer the appeal, write the contract line

On the exam

The case ends with three tasks for the team. Each one tests the same skill: decide with both error counts, never with accuracy alone. The discussion that follows asks the same three things as team questions: which setting do you ship and what is your two-error defence; the appeal lands tomorrow morning, so which filter is in production and what does the reply concede; and does your contract line kill the filter that catches nothing? The format is one setting, one filter, one sentence per team.

Framework to remember

The three TeaPop tasks (3 tasks, in this order)

  1. Ship one setting: STRICT, BALANCED or SWEEP; defend the pick with both error counts, not with accuracy.
  2. Answer the appeal: a food blogger with 30,000 followers posts "TeaPop deleted my question about sugar levels. Censorship!" Which filter do you want in production when you draft the public reply, and what does the reply say?
  3. Write the contract line: one sentence of acceptance criterion, using the two kinds of error, so that 68.8 percent while catching nothing can never pass again.

How to recall it: setting, appeal, contract: one setting, one filter, one sentence.

Task 1: ship one setting

A good answer names the setting, quotes both error counts out of the hidden 1,000, and says which error the brand prices higher. The course gives no single correct pick; the defence is what earns the marks. A typical defence of BALANCED: "BALANCED misses 12 spam and deletes 9 customers. Moving to STRICT saves only 1 customer but lets 33 more spam through; moving to SWEEP catches only 2 more spam but deletes 28 more customers. Both moves give up a lot to gain a little, so we stay at 0.5." STRICT can be defended only if one wrongly deleted customer is very costly (for example, during a public censorship row). SWEEP can be defended only if each missed scam link is very costly (for example, during a scam wave).

Task 2: answer the appeal

The appeal is a false positive in public: a genuine question about sugar levels, deleted. Here is how I would reason it out (the course gives no model answer). Which filter you want in production is a question about interpretability, not accuracy. With the decision tree you can walk her comment down the flowchart and say which question sent it to deletion; with logistic regression, the readable scorecard, you can say which signals pushed its score up. With the random forest you can only say that 200 trees voted, which is no public reply. So the reply is drafted with the tree or the logistic regression in production. What the reply concedes: the deletion was an error, the kind every setting makes (BALANCED deletes 9 genuine comments in 1,000). What it says: the question is restored and answered, the reason the filter flagged it is explained in plain words, and the brand states what it changes, for example sending borderline scores to a human reviewer.

Task 3: write the contract line

The contract line must name both errors, so a filter cannot pass by being perfect on one and useless on the other. The do-nothing filter scores 68.8 percent accuracy while catching 0 of 312 spam, so any criterion written in accuracy alone lets it pass.

Worked example: a contract line, tested (my own illustration of the thresholds)

Contract line: "On at least 1,000 held-out comments whose labels were hidden during training, the filter must catch at least 95 percent of spam and wrongly delete no more than 2 percent of genuine comments."

Do-nothing filter: catches 0 of 312 = 0 percent. Fails. The 68.8 percent can never pass again.

BALANCED: catches 300 of 312 = 96.2 percent; deletes 9 of 688 = 1.3 percent. Passes.

STRICT: catches 267 of 312 = 85.6 percent. Fails on spam caught.

SWEEP: catches 96.8 percent, but deletes 37 of 688 = 5.4 percent. Fails on genuine deleted.

The exact percentages (95 and 2) are my own choice; what the task requires is one sentence that sets a floor on spam caught and a ceiling on genuine deleted, measured on held-out data.

The dial is asymmetric

On the exam

The case reads the posture table as steps away from BALANCED, turning it into a trade-off you can state in one sentence.

Worked example: pricing each step of the dial

BALANCED to STRICT (0.5 up to 0.9): genuine deleted goes from 9 down to 8, so it saves exactly 1 customer. Spam caught goes from 300 down to 267, so it lets 33 more spam through.

BALANCED to SWEEP (0.5 down to 0.1): spam caught goes from 300 up to 302, so it catches 2 more spam. Genuine deleted goes from 9 up to 37, so it deletes 28 more genuine voices.

Reading, in the case's words: one step strict is cheap on paper and expensive in spam; one step sweep is the reverse. In both directions you give up a lot to gain a little, which is why BALANCED sits near the corner of the threshold curve.

"Asymmetric" means the two sides of a move are not the same size: the dial swaps 1 for 33, or 2 for 28. A brand should leave BALANCED only if it prices one error far above the other. STRICT pays off only if one deleted customer costs more than 33 visible spam comments, as in a public censorship storm. SWEEP pays off only if 2 more spam caught are worth more than 28 silenced customers, as when each missed scam link is very costly during a scam wave. The case's closing question: where on that dial is the brand safe?

Trap

Do not assume a move of the dial changes both errors by similar amounts. Here the step from 0.5 to 0.9 and the step from 0.5 to 0.1 behave very differently. Always read the actual counts at each setting before you claim what a move will do.

What the report does not say: interpretability is operational

On the exam

The case closes with two lessons the consultant's tables do not state. The first repeats the accuracy warning with the case's extremes: 0.688 for deleting nothing, 0.980 for the best filter. Report the two errors separately.

The second breaks the tie. Interpretability is operational: explaining a decision is not a nice extra but part of running the filter. When a commenter appeals in public that her comment was deleted, the brand must say why. With the tree it can walk her comment down the flowchart, question by question. With logistic regression, the readable scorecard, it can say which signals pushed the score up. With the forest it can only say that 200 trees voted, which is no public reply. The case's line: "The appeal is answered line by line only with the tree or the readable scorecard. The forest's extra 0.001 buys nothing in a public reply", the 0.001 being the gap between 0.980 and 0.979.

Framework to remember

What the report does not say (2 lessons)

  • Accuracy flatters: 0.688 for deleting nothing; 0.980 for the best filter. Report the two errors separately, or the number lies by omission.
  • Interpretability is operational: the appeal is answered line by line only with the tree or the readable scorecard; the forest's extra 0.001 buys nothing in a public reply.

How to recall it: pick the posture you can defend, then price it in both error columns.

The case takeaway: pick the posture you can defend, then price it in both error columns. Every posture is one answer to the week's decision: which comment a person handles.

Exam tip

If an exam question shows models with near-equal scores and asks which to deploy, the expected reasoning is: the scores cannot separate them, so choose on an operational property such as whether you can explain each decision to the people affected. Name the model and the reason.

Who joins the private domain: the same two errors

On the exam

The second case of the week carries the logic to a new decision: not which comment a person handles, but who gets invited in. A skincare brand sells in a nightly livestream room on Douyin. After each stream it can invite viewers into its private domain, the owned channel where a brand messages buyers directly, such as a WeChat Work (企业微信) group. Messages there cost no media spend, but every invited viewer costs staff time.

The same two errors appear. Inviting a viewer who never buys again spends staff time on someone who only browses: a false positive. Leaving out a viewer who would have come back loses repeat revenue: a false negative. The two errors carry different prices, so the cut is a business choice. The decision: which tier of livestream viewers gets an invitation into the private domain, and which tier stays on public reach.

Framework to remember

One room, three tiers, three Monday actions (3 tiers, from strongest to weakest evidence)

  1. Tier one: watches most streams and has bought more than once → invite into the private domain and give the group a named host.
  2. Tier two: watches often but has bought once → invite with a first repeat offer, then review after one month.
  3. Tier three: watched a single stream and has never bought → keep on public reach; no invitation, no staff time.

How to recall it: the same two errors as the filter. Inviting a browser wastes staff time (false positive); leaving out a returner loses repeat revenue (false negative). The cut, like the threshold, is set by what each error costs.

TierWhoMonday action
Tier oneWatches most streams and has bought more than onceInvite into the private domain and give the group a named host
Tier twoWatches often but has bought onceInvite with a first repeat offer, then review after one month
Tier threeWatched a single stream and has never boughtKeep on public reach; no invitation, no staff time

One way to read the tiers is as the review queue in another form. Tier one has strong evidence, so the brand acts at once and invests a named host. Tier three has weak evidence, so it spends nothing. Tier two is the uncertain middle: invite with an offer, then review after a month, much like a human check. The course says these tiers are illustrative, not a reading of campaign data. Its point is that the cut, like the threshold, is set by what each error costs.

The short version

A classifier gives a probability; a person turns it into a verdict by choosing a threshold, so the model proposes and the threshold disposes. Every verdict lands in one of four cells, and the two wrong cells hurt different people: a false positive deletes a real customer, a false negative leaves spam up for the audience. Build the confusion matrix from the counts (for the tree: TP 298, FP 9, FN 14, TN 679), then remember that precision is TP / (TP + FP), the cleanliness of the delete pile, and recall is TP / (TP + FN), the share of spam caught. Accuracy flatters on lopsided data: deleting nothing scores 0.688 here, so always report both errors. Raising the threshold raises precision and lowers recall, and lowering it does the reverse; F1 (0.963 for the tree) ranks models but never sets the dial. The curve of every threshold plots catch rate against false-alarm rate, with up and left better and the diagonal a coin flip. Two thresholds make a review queue: keep below 0.1, review 0.1 to 0.9, delete 0.9 and above. In the TeaPop case the three filters tie on accuracy, the dial is asymmetric (BALANCED to STRICT saves 1 customer for 33 more spam; BALANCED to SWEEP catches 2 more spam for 28 more customers), and the tie is broken by interpretability, because a brand must be able to explain a deletion in public.

Practice

P4.1 A fictional food-delivery app screens restaurant reviews with a small decision tree. It first asks: "Was the review posted within 1 hour of the account being created?" If no, it keeps the review. If yes, it asks: "Does the review contain 3 or more exclamation marks?" If yes, it deletes the review; if no, it keeps it. A review with 5 exclamation marks is posted 3 hours after its account was created. What happens to it? (a) It is deleted, because 5 is at least 3. (b) It is kept at the first question, because 3 hours is not within 1 hour. (c) It is sent to the second question, because every review is checked for exclamation marks. (d) It cannot be classified without the follower count.

Show the answer

(b). Follow only the path the review actually takes. The first question asks whether it was posted within 1 hour of account creation. Three hours is not within 1 hour, so the answer is no, and the no branch ends in a leaf: keep. The second question is never asked, so the 5 exclamation marks do not matter. Option (a) is the trap of reading the second question without passing the first. Option (c) misunderstands a tree: each review only meets the questions on its own path.

P4.2 A tree node holds 60 fake and 60 real reviews. Split P gives branch 1 with 50 fake and 10 real, and branch 2 with 10 fake and 50 real. Split Q gives branch 1 with 30 fake and 30 real, and branch 2 with 30 fake and 30 real. Which split should the tree prefer? (a) Q, because its branches are exactly equal in size. (b) Q, because it keeps the original 50/50 mixture. (c) P, because each branch is much closer to containing a single label. (d) Neither, because both keep all 120 reviews.

Show the answer

(c). A tree looks for questions that make the branches as pure as possible, meaning each branch holds mostly one label. Split Q leaves both branches exactly as mixed as the parent node (half fake, half real), so it has separated nothing. Split P sends most fakes one way (50 of 60) and most real reviews the other (50 of 60). It is not perfectly pure, but it is far less mixed. Equal branch sizes, option (a), are irrelevant; what matters is the mix of labels inside each branch.

P4.3 A fictional marketplace tests a fake-review filter on 2,000 hidden reviews, of which 400 are truly fake. The filter flags 380 reviews for removal, and 340 of the flagged reviews are truly fake. Fake is the positive class. (i) Build the confusion matrix. (ii) Compute accuracy, precision, recall and F1. (iii) Compute the accuracy of a filter that removes nothing, and say in one sentence what it hides.

Show the answer

(i) TP = 340 (fake, removed). FP = 380 − 340 = 40 (real, removed). FN = 400 − 340 = 60 (fake, left up). TN = 1,600 − 40 = 1,560 (real, kept), since 2,000 − 400 = 1,600 reviews are real. Check: 340 + 40 + 60 + 1,560 = 2,000.

(ii) Accuracy = (340 + 1,560) / 2,000 = 1,900 / 2,000 = 0.950. Precision = 340 / 380 = 0.895: about 89 of every 100 removed reviews were fake, so about 11 in 100 were honest reviewers silenced. Recall = 340 / 400 = 0.850: the filter caught 85 percent of fakes and left 60 up. F1 = 2 × 340 / (680 + 40 + 60) = 680 / 780 = 0.872. Check with the other form: 2 × 0.895 × 0.850 / (0.895 + 0.850) = 1.5215 / 1.745 = 0.872.

(iii) Remove nothing: right on all 1,600 real reviews, so accuracy = 1,600 / 2,000 = 0.800 while catching 0 of 400 fakes. The 0.800 hides that every fake review stays up; the filter's 0.950 is only 0.15 above doing nothing, so the two error counts (40 honest reviews removed, 60 fakes missed) are what the team should report.

P4.4 A fictional gaming channel routes chat messages by spam score: keep below 0.15, send 0.15 up to but not including 0.85 to a human reviewer, delete 0.85 or more. In one evening the scores fall like this: below 0.15, 610 messages; 0.15 up to 0.50, 45 messages; 0.50 up to 0.85, 30 messages; 0.85 or more, 115 messages. The moderator can review at most 50 messages. (i) How big is the current queue, and by how much does it exceed capacity? (ii) Option A raises only the lower boundary to 0.50. Option B lowers only the upper boundary to 0.50. For each, give the new queue size, where the moved messages go, and the error that may rise. True labels are not known.

Show the answer

(i) Queue = 45 + 30 = 75 messages, which is 75 − 50 = 25 over capacity.

(ii) Option A: the queue becomes the 30 messages from 0.50 up to 0.85, within capacity. The 45 messages scoring 0.15 up to 0.50 now go straight to keep. Risk: some of them may be spam, so missed spam (false negatives) may rise; we cannot say how many without labels. Option B: the queue becomes the 45 messages from 0.15 up to 0.50, also within capacity. The 30 messages scoring 0.50 up to 0.85 now go straight to delete. Risk: some may be genuine, so wrongly deleted messages (false positives) may rise. Both options fix the workload; they differ in which error they risk. A channel that fears complaints about deleted messages would prefer A; a channel that fears scam links in chat would prefer B. In A the keep band grows from 610 to 655 and the delete band stays at 115; in B the keep band stays at 610 and the delete band grows from 115 to 145.

P4.5 A fictional bubble-tea chain tests one filter on 1,000 hidden comments (200 spam, 800 genuine) at three thresholds. At 0.8 it catches 150 spam and deletes 4 genuine. At 0.5 it catches 180 spam and deletes 6 genuine. At 0.2 it catches 186 spam and deletes 30 genuine. In four or five sentences: describe what each move away from 0.5 costs and gains, and recommend a setting for a week when a scam-link wave is expected.

Show the answer

Moving from 0.5 to 0.8 saves 2 genuine comments (6 down to 4) but lets 30 more spam stay up (180 down to 150). Moving from 0.5 to 0.2 catches 6 more spam (180 up to 186) but deletes 24 more genuine comments (6 up to 30). The dial is asymmetric: each move gives up much more of one error than it gains in the other. In a scam-link wave, each missed link can cost followers money and the brand their trust, so the miss becomes the expensive error; the chain might accept 0.2, catching 186 of 200 and paying with 30 deleted customers, but only with an appeal route or a review of the 0.2 to 0.5 band so the extra 24 genuine deletions can be reversed. If the team cannot staff that, 0.5 remains the defensible default because both error counts are low there (200 − 180 = 20 missed, 6 deleted). Note that accuracy would not help here: the do-nothing filter already scores 800 / 1,000 = 0.80.

Week 5 Regression

Predicting a number: residuals and the least-squares line

Background: not examined directly, but needed to understand the exam material

The lecture opens with twenty weeks of one shop's ad spend and sales. The points rise uphill, and a straight line measures how steeply. To say which line is "better", you use the residual, the miss on one point: residual = actual − fitted. A positive residual means the line underestimated that week, and a negative one means it overestimated. Ordinary least squares (OLS) picks the intercept b0 and slope b1 of y-hat = b0 + b1x that make the total squared miss, Σ(yi − y-hati)2, as small as possible. The misses are squared so that positive and negative misses cannot cancel, and so that one big miss costs more than several small ones. The correlation coefficient r (from −1 to +1) measures how closely two columns follow a straight line, but a strong r is not yet a good model.

The output table and the slope read aloud

On the exam

When you fit a regression, a statistics program answers with a table. The course uses a separate Excel example with 30 observations here. It is not the twenty-week table above, so do not mix the two. Learn this table well: the lecture builds it up column by column and then reads it all at the end.

CoefficientStd. errortpLower 95%Upper 95%
Intercept−3.6041.279−2.8180.009−6.225−0.984
Ad spend2.0970.2488.4540.0001.5892.605

Each row is one fitted number. The coefficient column holds the estimate itself. The standard error (SE) says how much that estimate would wobble if you collected a fresh sample, so it measures noise. The t column is the coefficient measured in noise units. The p column says how surprising such a t would be if the true value were zero. The last two columns give the 95 percent confidence interval (CI), a range of believable values for the true coefficient.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The regression output table (6 columns, read left to right)

  1. Coefficient: the estimate. Read aloud: "one more unit of the feature comes with b more units of the target".
  2. Standard error: the noise around the estimate. Read aloud: "give or take about SE".
  3. t: coefficient / standard error. Read aloud: "the estimate sits t noise units away from zero".
  4. p: how surprising this t would be if the true value were zero. Read aloud: "p below 0.05, so reject H0: significant".
  5. Lower 95%: the low end of the range of believable true values.
  6. Upper 95%: the high end. Read the two together: "the interval excludes zero" (significant) or "includes zero" (not significant).

How to recall it: estimate, noise, estimate in noise units, surprise, range. Each row is one fitted number; the intercept row and each feature row read the same way.

Begin with the coefficient on ad spend. Read aloud, it says: "One more unit of ad spend comes with 2.097 more units of sales." The coefficient is the slope of the line: move one unit to the right on the ad-spend axis, and the line rises 2.097 units of sales. The course calls this per-unit economics. A coefficient prices one more unit of the feature, and the price is paid in units of the target. Notice the careful word "comes with", not "causes". The section A coefficient is not a lever below explains why that matters.

Framework to remember

Reading a slope aloud: per-unit economics (3 parts, in this order)

  1. One more unit of the feature: step one unit to the right on the feature axis.
  2. Comes with b more units: the line rises by the coefficient (here 2.097). Say "comes with", never "causes".
  3. Of the target: the price is paid in units of the target (here units of sales).

How to recall it: "A coefficient prices one more unit of the feature. The price is paid in units of the target."

Trap

Do not confuse 0.248 with the slope. The slope is 2.097. The number 0.248 is its standard error, the size of the noise around the slope. The lecture stresses this point: 0.248 remains the standard error, not the slope.

Units, and the intercept as an anchor

On the exam

A coefficient inherits the units of the data. Its unit is "units of the target per unit of the feature". If you report sales in thousands instead of single units, and you keep ad spend in the same units, every sales number becomes a thousand times smaller, so the slope is divided by a thousand: 2.097 becomes 0.002097 thousand units of sales per unit of ad spend. The shop did not change; only the ruler did. The lecture's donut, priced at 1.19 in lev and 0.61 in euro, makes the same point: "the number moves with the unit; the donut does not." So a big coefficient is not automatically an important one. Ask "in which units?" before you compare sizes. This idea returns in the Ridge and LASSO section, where it explains why predictors must be standardized before a penalty is applied.

The intercept, −3.604, is the fitted sales when ad spend is zero. Read literally, it says the shop sells minus 3.6 units with no advertising, which cannot happen. The course's reading is that the intercept is an anchor, not a prediction. The line is a local approximation: it describes the range of ad spend the shop actually used. The intercept fixes where the line sits so that it passes through that cloud of data in the right place. If zero ad spend lies outside the data, the line's value there has no business meaning.

Framework to remember

Units, and why the intercept needs care (2 rules)

  • The coefficient inherits the units: report sales in thousands (ad-spend units fixed) and the slope is divided by a thousand. The number moves with the unit; the donut does not.
  • The intercept is an anchor, not a prediction: −3.604 is fitted sales at zero ad spend. Negative sales cannot exist; the line is a local approximation.

How to recall it: the ruler changes the number, not the shop; the intercept holds the line in place, it does not forecast.

Exam tip

If a question gives you a negative intercept and asks what it means, the full answer has two parts. Say that it is the fitted value when the feature is zero, then say that it is an anchor for a line that only approximates the data range, so it is not a forecast of negative sales.

A coefficient is not a lever

On the exam

The fitted line describes association: what went together in the data. Larger budgets and larger sales moved together. That is not the same as causation, the claim that pushing spend up will push sales up. For example, the shop may simply spend more in busy seasons, when sales would have been high anyway. Proof that more spend causes more sales needs a designed experiment, in which spend is changed on purpose (for example at random across weeks or markets) while everything else is kept comparable. A fitted line alone cannot deliver that proof. The course's rule: "Use coefficients for prediction and prioritisation; bring causal claims to an experiment."

Framework to remember

A coefficient is not a lever: association versus causation (2 sides)

AssociationCausation
The line describes what went together in the data.Proof that more spend causes more sales needs a designed experiment.
Larger budgets and larger sales moved together.A fitted line alone cannot deliver that proof.
Use for: prediction and prioritisation.Use for: causal claims, which go to an experiment.

How to recall it: a coefficient tells you what moved together, not which lever to pull.

Trap

"The coefficient is 2.097 with p = 0.000, so each extra unit of spend will bring 2.097 more units of sales." This is false. The p-value says the association is unlikely to be pure noise. It says nothing about cause. A tiny p-value does not turn a coefficient into a lever you can pull.

Many features at once

On the exam

Real models have several drivers. Multiple regression gives every feature its own coefficient:

yi = β0 + β1·X1 + β2·X2 + … + βp·Xp + εi

yi is the outcome for row i. β0 is the intercept. Each βj is the slope of feature Xj, and there are p features. εi (epsilon) is the error, the part of row i that the features do not explain.

The course example models sales on ad spend, price and streamer hours. The reading rule is the one you will use for the rest of the course: read each coefficient holding the rest fixed. The ad-spend coefficient is the change in predicted sales for one more unit of ad spend while price and streamer hours stay the same. That clause matters. If two features usually move together, the model has little evidence about either one alone, and it splits the credit between them almost arbitrarily. The Week 5 notebook shows this with likes and shares, which mostly echo sessions.

Framework to remember

Reading a multiple regression (one rule, 3 parts)

  1. One coefficient per feature: every feature (ad spend, price, streamer hours) gets its own slope.
  2. One more unit: move only the feature you are reading by one unit.
  3. Held fixed: all the other features stay where they are.

How to recall it: "Every feature gets its own slope. Read each one holding the rest fixed." This is the working reading for the rest of the course.

Is the gap real? The two-campus t-test

On the exam

Before going back to the table, the lecture teaches the logic of testing with a simpler question. Two campuses report mean spend on one common scale. Is the 5-point gap between them real, or just sampling noise?

The test always runs through the same steps. This section and the next two teach each step; the box gives the whole sequence at once.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The hypothesis-test sequence (6 steps, in order)

  1. Null hypothesis H0: state "nothing is there" (the two means are equal; the true coefficient is zero).
  2. Test statistic: t = (data − null value) / standard error, the gap in noise units.
  3. Significance level α and degrees of freedom: fix the false-alarm budget (α = 0.05) before the test; df = n − k sets the shape of the reference curve.
  4. Rejection region: values of the statistic that are rare when H0 is true (two-sided: 2.5 percent in each tail).
  5. p-value: P(data at least this extreme | H0 true), the tail area beyond your statistic.
  6. Decision: p below 0.05, reject H0. Otherwise, do not reject.

How to recall it: assume nothing, measure the gap in noise units, compare it with the rare zone, decide.

CampusStudents (n)Mean spendStandard deviation
Fudan2007512
Tongji1508013

The starting point is the null hypothesis (H0): the two campus means are equal. It plays the role of "nothing is there". Then you measure how far the data sit from that null, in units of noise. That number is the test statistic.

t = (80 − 75) / √(122/200 + 132/150) = 3.68

The top is the data minus the null value: the observed gap, 5, minus the gap of 0 that H0 expects. The bottom is the standard error of the gap. Each campus adds its own noise, its variance (standard deviation squared) divided by its sample size, and the square root turns the total back into points of spend. So t is the gap in noise units.

Worked example: the two-campus t

Step 1, the gap: 80 − 75 = 5 points.

Step 2, Tongji's noise share: 132 = 169, and 169 / 150 = 1.127. Fudan's noise share: 122 = 144, and 144 / 200 = 0.720.

Step 3, add the two shares: 0.720 + 1.127 = 1.847.

Step 4, take the square root to get the standard error of the gap: √1.847 = 1.359 points.

Step 5, divide: t = 5 / 1.359 = 3.68.

Reading: the gap is 3.68 times as large as the noise you would expect in a gap between two samples of this size. The lecture's reading: "A gap of 3.68 noise units is large. Chance alone rarely makes a gap this wide." The business reading is that the Tongji students in this data really do spend more on average than the Fudan students. The difference is not just an accident of who was sampled.

Exam tip

Keep each sample size under its own variance: 144 goes with 200 and 169 goes with 150. Square the standard deviations before dividing, and take the square root only at the end. Write every step. A clear method earns marks even after a slip in the arithmetic.

Significance level, degrees of freedom and the rejection region

On the exam

To turn a t into a decision, you need a rule fixed in advance. The significance level alpha (α) is the probability of rejecting a true null hypothesis, chosen before the test. The common choice is 0.05. The course calls it the false-alarm budget. If nothing is really there, you accept that 5 tests in 100 will still raise an alarm by chance. A smaller α means fewer false alarms but needs stronger evidence.

Degrees of freedom (df) count how many numbers are free to move once the model has used some of the information. The formula is df = n − k: observations minus fitted coefficients. The course example: four numbers have mean 5, so they must add up to 20. Three are reported, 4, 2 and 5, which add to 11, so the last one must be 20 − 11 = 9. Only three numbers were free to move, so df = 3. Fixing the mean used up one degree of freedom. In the regression table, 30 observations and 2 fitted coefficients (intercept and slope) leave df = 30 − 2 = 28. The df set the exact shape of the reference curve that t is compared with.

The rejection region is the set of values of the statistic that would be rare if H0 were true. Picture the bell-shaped curve of values t would take under H0. With α = 0.05 and a two-sided test, the middle 95 percent is "do not reject H0". The outer 2.5 percent in each tail, beyond the two critical values, is "reject H0". The 5 percent budget is split in half because a gap could be extreme in either direction. The campus t of 3.68 lands deep in the right tail, so you reject H0: "a statistic in a tail is rare if H0 is true, so we reject H0." The course deliberately gives no numerical critical values here; what you need is the picture and the logic.

Framework to remember

The rejection region at α = 0.05, two-sided (3 zones)

  • Left tail, 2.5 percent: beyond the lower critical value. Reject H0.
  • Middle, 95 percent: between the two critical values. Do not reject H0.
  • Right tail, 2.5 percent: beyond the upper critical value. Reject H0 (the campus t = 3.68 lands deep here).

How to recall it: 2.5 + 95 + 2.5 = 100. The 5 percent false-alarm budget is split between the two tails.

The p-value and the decision rule

On the exam

p = P(data at least this extreme | H0 true)

The p-value is the probability, calculated assuming the null hypothesis is true, of seeing data at least as extreme as what you observed. On the reference curve under H0, it is the shaded tail area beyond your statistic.

A one-tail p-value counts results extreme in one direction only. A two-tail p-value counts results extreme on either side, and it is the common choice for coefficients, because a coefficient could be wrongly positive or wrongly negative. The decision rule is simple: if p is below 0.05, reject H0. The rejection region and the p-value always agree. A t in the 2.5 percent tail is the same event as a two-tail p below 0.05.

Framework to remember

One-tail versus two-tail p-value (2 types), plus the decision rule

TypeWhat counts as extremeShaded areaWhen used
One-tailExtreme in one direction onlyOne tailWhen only one direction matters
Two-tailExtreme on either sideBoth tailsThe common choice for coefficients

Decision rule: p below 0.05, reject H0. The shaded area under the curve is p.

Be careful about what p is not. It is not the probability that H0 is true, because it is calculated by assuming H0 is true. And it says nothing about whether the effect is big enough to matter for money. That judgment needs the coefficient and its units.

Every number in the table now reads

On the exam

In a regression table the null hypothesis for each row is "the true coefficient is zero", meaning the feature has no linear association with the target. With that in mind, read the ad-spend row from left to right.

Worked example: reading the ad-spend row

The slope. 2.097, with standard error 0.248. One more unit of ad spend comes with 2.097 more units of sales, give or take about a quarter of a unit of noise.

The t. t = coefficient / standard error = 2.097 / 0.248 = 8.46. The printed 8.454 differs in the last digit only because the program divides unrounded numbers. Because the null value is zero, t is simply the coefficient divided by its noise: the slope sits 8.454 noise units away from zero. The same check works on the intercept: −3.604 / 1.279 = −2.818.

The p. 0.000 is a printed rounding. It means p is below 0.0005, not exactly zero. A slope this many noise units from zero would almost never appear if the true slope were zero. Since p < 0.05, reject H0: ad spend is significantly associated with sales. The intercept's p of 0.009 is also below 0.05.

The interval. The 95 percent CI runs from 1.589 to 2.605. It is the coefficient plus or minus about two standard errors (here 0.508, which is 2.05 standard errors at df = 28). The interval excludes zero, which is the third way of reaching the same conclusion as t and p. For the business, the honest statement is a range: each extra unit of spend comes with somewhere between about 1.6 and 2.6 extra units of sales.

The equation as a whole. Below the table the program prints an F test, which asks whether the slopes, taken together, are all zero. F = 71.47 with significance 3.41E-09, that is 0.00000000341, far below 0.05. The equation as a whole holds. With only one feature, F asks the same question as the slope's t, and indeed 8.4542 = 71.47.

Exam tip

Three signals always agree for a single coefficient at α = 0.05: |t| in the rejection tails, p below 0.05, and a 95 percent CI that excludes zero. If an exam table gives you two of them, you can state the third. If the CI includes zero, then p is above 0.05.

From a number to a decision: three discussion questions

On the exam

The first discussion set turns the table into decisions. Here are the three questions, each with a full model answer.

Q1. b = 2.097 with p = 0.000. The shop doubles its ad budget. What does the line predict, and what could go wrong? The line predicts that every extra unit of spend comes with 2.097 extra units of sales. If spend goes from some level x to 2x, the predicted rise is 2.097 × x units. Several things can go wrong. A doubled budget may lie outside the range of spend in the data, where the line is only a local approximation. The coefficient is an association, not a lever, so the extra sales may not appear if spend and sales moved together for another reason, such as seasons. And the CI says the true slope could be as low as 1.589. A sound answer recommends testing the bigger budget in an experiment first.

Q2. A classmate reads the intercept as negative sales at zero ads. What is the right reading? The intercept is the fitted value of the line at zero ad spend. It anchors the line so that the line fits the range of spend actually observed. Negative sales cannot exist, and zero spend is probably outside the data, so it is not a prediction.

Q3. A coefficient in a multiple regression is not significant. Drop the feature? Not automatically. A large p means the data cannot distinguish this feature's effect, holding the others fixed, from zero. It does not prove the effect is zero. The feature may be overlapping with another feature that took its credit, or the sample may be too small. Dropping it can also change the other coefficients. Decide with the business question and with performance on new data, which is exactly the complexity budget later in this chapter.

Three yardsticks, the split of the total miss, and R squared

On the exam

Once the line is drawn, the lecture uses three yardsticks to say how good it is. R squared says how much of the ups and downs the line explains. The residuals show what the misses look like when the model is wrong. The complexity budget tells you when a fancier model stops helping.

Core framework: learn this by heart

How good is this fit? The three yardsticks (3 items, in the order taught)

  1. R squared: how much of the ups and downs the line explains.
  2. The residuals: what the misses look like when the model is wrong.
  3. The complexity budget: when a fancier model stops helping.

How to recall it: how much is explained, what is left over, and when to stop adding.

Σ(yi − ȳ)2 = Σ(y-hati − ȳ)2 + Σ(yi − y-hati)2, that is, SST = SSR + SSE

ȳ ("y-bar") is the mean of the actual values. The total sum of squares (SST) measures how much sales vary at all around their mean. The regression sum of squares (SSR) is the part the line accounts for: how far the fitted values move away from the mean. The error sum of squares (SSE) is what the line still misses: the squared residuals added up. It is the same quantity OLS minimises.

Framework to remember

The total miss splits in two: SST = SSR + SSE (3 sums of squares)

  • TOTAL, SST: total sum of squares, how much sales vary at all around their mean.
  • EXPLAINED, SSR: regression sum of squares, the part the line accounts for.
  • LEFTOVER, SSE: error sum of squares, what the line still misses (the squared residuals).

How to recall it: total = explained + leftover. R squared = explained / total = 1 − leftover / total.

Because the explained part and the leftover add up to the whole, the explained part can be written as a share:

R2 = SSR / SST = 1 − SSE / SST

R squared is the share of the total variation that the line explains. Read the ends: it runs from 0 (the line explains nothing) to 1 (every point sits exactly on the line).

For the 30-observation example, R2 = 0.719. Read it as: the line explains about 72 percent of the weekly swings in sales, and about 28 percent (1 − 0.719 = 0.281) is left in the residuals. Look again at the second form of the formula. On the same training rows, a lower SSE always means a higher R2. That is useful, but it is also the start of a trap, because you can always lower the training SSE by making the model more flexible.

Reading the top of the output

On the exam

The same program output has a block above the coefficient table (same 30 observations). It describes the fit of the whole equation.

Fit measureValueReading
Multiple R0.848Correlation of actual and fitted values
R Square0.719Share of variation explained
Adjusted R Square0.708R Square with a per-feature penalty
Standard error2.320Typical leftover, in target units
Observations30Rows the line was fitted on

Multiple R is the correlation between the actual values and the fitted values. Squaring it gives R Square: 0.8482 = 0.719. Adjusted R Square takes away a little for each feature in the model. This matters because adding a column can never lower R Square on the training data. With only one feature, the adjustment is small: 0.719 falls only to 0.708. The standard error here is not the standard error of a coefficient. It is the typical size of a residual, in the units of the target, so a typical week's sales are missed by about 2.3 units. Observations is 30, the rows the line was fitted on, and the same 30 that gave df = 28.

Framework to remember

Reading the top of the output (5 fit measures, in printed order)

  1. Multiple R (0.848): correlation of actual and fitted values.
  2. R Square (0.719): share of variation explained; equals Multiple R squared.
  3. Adjusted R Square (0.708): R Square with a per-feature penalty; with one feature it barely moves.
  4. Standard error (2.320): typical leftover, in target units (not a coefficient's SE).
  5. Observations (30): rows the line was fitted on.

How to recall it: 0.8482 = 0.719 ties the first two lines together.

Trap

The output has two different "standard errors". The 0.248 in the coefficient table is the noise around the slope. The 2.320 at the top is the typical miss of the whole line, in sales units. Check which block a number comes from before you read it.

Residual plots, and residuals on new weeks

On the exam

R squared gives you one number. The residual plot shows whether the line had the right shape in the first place. You plot each residual against its fitted value and look for a pattern. In the GOOD picture the points are scattered above and below the zero line with no pattern. The misses look like noise, so the model has captured the structure. In the WARNING picture the residuals rise and then fall: a curve remains. A curve means the line is systematically too low in one range and too high in another. Something systematic is left behind, usually because the true relationship bends and a straight line was the wrong shape.

Framework to remember

Residual plots: what the misses say (2 patterns, residuals versus fitted)

PatternVerdictMeaning
No pattern: points scattered evenly around zeroGOODThe misses look like noise; the model took the structure.
A curve remains: residuals rise then fall (or the reverse)WARNINGThe misses still curve; something systematic is left behind. Consider adding curves.

How to recall it: random scatter is good news; any shape is a message the model missed. The mock exam asked exactly this: a curved residual pattern means systematic structure remains unexplained.

Trap

A curved residual plot does not mean "the errors are random", "every point was predicted exactly", or "the relationship is causal". It means only one thing: some systematic structure remains unexplained by the fitted model.

Residuals are also how you judge a forecast on new data. The method is short, and exams like it. For each new week, compute residual = actual − predicted and keep its sign. Square each residual. Add the squares. The total is the total squared prediction error, which is the same idea as SSE, but measured on weeks the model did not see during fitting. A smaller total is better.

Framework to remember

Scoring forecasts on new weeks (5 steps, in order)

  1. Residual with sign: actual − predicted for each new week (+ means the model was too low).
  2. Square: square each residual, so misses cannot cancel.
  3. Add: the sum is the total squared prediction error.
  4. Compare: on the same new weeks, the model with the smaller total wins, whatever the training SSE or R squared said.
  5. State a limit: a few weeks are a small sample; demand or campaigns may change, so further validation is needed.

How to recall it: my own summary, not a course term: "sign, square, sum, compare, caveat".

Worked example (my own illustration): two forecasts on three new weeks

Two models were fitted on earlier weeks. Model M had a training SSE of 40. Model N, a more flexible model, had a training SSE of 6. On three later weeks that neither model saw during fitting, the actual orders were 50, 64 and 58. Model M predicted 54, 60 and 58. Model N predicted 52, 70 and 55.

Model M's residuals: 50 − 54 = −4, 64 − 60 = +4, 58 − 58 = 0. Their sum is 0. Squares: 16 + 16 + 0 = 32.

Model N's residuals: 50 − 52 = −2, 64 − 70 = −6, 58 − 55 = +3. Squares: 4 + 36 + 9 = 49.

On the new weeks Model M wins, 32 against 49, even though Model N had the much smaller training error. Training error measures fit to the data the model was built from; prediction error measures what you care about, the next weeks. A colleague might say Model M "made no error" because its residuals sum to zero. That is wrong. It missed week 1 by 4 orders (too high) and week 2 by 4 orders (too low), and the two misses cancelled in the sum. This is the same reason OLS squares the misses in the first place. Squared errors cannot cancel, so they show the real size of the misses. And with only three weeks, the comparison is evidence, not proof. Other weeks might favour the other model, so the result needs further validation.

Exam tip

The mock exam's case asked exactly this kind of comparison. A full-mark answer has four parts: each residual with its correct sign, the sum of squares written out (for example 102 + (−10)2 + 02 = 200 squared orders), the choice of the model with the smaller total on the same later weeks together with the reason that a smaller training error only shows closer fitting of earlier data, and one explained limit such as "only three validation weeks". If a colleague claims "zero residual sum means no error", explain that an underestimate and an overestimate cancelled.

Trap

A sum of residuals equal to zero does not mean zero error. In fact an OLS line with an intercept always has training residuals that sum to zero, however poor it is.
