The Week 5 notebook shows this: its residual mean is −0.00 minutes, yet the typical miss is 86.7 minutes. To measure error, square the residuals before adding them.

The wiggly line and the tailor's suit

On the exam

If the residual plot shows a curve, the truth probably curves, and the course's advice is: if the truth curves, add curves. Polynomial regression bends the line by adding terms such as x2 and x3, so the fitted shape can follow a bend. Then comes the temptation. On the twenty-week data (weekly ad spend against weekly sales), a straight line runs through the middle of the points, while a wiggly curve hits every one of them. The warning: a flexible enough curve fits history perfectly, and new data badly. The wiggles follow the noise of those twenty weeks, and that noise will not repeat next month.

The picture to remember is a tailor taking a customer's measurements (a photo from Belgrade, 1937). A suit cut to one customer's every bump fits that customer perfectly, and nobody else. An overfit model is that suit: it has learned the quirks of the fitting data as if they were rules. A perfect fit on the fitting data is evidence of nothing. The test is the next customer through the door, meaning new data the model has not seen.

Training fit versus prediction error

On the exam

The course puts the two kinds of error side by side. On the left is the OLS training fit: SST = SSR + SSE and R2 = 1 − SSE/SST, so on the same training rows lower SSE means higher R2. More flexibility can always improve this training fit while it hurts new predictions. On the right is what matters for a forecast, the expected squared prediction error on new data, which splits into three parts:

Total = bias2 + variance + irreducible noise

Bias squared is the error from a missed pattern: the model is too simple, like a straight line through a curved truth. Variance is the error from an unstable fit: the model is so flexible that it changes a lot when the sample changes, so it chases noise. Irreducible noise is the part no model can remove, because the outcome has real randomness in it.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Expected squared prediction error = bias2 + variance + irreducible noise (3 parts)

  • Bias2: missed pattern (model too simple). Falls as complexity grows.
  • Variance: unstable fit (model chases noise). Rises as complexity grows.
  • Noise: unavoidable. Flat, whatever the model.

How to recall it: the total (error on new data) is U-shaped; its lowest point is the sweet spot. Training error keeps falling, so it cannot find the sweet spot.

The schematic in the lecture plots these against model complexity from 1 to 8. As complexity grows, bias squared falls (the model can follow more patterns) and variance rises (the model becomes jumpier). The noise line stays flat. Their sum, the total error on new data, first falls and then rises, giving a U shape with its lowest point, the sweet spot, in the middle (at 4 on the schematic). Left of the sweet spot a model underfits; right of it, it overfits. Training error keeps falling as you move right, which is why it cannot find the sweet spot.

The complexity budget and the penalty dial

On the exam

The course turns this trade-off into business language as the complexity budget: a feature stays only if its evidence pays for it. There is a fit benefit, because extra flexibility may reduce training error. There is a complexity cost, because large coefficients add a penalty to the fit. The penalty dial sets how strongly the fit discourages large coefficients. Turn it to zero and you have plain OLS. Turn it up and the model is pushed toward smaller, simpler coefficients.

How do you set the dial? Choose the strength using validation data: rows held back from fitting. Try several strengths, fit on the training rows, and keep the strength with the lowest error on the validation rows. Do not choose by training error, because training error always prefers the weakest penalty. The course adds: shrinking coefficients and selecting features are different decisions. Making every coefficient smaller is one thing; removing some features entirely is another. The next section shows which method does which.

Framework to remember

The complexity budget in business terms (3 parts, plus 2 rules)

  • The penalty dial: how strongly the fit discourages large coefficients (zero = plain OLS).
  • Fit benefit: extra flexibility may reduce training error.
  • Complexity cost: large coefficients add a penalty to the fit.

Two rules: choose the strength using validation data. Shrinking coefficients and selecting features are different decisions.

OLS, Ridge and LASSO, and what happens as the penalty grows

On the exam

All three methods start from the same SSE, the sum of squared residuals. The penalised methods add a cost for coefficient size. Here p is the number of predictors, bj is slope j, and λ (lambda) ≥ 0 is the penalty strength, the dial from the previous section.

MethodEach method minimisesEffect and use
OLSSSENo shrinkage. Baseline for comparison.
Ridge (L2): squared sizesSSE + λ Σj=1..p bj2Coefficients smaller, usually nonzero. Many correlated predictors.
Least absolute shrinkage and selection operator (LASSO) (L1): absolute sizesSSE + λ Σj=1..p |bj|Can set some coefficients to zero. Fewer predictors.

Ridge charges for the squared size of each slope. Squaring makes large coefficients very expensive, so Ridge pulls all of them toward zero, but it rarely puts any of them exactly at zero. Every feature stays, just smaller, which suits many correlated predictors: where OLS lets echo columns split credit wildly, Ridge calms them together. LASSO charges for the absolute size. Under that penalty the cheapest choice for a weak predictor is often exactly zero, so LASSO can set some coefficients to zero. It shrinks and selects at once, leaving fewer predictors. A zero coefficient removes that predictor's contribution from the fitted equation; it does not prove the feature could never matter in other data.

Core framework: learn this by heart

OLS, Ridge and LASSO: what changes? (3 methods)

MethodPenalty (fit + penalty)EffectUse
OLSNone: SSE onlyNo shrinkageBaseline for comparison
Ridge (L2)Squared sizes: λ Σ bj2Smaller, usually nonzero (shrinks, keeps all)Many correlated predictors
LASSO (L1)Absolute sizes: λ Σ |bj|Can set some exactly to zero (shrinks and selects)Fewer predictors

How to recall it: my own mnemonic, not a course term: L1 = "LASSO Lets coefficients hit zero"; L2 = "Ridge Reduces but keeps". Rules under the table: standardize predictors; the intercept b0 is not penalized; λ = 0 gives back OLS.

Two rules sit under the table. First, standardize predictors (put them on a common scale) before penalising. Remember the units section: a coefficient's size depends on the units, so without standardizing, a feature measured in small units gets a large coefficient and is punished harder for no real reason. Second, the intercept b0 is not penalized; the penalty sums start at j = 1. The intercept is the anchor that sets the level of the line, and shrinking it would only drag predictions toward zero. When λ = 0 both methods become plain OLS; as λ grows the coefficients shrink more.

Worked example: the coefficient-path figure

The lecture shows a short animation of coefficient paths. It uses three standardized, independent features, A, B and C, as an illustration, and shows each method as the penalty strength runs from none to stronger.

FeatureOLS (no penalty)Ridge (L2)LASSO (L1)
A+1.80+0.82+0.60
B−1.00−0.450
C+0.35+0.160

Read across the rows. Ridge has shrunk every coefficient, and all three remain nonzero. B kept its negative sign, just smaller. LASSO has kept only A, at +0.60, and set B and C exactly to zero, so its model has one predictor instead of three. So the answer to the lecture's question, "Which method can set a coefficient exactly to zero?", is LASSO.

Trap

"Ridge deletes correlated predictors" is wrong. Ridge shrinks and keeps everything. "OLS selects a subset" is also wrong, because OLS has no penalty and keeps every feature you give it. Adding more polynomial terms without a penalty does not remove anything either; it only adds flexibility. Only LASSO directly offers coefficients of exactly zero. And the strongest penalty is not automatically best. Pushed too far, LASSO zeroes out useful predictors and validation error rises. Among the settings that meet the team's practical limits (for example a maximum number of predictors), choose the one with the lowest validation error.

The mock exam tested this last rule directly: a team that can maintain at most four predictors compared three LASSO strengths. The weak setting had the lowest validation error (12) but needed six predictors, so it was infeasible. The medium setting used three predictors with error 15, and the strong setting kept only one predictor with error 29. The right choice is medium.

Framework to remember

Choosing a penalty strength under a constraint (4 steps, in order)

  1. Drop the infeasible: remove settings that break the practical limit (for example too many nonzero predictors), even if their error is lowest.
  2. Compare validation error: among the feasible settings, on the same validation rows, pick the lowest.
  3. Explain a zero: a zero coefficient removes that predictor's direct contribution from the fitted equation (it does not prove the feature never matters).
  4. Reject "strongest is best": a stronger penalty can drop useful predictors, and validation error rises.

How to recall it: my own summary, not a course term: feasible first, then lowest validation error.

A complexity budget for your team: three discussion questions

On the exam

The second discussion set applies the complexity budget to a team decision. Here are the three questions, each with a full model answer.

Q1. Your team forecasts next month's livestream sales. Name one feature you would make prove itself before it enters. A good answer names a feature that is likely to echo an existing driver or is costly to collect, for example a likes count that mostly moves with sessions. Then it says what "prove itself" means: the feature must lower the error on validation data, not just raise the training R squared. A penalty such as LASSO can make that test automatic by zeroing out features that do not pay.

Q2. Who should set the budget: the analyst, the manager, or the platform? There is no single printed answer, so argue it. A defensible position is that the budget is a business decision: the manager weighs what complexity costs, such as data collection, maintenance, and the need to explain the model. The analyst then chooses the penalty strength on validation data inside that budget. The platform limits which data exist at all. The weak answer is "the analyst picks whatever gives the highest R squared".

Q3. The straight line has R squared 0.719. Your wiggly curve hits 0.99 on the same weeks. Which do you ship, and why? Neither number settles it, because both are training fit on the same weeks, and 0.99 is exactly what an overfit suit looks like. Compare the two on held-out weeks by their prediction error. Unless the curve wins there, ship the straight line: it is simpler and more stable, and its 0.719 is more likely to survive next month.

The short version and practice

On the exam

The short version

A coefficient is per-unit economics: one more unit of the feature comes with b more units of the target, holding the other features fixed, in units that change with the ruler. It is association, not a lever, and the intercept is an anchor, not a prediction. t = coefficient / standard error is the estimate in noise units. A t in the 2.5 percent tails, a p below α = 0.05, and a 95 percent interval that excludes zero always agree; p printed as 0.000 means below 0.0005. SST = SSR + SSE and R2 = 1 − SSE/SST, so 0.719 means about 72 percent of the swings are explained, and 0.8482 = 0.719. A curved residual plot means structure was left behind. Residuals that sum to zero can hide big misses, so square them before adding. Training fit always improves with flexibility, but error on new data, bias2 + variance + noise, has a sweet spot. Set the penalty on validation data. Ridge shrinks and keeps every predictor, while LASSO can set coefficients exactly to zero.

Practice

P5.1 (Multiple choice) A tea shop regresses daily sales, measured in yuan, on minutes of livestream that day. The coefficient is 36. The analyst refits after reporting sales in thousands of yuan, with minutes unchanged. What is the new coefficient? (a) 36, because the shop did not change. (b) 36,000. (c) 0.036. (d) It cannot be known without new data.

Show the answer

(c). A coefficient inherits the units of the target. Every sales value is divided by 1,000, so the slope is divided by 1,000: 36 / 1,000 = 0.036 thousand yuan per minute, which is still 36 yuan per minute. Option (a) confuses the shop with the number. The shop is unchanged, but the number moves with the unit, like the donut priced in lev and in euro.

P5.2 (Short answer) A small analytics team can maintain at most three predictors in a weekly forecast. It fits LASSO at four penalty strengths on the same training rows and compares error on the same validation rows (lower is better). Very weak: 7 nonzero coefficients, validation error 40. Medium: 3 nonzero, error 44. Strong: 2 nonzero, error 47. Very strong: 0 nonzero, error 90. Which setting should the team choose? Explain what a zero coefficient does, why Ridge would not solve this team's problem, and why "always take the strongest penalty" is a poor rule.

Show the answer

Choose Medium. Very weak has the lowest error (40), but it needs 7 predictors, so it breaks the team's limit of three and is not feasible. Among the feasible settings, Medium (3 predictors, error 44) beats Strong (error 47) and Very strong (error 90). A zero coefficient removes that predictor's contribution from the fitted equation, so the team does not have to collect or maintain it. Ridge shrinks coefficients but usually leaves them nonzero, so it would keep all the predictors and could not bring the model down to three. The strongest penalty is not automatically best: as the penalty grows, LASSO removes useful predictors too, and the validation error climbs to 90 when none are left. Pick the penalty from validation error within the constraint.

P5.3 (Calculation and reading) A pet-food brand fits weekly sales (in thousand yuan) on livestream hours and price, using 26 weeks. Part of the output:

CoefficientStd. errortpLower 95%Upper 95%
Livestream hours1.350.50?0.0130.322.38
Price−0.800.70?0.265−2.250.65

(a) Compute both t values and the degrees of freedom (the model also has an intercept). (b) Which coefficient is significant at α = 0.05? Give two reasons from the table. (c) Read the hours coefficient aloud. (d) The manager wants to drop price. What do you say?

Show the answer

(a) t = coefficient / standard error. Hours: 1.35 / 0.50 = 2.70. Price: −0.80 / 0.70 = −1.14. Three coefficients are fitted (intercept, hours, price), so df = n − k = 26 − 3 = 23. (b) Hours is significant: p = 0.013 is below 0.05, and its interval 0.32 to 2.38 excludes zero. Price is not: p = 0.265 is above 0.05, and its interval −2.25 to 0.65 includes zero. (c) "One more livestream hour in a week comes with 1.35 thousand yuan (1,350 yuan) more weekly sales, holding price fixed." This is an association, not proof that adding hours will cause the extra sales. (d) Not automatically. A large p means these 26 weeks cannot separate price's effect from zero. It does not prove the effect is zero, and the interval still allows a sizeable negative effect. Price may also overlap with another feature. Keep or drop it based on the business question and on validation error, not on the p-value alone.

P5.4 (Calculation) A study app compares weekly study minutes in two cities. City A: n = 100, mean 52, standard deviation 10. City B: n = 100, mean 55, standard deviation 10. H0: the two means are equal. Compute t with every step. The software reports a two-sided p = 0.035. What is your decision at α = 0.05, and what exactly does 0.035 mean?

Show the answer

The gap is 55 − 52 = 3. City A's noise share: 102 / 100 = 100 / 100 = 1. City B's noise share is also 1. Sum: 1 + 1 = 2. Standard error of the gap: √2 = 1.414. t = 3 / 1.414 = 2.12. Since p = 0.035 is below 0.05, reject H0: the 3-minute gap is statistically significant. The value 0.035 is the probability of a gap at least this extreme, in either direction, if the two cities truly had equal means. It is not the probability that H0 is true, and it does not say whether 3 minutes is a gap worth acting on.

P5.5 (Case reading) A noodle delivery brand compares two forecasting models fitted on the same earlier weeks. Model S, a straight line, had training SSE 90 and R squared 0.70. Model W, a high-degree polynomial, had training SSE 4 and R squared 0.99. On four later weeks that neither model used, the actual orders were 200, 240, 220 and 260. S predicted 210, 230, 225 and 255. W predicted 190, 270, 200 and 280. (a) Compute each model's residuals and total squared prediction error. (b) A colleague says S made no error because its residuals sum to zero. Respond. (c) Which model goes forward, and why do the training numbers not reverse the choice? Give one limit of this comparison.

Show the answer

(a) S residuals (actual − predicted): −10, +10, −5, +5. Squares: 100 + 100 + 25 + 25 = 250. W residuals: +10, −30, +20, −20. Squares: 100 + 900 + 400 + 400 = 1,800. (b) S's residuals do sum to zero (−10 + 10 − 5 + 5 = 0), but only because overestimates and underestimates cancel. S still missed every one of the four weeks, by 10, 10, 5 and 5 orders. Squaring stops the cancelling and shows a total squared error of 250, not 0. (c) S goes forward: 250 is far below 1,800 on the same new weeks. W's tiny training SSE and 0.99 R squared show that it fitted the earlier weeks closely, like a suit cut to one body. That is overfitting, and it predicts new weeks worse. One limit: four weeks is a small sample, and a change in demand or a promotion could favour a different model, so the choice needs further validation on more new weeks.

Week 6 Clustering

Grouping without labels

Background: not examined directly, but needed to understand the exam material

In supervised learning (Weeks 4 and 5) every row has a target, and you can score a model with an accuracy or an error. In unsupervised learning, viewers arrive with behavior only, and there is no right answer to score against. So success is managerial: did the grouping change a decision? This explains why the exam material keeps giving the business, not the geometry, the last word.

The goal of clustering is "similar inside, different across": small distances within a group, large distances between groups. A cluster is a group whose members resemble each other more than they resemble the rest. The course warns that what "similar" means is a business choice: viewers can be similar on appetite (watch minutes, sessions), on engagement (likes, saves, questions) or on live habits. Choose the features, and you have already shaped the answer. The course clusters on what people do, not on demographics, which return only at the end to help name the segments. The test for whether clustering is worth doing: name the action per segment before you run the algorithm. The Airbnb case shows why: two hosts both available about 30 percent of the year, one on weekends and one in a summer block, need different advice, and Airbnb used K-means to find 8 availability groups.

Distance and standardization

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

To say two viewers are "close", you need a ruler. The default ruler is Euclidean distance: for each feature, take the gap between the two viewers, square it, add all the squared gaps, and take the square root. It is the straight-line distance you know from a map, extended to many features.

d(x, x′) = √[ Σj (xj − x′j)2 ]

x and x′ are two viewers, j runs over the features, and (xj − x′j) is their gap on feature j. Every feature adds its squared gap to the sum, so the scale of a feature decides how much its gap costs.

The course calls this the unit trap. Two viewers differ by 300 watch minutes, 10 sessions and 2 questions. The squared gaps are 90,000, 100 and 4, so the minutes gap outvotes the sessions gap 900 to 1 and the distance is really just watch time. That is an accident of units, not a business decision. The fix is to standardize every feature first, turning each value into a z-score: how many standard deviations it sits above or below its column's mean.

z = (x − μ) / σ

x is the viewer's raw value, μ is the feature's mean across all viewers, and σ is its standard deviation, the typical spread. After this, every feature has mean 0 and spread 1, so a gap of one σ costs the same in every feature. Each feature gets an equal vote.

The lecture's worked number: a feature with mean 600 and spread 300, and a viewer at 1,200. Then z = (1,200 − 600) / 300 = 2, two typical spreads above average. The mock exam (A11) asked the same thing the other way round: a viewer at 180 minutes, mean 240, standard deviation 120, gives z = (180 − 240) / 120 = −0.5, half a standard deviation below the mean. Read the sign: negative means below average. There is also an honesty note: outliers stretch σ, so with heavy tails you take logs or clip the extremes first.

Other rulers exist (Manhattan, cosine for Week 7's word counts, Jaccard for yes/no features), but Euclidean on standardized features is the default.

Trap

Standardizing does not make every feature equally important to the business. It only stops the units from choosing for you. Which features to include is still a business choice, made before the algorithm runs.

The K-means algorithm

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

K-means (MacQueen 1967) is the workhorse of the week. You must first choose k, the number of groups. Then the algorithm runs four steps. Initialize: place k centroids somewhere, at random or with a smart method. Assign: every viewer joins the cluster of its nearest centroid. Update: every centroid moves to the mean of its members. Repeat: assign and update again until nothing moves.

Framework to remember

The K-means pipeline (6 steps, in order)

  1. Standardize: turn every feature into a z-score, z = (x − μ) / σ, so units do not choose for you.
  2. Choose k and initialize: place k starting centroids (random, or far apart with k-means++).
  3. Assign: every viewer joins its nearest centroid. Nobody is left out, and no data changes.
  4. Update: every centroid moves to the mean of its members.
  5. Repeat: assign and update until nothing moves (WCSS never rises, so it stops).
  6. Restart and keep the lowest WCSS: run from many starts; keep the tightest run, "best among these starts", not "the best possible".

How to recall it: scale, start, assign, update, repeat, restart. The mock exam tested step 1 (A11), step 3 (A12) and step 6 (B4).

A centroid is the average viewer of its group, feature by feature: a representative, not a person. In the course's illustration, one centroid has 610 watch minutes, 22 sessions, 68% completion and 3 live classes. No real viewer may look exactly like it, and that is fine: "it speaks for the group, it does not live in it". Profiling means translating its numbers into a plain-language kind of viewer; "the weekend binge learner" is a centroid wearing a name.

K-means has one score it tries to make small, the within-cluster sum of squares (WCSS). For every viewer, take the squared distance to the centroid of its own group, then add these up over all viewers in all groups. It measures how tightly the groups hold together: how much, in total, the members "miss" their own centre. Lower is tighter, but tighter is not automatically better. Software often reports it as inertia.

WCSS = Σgroups Σmembers x d(x, c)2

c is the centroid of the group that x belongs to, and d is the distance. The inner sum adds up one group's squared misses; the outer sum adds up all groups.

Both steps of the loop can only lower WCSS or leave it unchanged. Moving a viewer to a nearer centroid shrinks its squared miss, and the mean is the point that makes a group's squared misses as small as possible. Because WCSS can never rise and cannot go below zero, the loop must stop, usually within a handful of rounds.

Worked example: one round by hand

Five viewers sit on one standardized axis, and k = 2. The starting centroids are at −0.5 and +0.5. Reading the lecture's figure, the viewers sit at roughly −2.1, −1.8, −0.2, 1.9 and 2.2 (the figure prints only the centroids, so these positions are my own reading of the dots).

Assign: the three left viewers are nearer to −0.5. For example, the viewer at −0.2 is 0.3 from −0.5 and 0.7 from +0.5. The two right viewers are nearer to +0.5. Update: the left centroid moves to the mean of its members, (−2.1 − 1.8 − 0.2) / 3 = −4.1 / 3 ≈ −1.37, and the right one to (1.9 + 2.2) / 2 = 2.05. These are the lecture's numbers, −1.37 and +2.05. In the next round nobody switches sides, so the run has converged.

The mock exam (A12) tested only the assign step. Centroids at −2 and 4, a viewer at 1.5. The distances are |1.5 − (−2)| = 3.5 and |1.5 − 4| = 2.5, so the viewer joins the centroid at 4. Notice what assignment does not do: it never changes the viewer's data, and it never leaves a viewer unassigned.

Local solutions, restarts and K-means' opinions

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

"It must stop" is a trap. The loop is guaranteed to reach a minimum, not the minimum. An unlucky start, such as two of three starting centroids landing inside the same natural group, can freeze a stable but second-best grouping. Same data, different seed, different segments: the lecture calls this "a property, not a bug". There are two "seatbelts": k-means++, which places the starting centroids far apart on purpose, and restarts, which run the whole algorithm many times from different starts and keep the run with the lowest WCSS. The rule: never present a single run as "the" segmentation.

Worked example: the restart rule (mock exam B4)

Same data, features, scaling and k. Three starts converge with WCSS 210, 168 and 189. You keep the run with 168, because it is the tightest grouping by the K-means objective. But be careful with words. Each run converged, which only means its loop stopped changing. Different starts can stop at different local solutions, so 168 is the best among these starts, not proof of the best possible grouping. Another start could still find something lower. And a low WCSS on its own says nothing about commercial usefulness.

K-means also has four "opinions": it draws round-ish territories, prefers groups of similar size (so a huge group can swallow a niche), needs k before it starts, and drafts every viewer into some group. Know the opinions, and you know when to doubt the map.

A bad k shows on the map too: k too small fuses unlike viewers into one blob, and k too large splits real kinds into slivers; only profiling exposes either. And inertia always falls as k grows (145 at k = 2, 24 at k = 3 and 12 at k = 5 in the course illustration). At k = n every viewer is its own group with inertia 0. Perfectly tight, perfectly useless, so the objective itself cannot pick k.

How many groups is a judgment

On the exam

Picture the same points circled three ways: as two groups, as four, and as six. Every version is defensible. The lecture's message is that the definition of a cluster is not precise, so the count of clusters is "a judgment, not a lookup". There is no hidden "true k" that a formula can read off. The useful question is which grouping pays, and the rest of the topic prices it: two geometric tools (the elbow and the silhouette), a stability check, and the business tests.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Choosing k: the evidence you combine (4 kinds of evidence, in this order)

  1. Elbow: read WCSS by k as marginal returns; stop at the knee (tightness).
  2. Silhouette by k: the average silhouette peaks where membership is most confident (separation).
  3. Stability: re-run on resamples and seeds; the same segments must reappear.
  4. Business tests: Kotler's four tests (measurable, substantial, reachable, actionable) and the floor (k ≥ number of planned campaigns).

How to recall it: the curves nominate, stability cross-examines, the business decides. When geometry and business disagree, geometry loses.

So never write "the algorithm found 4 segments". It found 4 because someone told it k = 4; a good answer says who chose k and on what evidence.

The elbow, read as marginal returns

On the exam

The elbow plot puts k on the horizontal axis (1 to 8 in the course illustration) and WCSS on the vertical axis. Remember what WCSS measures: the total squared distance from every viewer to the centre of its own group. When k grows, every viewer has more centres to choose from, so WCSS always falls. That is why you cannot simply pick the k with the lowest WCSS; you would always pick the largest k.

Instead, read the curve as marginal returns. Ask: what does one more segment buy? Look at the drop in WCSS from k to k + 1. At first the drops are big, because a new centroid rescues many badly placed viewers. Later the drops become small, because a new centroid only splits an existing group that was already tight. The point where the curve bends from steep to flat is the knee or elbow. On the illustrative curve, the knee is at k = 3: big drops from 1 to 2 and from 2 to 3, then only small drops after. The rule: stop when the next segment's geometric gain no longer justifies its keep. "Keep" is a business word: every extra segment costs a campaign, a manager, content and reporting.

Framework to remember

Reading the elbow as marginal returns (3 steps, in order)

  1. Ask what one more segment buys: compute the drop in WCSS per added k, not the levels.
  2. Find the knee: where big drops turn into small ones.
  3. Stop: when the next segment's geometric gain no longer justifies its keep (campaign, manager, content, reporting).

How to recall it: no automatic rule. "We stopped at the knee" beats pretending a formula chose it.

The bottom line is important: there is no automatic rule. Saying "we stopped at the knee" is honest, because it admits a judgment. Pretending a formula chose k is not. Real curves are often smooth, and two people may see the knee in different places.

Worked example: reading the SkillCraft elbow (from the course notebook)

The notebook fits K-means on the eight standardized SkillCraft columns for k = 1 to 8. The WCSS values are 32,000, 21,470, 17,067, 14,552, 13,094, 11,869, 11,073 and 10,624. Compute the drop per added k: 10,530 (1 to 2), 4,403 (2 to 3), 2,515 (3 to 4), 1,458 (4 to 5), 1,225 (5 to 6), 796 (6 to 7) and 449 (7 to 8). The first segment split buys a lot. Each later one buys less. The notebook's reading is that returns shrink after k = 4, but you can see the curve has no sharp corner. This is exactly why there is no automatic rule, and why you want a second piece of evidence: the silhouette.

Exam tip

If a question shows a WCSS table, compute the drops, not just the levels. Say where the drops become small, call that the knee, and add that the choice is a judgment that the business tests must still confirm.

The silhouette score

On the exam

The elbow judges the whole map at once. The silhouette (Rousseeuw 1987) is "membership evidence, per viewer". It asks: how similar is this viewer to its own group, compared with the nearest other group? You compute two numbers for each viewer. The first, a, is the average distance from the viewer to the other members of its own group. The second, b, is the average distance from the viewer to the members of the nearest other group, the neighbouring crowd it would most likely be confused with.

s = (b − a) / max(a, b)

The top, b − a, is how much closer the viewer is to its own group than to the next one. The bottom, max(a, b), is the larger of the two distances; dividing by it keeps s between −1 and +1, so viewers can be compared.

The score runs from −1 to +1, with three readings. Near +1, a is much smaller than b: the viewer is well inside its own group, a confident member. Near 0, a and b are about equal: the viewer sits on the boundary between two groups, a coin flip. Negative, b is smaller than a: the viewer is closer to another group than to its own, so it was probably filed in the wrong group. If you average s over all viewers, you get one number per candidate k, which you can compare against the elbow.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Silhouette s = (b − a) / max(a, b) and its three zones (3 zones, from −1 to +1)

  • Negative: b < a, closer to another group; probably filed in the wrong group.
  • Near 0: a ≈ b, on the boundary between two groups; a coin flip.
  • Near +1: a much smaller than b, well inside its own group; confident membership.

How to recall it: a = own group, b = nearest other group; divide by the larger. Lecture numbers: a = 2.0, b = 5.0 gives 0.6; a = 4.8, b = 5.0 gives 0.04. Average over all viewers for one number per candidate k.

Worked example: one viewer's silhouette

One viewer has a = 2.0 (average distance to its own group) and b = 5.0 (average distance to the nearest other group). The larger of the two is 5.0. So s = (5.0 − 2.0) / 5.0 = 3.0 / 5.0 = 0.6. The lecture's reading: comfortably placed, well inside its own group.

Now move its own group farther away, so a = 4.8 while b stays 5.0. The larger is still 5.0. So s = (5.0 − 4.8) / 5.0 = 0.2 / 5.0 = 0.04. That is almost zero: the viewer is on the boundary. It is barely closer to its own group than to the next one.

Finally, if b is smaller than a, the top of the fraction is negative, so s is negative. The viewer sits closer to another group. As my own illustration with the same viewer: if a = 4.8 and b = 2.0, the larger is now a, and s = (2.0 − 4.8) / 4.8 = −2.8 / 4.8 ≈ −0.58.

Trap

The bottom of the fraction is the larger of a and b, not always b. When b is smaller than a, you divide by a. Students who always divide by b get a number below −1 in the negative case, which is impossible. If your silhouette falls outside −1 to +1, check the denominator.

Reading a silhouette plot, and silhouette by k

On the exam

A silhouette plot draws one horizontal bar per viewer, with length equal to that viewer's silhouette. The bars are sorted from longest to shortest inside each group, the groups are stacked, and a dashed vertical line marks the average. In the course's illustrative plot, the first of three groups has long bars, the second falls to short bars at the bottom, and one small bar at the very bottom sticks out to the left of zero.

There are three things to look for. Wide, even bars mean a healthy, confident grouping. Bars dipping below the group's average are boundary members, worth a second look. Bars below zero are viewers filed in the wrong group. The plot shows you what a single average can hide: one group can be fuzzy while the others are clean, and the average alone will not tell you which. In the SkillCraft notebook, for instance, the four clusters at k = 4 have average silhouettes of 0.412 (casual scrollers), 0.223 (live regulars), 0.164 (systematic learners) and 0.331 (credential seekers), around an overall average of 0.3115. The systematic learners hold together much less firmly than the largest group.

Framework to remember

Reading a silhouette plot (3 things to look for)

  • Wide, even bars: a healthy, confident grouping.
  • Bars dipping below the group's average: boundary members, worth a second look.
  • Bars below zero: viewers filed in the wrong group.

How to recall it: one bar per viewer, sorted within each group. The plot shows which group is fuzzy, which a single average hides.

The silhouette then becomes a tool for choosing k. For each candidate k (2 to 8 in the course illustration), fit K-means, compute every viewer's silhouette and take the average. That gives one number per candidate. You cannot compute it for k = 1, because there is no "other group" to measure b. The average silhouette peaks where membership is most confident; in the illustration the peak is at k = 4.

The second message is "two evidences, one answer". The elbow and the silhouette measure different things. The elbow measures tightness: how much WCSS one more group buys. The silhouette measures separation: how much closer viewers are to their own group than to the next. When the structure in the data is real, the two point the same way. When they disagree, the structure is weak and your judgment, and the business, must carry more of the decision.

Framework to remember

Silhouette by k: one number per candidate (3 steps, in order)

  1. Fit: run K-means for each candidate k (from 2; k = 1 has no "other group").
  2. Average: compute every viewer's silhouette and average it, one number per k.
  3. Compare with the elbow: the peak is the most confident k; "two evidences, one answer".

How to recall it: elbow = tightness, silhouette = separation. When the structure is real, both point the same way.

Worked example: SkillCraft's silhouette by k (from the course notebook)

The average silhouettes are 0.3078 at k = 2, 0.3101 at k = 3, 0.3115 at k = 4, then 0.2212 at k = 5, 0.2263 at k = 6, 0.2201 at k = 7 and 0.1860 at k = 8. The peak is k = 4 at 0.3115. Read it honestly: k = 2, 3 and 4 are very close, and the big fall comes after 4. The elbow's drops also shrink after k = 4. So both evidences allow four, and the silhouette is what nominates it. A silhouette around 0.31 is a moderate, not a strong, separation, which is one more reason to check the business tests next.

Kotler's four tests and the floor

On the exam

The curves are geometry. A segment must also work as a business object. The course uses four tests from Kotler and Keller (2016). Measurable: you can count it and describe it, with a size and a profile. Substantial: it is big enough to serve profitably. This is about profit, not about a percentage on its own. Reachable: your channels actually touch it. A group you cannot target with a post, a message or a live room is a fact, not a segment. Actionable: someone will run a campaign for it. The rule is strict: a segment earns a budget only if it passes all four tests. (Some marketing textbooks add a fifth test, "differentiable", meaning segments respond differently to different actions, but the course uses these four.)

Core framework: learn this by heart

Kotler's four tests and the floor (4 tests, all must pass, plus 1 floor rule)

  • Measurable: you can count it and describe it.
  • Substantial: big enough to serve profitably.
  • Reachable: your channels actually touch it.
  • Actionable: someone will run a campaign for it.
  • The floor: three campaigns planned means k is at least 3, whatever the curves say.

How to recall it: my own mnemonic, not a course term: "MSRA", count it, afford it, reach it, act on it. A segment earns a budget only if it passes all four.

Then comes the floor: if the business has three campaigns planned, k is at least 3, whatever the curves say. The plan sets a lower limit on the number of groups, because with fewer groups at least one campaign would have no audience of its own. So the business can overrule the curves from below. The lab discussion makes the same point: the silhouette peaks at k = 4, but a team needs three segments for a campaign. A sensible answer reports both: what the geometry nominates, what the plan needs, and why the final k was chosen.

Exam tip

When a case question asks you to choose k, use the course's order: the curves nominate, stability cross-examines, the business decides. Then apply the four tests by name to the segment in question.

When geometry and business disagree

On the exam

Picture a conflict. A loyalty file has a silhouette that peaks at k = 2: one clean split, "active versus gone". Geometrically it is beautiful. But the actionable structure sits at k = 5, including a 4 percent premium niche. With k = 2, that niche is silently dissolved into the mass: its members are drafted into the big "active" group, and nobody ever sees them as a separate kind of customer. The lecture's image is a boutique: "a boutique, not a sliver: small, premium, and worth keeping".

Framework to remember

When geometry and business disagree, geometry loses (2 x 1 comparison and a rule)

Geometry says k = 2Business says k = 5
Silhouette peaks at k = 2: one clean split, "active versus gone". Geometrically beautiful.The actionable structure sits at k = 5, including a 4% premium niche. k = 2 silently dissolves the niche into the mass.

How to recall it: the silhouette is a geometric criterion. When they disagree, geometry loses. A boutique, not a sliver.

The verdict: the silhouette is a geometric criterion, and when geometry and business disagree, geometry loses. The silhouette rewards groups that are far apart. It knows nothing about margins, prices or campaigns. A small group that is very valuable may be close to a bigger group in behavior space, so splitting it off lowers the average silhouette, but it still pays. Recall also K-means' opinions: it prefers groups of similar size, so a huge group can swallow a niche. Choosing a larger k is one way to protect the niche.

Trap

"The silhouette is highest at k = 2, so k = 2 is correct" is the wrong reasoning. The silhouette nominates; it does not decide. A higher score does not mean more business value.

Stability, and the 2 percent sliver

On the exam

Ask: would the same k win again tomorrow? A k that wins once may be "one seed's opinion", because K-means depends on its random start and on the exact rows in the file. There are three steps. Re-run the whole pipeline on resamples of the data and with different seeds. Compare: do the same segments reappear, with similar sizes and profiles, not just the same count? Report stability next to the silhouette, because both are evidence. A stable structure is safe to plan campaigns against; an unstable one is "a story about the seed". Note the difference from restarts: restarts pick the best of several runs on the same data; stability asks whether the answer survives when you change the data and the seed.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Stability check (3 steps, in order)

  1. Re-run: the whole pipeline on resamples and with different seeds.
  2. Compare: do the same segments reappear, in sizes and profiles, not just the count?
  3. Report: stability next to the silhouette; both are evidence.

How to recall it: a k that wins once may be one seed's opinion. Stable = safe to plan campaigns against; unstable = a story about the seed.

Then a discussion: a 2 percent sliver with extreme watch time and high conversion. Serve it, or fold it into the mass? You are asked to argue substantial versus actionable. For folding it in: 2 percent of the file may be too few people to pay for its own content and campaign, so it may fail "substantial"; it may also be unstable, a group that appears with one seed and vanishes with another. For serving it: its behavior is extreme and it converts well, so a campaign for it is easy to design and likely to work, which makes it actionable; and the loyalty-file example already showed that a small premium niche can be worth keeping.

The third question asks what single number would settle it. The course does not print an answer, so here is the reasoning that follows from the tests. "Substantial" means big enough to serve profitably, so the deciding number is the profit from serving the sliver: roughly its size times its conversion times the value per conversion, minus the cost of running a separate campaign for it. If that is positive, the sliver passes, however small its share. You should also confirm it reappears across resamples before you spend on it.

Case: Etsy and a buyer's lasting interests

On the exam

The Etsy case (Bhadauria et al. 2024) shows clustering used on products, not people. Etsy is a marketplace with a huge number of listings. K-means groups products using image and text features, meaning the product photos and descriptions. The result is about 1,000 interest groups of similar listings. The flow has four boxes: product features (images and descriptions), interest groups (1,000 groups of similar listings), buyer history (purchases over time), and recommendations (items linked to lasting interests). The product groups feed a broader recommendation system.

Framework to remember

The Etsy pipeline (4 stages, in order)

  1. Product features: images and descriptions of each listing.
  2. Interest groups: K-means makes 1,000 groups of similar listings.
  3. Buyer history: purchases over time, read through those groups.
  4. Recommendations: items linked to the buyer's lasting interests.

How to recall it: follow the products first, then the buyer. Product groups feed a broader recommendation system.

Follow the products first, then the buyer. First the clustering turns millions of individual listings into a manageable set of interests. Then a buyer's purchase history is read in terms of those groups: a buyer who bought several items from the same interest group over time shows a lasting interest, not a one-time need. The case question is how this helps Etsy stay relevant after a first purchase. The answer: a single purchase alone suggests "more of the same item", which is often useless (you do not need a second identical gift). The interest group lets Etsy recommend different listings from the same lasting interest, so the buyer has reasons to come back.

The case also echoes the point that "similar" is a business choice: Etsy chose to define "similar" by how products look and how they are described, and a recommendation job needs many fine groups, very different from four campaign segments.

SkillCraft: four crowds and naming the segments

On the exam

SkillCraft is the creator studio from Week 5: career-skill short videos, free live open classes and a paid certificate track. The data is the same 4,000 learners over the same 30 days. Week 5 asked what a learner will do. This week asks what kinds of learners exist. There is no target column: the segments come out of the eight behavior columns alone (sessions, watch minutes, completion, live attendance, likes, saves, questions and shares), standardized first. The two Week 5 targets (next month's watch time and conversion) stay out of the clustering. The deliverable is four segments, one sentence each, named so a campaign manager can aim at them. "The naming is the work, not decoration."

SegmentnShareWatch minCompletionLiveSavesQuestions
Casual scrollers1,89047.2%11532.60.81.00.6
Live regulars1,02425.6%24346.95.31.61.3
Systematic learners71617.9%37761.62.05.82.9
Credential seekers3709.2%31878.25.513.97.9

This table, "four crowds in one audience", gives each segment's size and average behavior in plain units. Read it row by row against the other rows. The lecture highlights the extremes: completion 78.2 against 32.6, and saves 13.9 against 1.0. The bottom line: "half the file barely watches" (47.2 percent are casual scrollers, with 115 watch minutes and 32.6 completion), and "one learner in eleven is the certificate track's home crowd" (370 of 4,000 is 9.25 percent, about 1 in 11).

Now see how each name comes from the columns. Casual scrollers are the lowest on every column: they scroll past, watch little and finish about a third. Live regulars stand out on one thing, live attendance of 5.3, while their saves and questions stay low: they show up in the live room. Systematic learners have the highest watch time, 377 minutes, and solid completion of 61.6, but low live attendance (2.0): they work through the videos on their own. Credential seekers are the extremes on completion (78.2), saves (13.9) and questions (7.9), with high live attendance too (5.5). The lecture's phrase for them: "finishes everything, saves everything, asks every session", which is how someone preparing for a certificate behaves.

Why is this work a deliverable? A cluster is machine output: a row of z-scores, labelled "cluster 2". "Cluster 2" tells a campaign manager nothing. A segment is a decision-ready sentence giving size, value and behavior in plain units. The rule: the name comes from the extremes, the columns that sit far from the whole-file average, high or low. "Near the average" names nothing, because everyone shares it. Then apply the line-up test: could a stranger pick this segment out of a line-up? "Credential seekers" passes, because the stranger can look for people who finish and save everything. "Cluster 2" fails, because the label carries no behavior.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Naming is a deliverable (4 ideas: cluster, extremes, segment, line-up test)

  1. A cluster: a row of z-scores, machine output. "Cluster 2" tells a campaign manager nothing.
  2. The name comes from the extremes: "finishes everything, saves everything, asks every session" names the credential seekers; "near the average" names nothing.
  3. A segment: a decision-ready sentence giving size, value, behavior, in plain units.
  4. The line-up test: could a stranger pick this segment out of a line-up? "Credential seekers" passes; "cluster 2" fails.

How to recall it: cluster → extremes → sentence → line-up. The naming is the work, not decoration.

Exam tip

To name a segment in a new table, find the one or two columns where the row is far from the others, high or low, quote the two numbers, and build the name from that behavior. Then check the name with the line-up test.

One campaign for one segment, and the honesty check

On the exam

Now aim one campaign at one segment. The segment: credential seekers, 370 learners, 9.2 percent of the file, with completion 78.2, saves 13.9 and questions 7.9. The campaign: the certificate bundle, mentor feedback plus the exam seat sold as one track, aimed only at this segment. The fit is natural: these learners already behave like certificate candidates. The metric: segment conversion, not file conversion. The baseline is the 84.1 percent of this segment already converting.

Framework to remember

One campaign for one segment (3 parts, in order)

  1. The segment: who, with numbers. Credential seekers: 370 learners, 9.2%, completion 78.2, saves 13.9, questions 7.9.
  2. The campaign: one action aimed only at them. The certificate bundle: mentor feedback plus the exam seat, sold as one track.
  3. The metric with its baseline: segment conversion, not file conversion; baseline 84.1% already converting.

How to recall it: who, what, measured against what. Read the baseline before you read the campaign.

Why does the baseline matter so much? The case brief gives the file-wide conversion rate as 22.9 percent (917 of 4,000). If the campaign reports, say, 86 percent conversion among credential seekers, comparing it with 22.9 percent would make it look like a huge success. But these learners were converting at 84.1 percent anyway. The honest comparison is against their own baseline, and the gain is only the difference above 84.1. Read the baseline before you read the campaign.

The honesty check, "the segment is not the whole answer", bridges back to Week 5. There you measured how much of next month's watch time a model explains with the coefficient of determination (R squared), the share of the variation in the outcome that the model accounts for, from 0 to 1. Now compare two models. Using only the segment ID (which of the four groups a learner is in), R squared is 0.4372. Using all eight behaviors, which is the full linear model from Week 5, R squared is 0.6807. The gap is 0.6807 − 0.4372 ≈ 0.244.

Read it in both directions. The segments are real: four labels alone explain about 44 percent of the variation in next month's watch time, which is a lot for one column with four values (by my own arithmetic, about 64 percent of what the full model explains, since 0.4372 / 0.6807 ≈ 0.64). But they are not the whole answer: 0.244 of explainable variation lives inside the segments, in the behavior columns that the four names ignore. The reason: the columns that separate the segments (saves, questions and live attendance, according to the case brief) are not the columns that drive watch time (sessions, watch minutes and completion). The working rule: use the segment as the language and the behaviors as the forecast.

Trap

"The four segments are the audience; the rest is noise" is wrong. A segment is a compression of the data. It makes the audience speakable, but it throws away detail that still predicts behavior. A good name should not oversell its signal.

Which segment gets the budget

On the exam

Next, add what each segment did after the snapshot: the share who converted to the paid track. Conversion played no part in forming the clusters; it is the payoff, not the input.

SegmentnConverts nowThe call
Casual scrollers1,89010.2%Top of funnel: free content, no paid push
Live regulars1,02415.4%Nurture through the live room they attend
Systematic learners71635.8%The budget: engaged, with room to move
Credential seekers37084.1%Already sold: low-touch service

Framework to remember

Which segment gets the budget: the four calls (4 segments, 4 calls)

  • Casual scrollers (10.2%): top of funnel; free content, no paid push.
  • Live regulars (15.4%): nurture through the live room they attend.
  • Systematic learners (35.8%): THE BUDGET; engaged, with room to move.
  • Credential seekers (84.1%): already sold; low-touch service.

How to recall it: the budget lands where engagement is high and conversion has room to move. Value times headroom.

The case brief tells you to read the conversion column as "headroom in reverse": the higher the current rate, the less room is left to gain. Go through it with the engagement numbers from the four-crowds table. Credential seekers already convert at 84.1 percent, so only 15.9 points are left to win; they are already sold, and they get low-touch service, not a paid push. Casual scrollers have the most headroom on paper, 89.8 points, but their engagement is weak (115 minutes, 32.6 completion), so they are expensive to move; they get free content at the top of the funnel and no budget. Live regulars convert at 15.4 percent and already come to the live room, so you nurture them there, where you can reach them cheaply. Systematic learners combine the deepest watch time (377 minutes) and solid completion with a conversion of 35.8 percent, which leaves 64.2 points of room. Engagement is high and conversion has room to move, so the budget lands there.

The brief's summary is "segment value times segment headroom", the same logic Week 5 used per learner, now applied per segment. A quick check, my own arithmetic: 1,890 × 0.102 + 1,024 × 0.154 + 716 × 0.358 + 370 × 0.841 ≈ 193 + 158 + 256 + 311 = 918, which matches the brief's 917 converters once you allow for rounding of the rates. So the credential seekers, only 9.2 percent of learners, already supply about a third of all conversions.

You may notice a tension. The one-campaign example aims the certificate bundle at the credential seekers, while the budget table puts the budget on the systematic learners. They answer different questions. The one-campaign example teaches how to aim one campaign and judge it against the right baseline. The budget table asks where the next yuan of budget earns the most. A closing discussion turns this into a debate: the credential seekers already convert at 84.1 percent, so argue for or against spending on them. For: they are the revenue core, and a small, well-fitted offer protects it. Against: most of them would buy anyway, so the marginal yuan earns more among the systematic learners. The case brief says both sides are defensible if you state the metric and the baseline. The same discussion also asks you to pick a segment, name it, profile it in one sentence, design one action, and state the metric and its baseline. That is the pattern a Part C answer should follow.

The short version

Clustering groups people with no target, so success means a better decision, not a score. Standardize first with z = (x − mean) / SD, or the feature with the biggest units decides every distance. K-means assigns each point to its nearest centroid and moves each centroid to its group's mean until nothing changes; it always stops, but only at a local solution, so use restarts and keep the lowest within-cluster sum of squares (WCSS), calling it the best among these starts, not the best possible. The number of groups is a judgment. The elbow reads WCSS as marginal returns and you stop at the knee, with no automatic rule. The silhouette s = (b − a) / max(a, b) compares a viewer's average distance to its own group (a) with that to the nearest other group (b): near +1 is confident, near 0 is on the boundary, negative is misfiled; 2.0 and 5.0 give 0.6, and 4.8 and 5.0 give 0.04. When the elbow and the average silhouette agree, the structure is real; still check stability across resamples and seeds. Then the business decides: Kotler's four tests (measurable, substantial, reachable, actionable), the floor (three campaigns means k is at least 3), and when geometry and business disagree, geometry loses. A cluster becomes a segment when you name it from its extremes and it passes the line-up test. Judge a campaign against the segment's own baseline, use segments as the language and behaviors as the forecast (R squared 0.4372 against 0.6807), and put the budget where engagement is high and conversion has room to move, which at SkillCraft means the systematic learners.

Practice

P6.1 (Multiple choice) A fictional podcast app standardizes "episodes finished per month" before clustering. The mean is 8 and the standard deviation is 2.5. A listener finished 3 episodes. What is the listener's z-score, and what does it mean? (a) +2.0, two standard deviations above the mean. (b) −2.0, two standard deviations below the mean. (c) −5.0, five episodes below the mean. (d) −0.5, half a standard deviation below the mean.

Show the answer

(b). z = (3 − 8) / 2.5 = −5 / 2.5 = −2.0. The listener sits two typical spreads below the average. Option (c) is the raw gap in episodes, not a z-score; the point of standardizing is to count in units of spread so that features with different units can be compared fairly in a distance.

P6.2 (Multiple choice) In a fitted K-means on a fictional shopping app, a customer's average distance to the other members of its own cluster is a = 3.6, and its average distance to the members of the nearest other cluster is b = 3.0. What is its silhouette, and what does it say? (a) +0.17, well inside its cluster. (b) −0.20, so the customer must be an outlier with no group. (c) −0.17, the customer sits closer to another cluster and was probably filed in the wrong one. (d) 0.83, a confident member.

Show the answer

(c). The larger of a and b is 3.6, so s = (3.0 − 3.6) / 3.6 = −0.6 / 3.6 ≈ −0.17. Because b is smaller than a, the score is negative: the customer is closer to the neighbouring cluster than to its own. Option (b) divides by b instead of max(a, b), and K-means never leaves a point without a group.

P6.3 (Calculation and short answer) A fictional language-learning app clusters its standardized user data. WCSS by k is: k = 1: 1,000; k = 2: 600; k = 3: 380; k = 4: 340; k = 5: 315. (i) Compute the drop in WCSS for each added k and say where the knee is. (ii) The marketing team has four campaigns planned, one per segment. What does Kotler's floor say, and what should the analyst report?

Show the answer

(i) The drops are 400 (1 to 2), 220 (2 to 3), 40 (3 to 4) and 25 (4 to 5). The first two added segments buy a lot; after k = 3 each new segment buys very little, so the knee is at k = 3. This is a judgment, since no formula fixes the knee. (ii) The floor says that with four campaigns planned, k must be at least 4, whatever the curve says. The analyst should report both pieces of evidence: the elbow nominates 3, but the plan needs 4. The fourth segment buys only 40 units of WCSS, so it is geometrically weak; before funding it, the analyst should check that it passes the four tests (measurable, substantial, reachable, actionable), look at the average silhouette at k = 3 and k = 4, and confirm that the four segments reappear across resamples and seeds. If they do not, the team may need to merge two campaigns.

P6.4 (Short answer) An analyst runs K-means once on a fictional gaming platform's player file with k = 5, gets a WCSS of 2,410, and says: "The algorithm converged, so this is the best segmentation." Give two reasons why this claim is too strong, in three to five sentences.

Show the answer

Convergence only means the assign and update loop stopped changing; it reaches a local solution that depends on the starting centroids, so another start could settle on a different grouping with a lower WCSS. The analyst should use restarts (and k-means++ starts) and keep the run with the lowest WCSS, and even then call it the best among those starts, not the best possible grouping. Second, WCSS measures only how tightly members sit around their centroids; it always falls as k grows, so it cannot say whether five is the right number of groups or whether the groups are useful. That needs the elbow and silhouette evidence, a stability check across resamples, and the business tests.

P6.5 (Case reading) A fictional fitness-video channel clustered 5,000 viewers on standardized behavior and named four segments. Each row gives size, average weekly workout minutes, and the share that currently buys the paid coaching plan.

SegmentnWorkout minBuys plan now
Browsers2,600204%
Challenge joiners1,2009012%
Steady trainers90016030%
Competition preppers30021088%

(i) Which segment should get the marketing budget, and why? (ii) A campaign aimed at the competition preppers reports 90 percent purchase. The manager compares it with the channel-wide rate and calls it a big success. Compute the channel-wide rate and explain what the right comparison is.

Show the answer

(i) The steady trainers. They are highly engaged (160 minutes a week) and only 30 percent buy now, so 70 points of conversion remain to win. Competition preppers already buy at 88 percent, leaving only 12 points: they are already sold and need low-touch service. Browsers have the most headroom on paper but weak engagement (20 minutes), so they are expensive to move; free content suits them better. The budget goes where engagement is high and conversion has room to move. (ii) Buyers: 2,600 × 0.04 + 1,200 × 0.12 + 900 × 0.30 + 300 × 0.88 = 104 + 144 + 270 + 264 = 782, so the channel-wide rate is 782 / 5,000 = 15.64 percent. Comparing 90 percent with 15.64 percent is misleading, because these viewers were buying at 88 percent anyway. The right metric is segment conversion against the segment's own baseline: the campaign moved the rate from 88 to 90 percent, a gain of 2 points.

Week 7 Text Analysis and Natural Language Processing (NLP)

Why models need numbers: four representations

On the exam

A classifier or a regression cannot read a sentence. It needs a table of numbers, one row per document and one column per feature. So the first job is to choose a representation: a rule that turns each review into a row of numbers. The course names four, and each keeps different information. Counts record how often each word occurs. TF-IDF (term frequency-inverse document frequency) weights those counts by how rare each word is across all reviews. N-grams count short runs of consecutive words, so they keep some local word order. Embeddings place words in a space of learned coordinates, so words used in similar ways sit close together and the numbers carry some meaning.

Core framework: learn this by heart

From words to numbers: four text representations (4 items, from simplest to richest)

  1. Counts (bag of words): word occurrence frequencies.
  2. TF-IDF: weight terms by rarity.
  3. N-grams: preserve local word order.
  4. Embeddings: represent semantic relationships.

How to recall it: each step keeps more information (frequency, then rarity, then order, then meaning) and usually costs more. Choose the one that supports the analytical question.

The course's message is that no representation is best in general. They keep different information and need different resources, so you choose the one that supports your analytical question. If you only need to know which product features people mention most, counts may be enough. If you need to find reviews that say the same thing in different words, you need something like embeddings. Keep this "fit the representation to the question" idea in mind, because the comparison table below returns to it.

Normalization: tokens, lemmas and stems

On the exam

Before you can count words, you must decide what a "word" is. Normalization is the cleaning step that does this, and the course names three operations.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Normalization: tokenize, lemmatize, stem (3 operations; tokenizing comes first)

  1. Tokenize: split into words and symbols ('The battery died fast!' → [ The · battery · died · fast · ! ]). Each piece is a token.
  2. Lemmatize: map to the dictionary form, the lemma (was, were, is → be; batteries → battery).
  3. Stem: remove word endings using rules (automation, automatic → automat); simple, but the result need not be a dictionary word.

How to recall it: split, then reduce. Lemma = real dictionary word; stem = whatever is left after chopping. Why managers care: removing 'not' turns 'not good' into 'good', so inspect the tokenizer's input and output.

Tokenize means split the text into words and symbols. "The battery died fast!" becomes [ The · battery · died · fast · ! ]. Each piece is a token, the unit a tokenizer produces; it may be a word, a punctuation mark or part of a word. Tokenizing only splits. It does not change any word.

Lemmatize means replace each word with its dictionary form, called the lemma. "was", "were" and "is" all become "be"; "batteries" becomes "battery". The point is that different grammatical forms of the same word should count as one feature. The mock exam asks exactly this: a pipeline that maps "was", "were" and "is" to "be" is doing lemmatization, not tokenization.

Stem means cut word endings off using simple rules. "automation" and "automatic" both become "automat". Stemming is computationally simple, but the result need not be a real dictionary word. That is the difference you must remember: a lemma is always a dictionary word found by knowing the grammar; a stem is whatever is left after chopping, and "automat" is not an English word.

Why should a manager care? Because preprocessing decides what information the model can see. The lecture's warning is that removing "not" can reverse sentiment: "not good" becomes "good". Preprocessing choices affect the information available to the model. The practical requirement is to inspect the tokenizer's input and output, even when you use prepared code. You do not need to write the code, but you must look at what it produced.

Worked example: one review through normalization

Take the illustrative review "The batteries barely lasted a day!". The raw text is a single string. After tokenizing (and lowercasing) it becomes seven tokens: [ the · batteries · barely · lasted · a · day · ! ]. After lemmatizing it becomes [ the · battery · barely · last · a · day · ! ].

Two things changed. "batteries" became "battery" and "lasted" became "last". Now this review and another review that says "the battery lasts" share the same features, so a count of battery complaints will find both. Nothing else changed: "barely" and "day" were already in dictionary form.

One more point. If the review had said "did not last", you must keep "not", because it carries the negative meaning. A cleaning step that drops "not" would turn a complaint into a neutral or positive statement about lasting.

OperationWhat it doesCourse exampleResult is a dictionary word?
TokenizeSplits text into words and symbols'The battery died fast!' → [The, battery, died, fast, !]Words unchanged
LemmatizeMaps each form to its dictionary formwas, were, is → be; batteries → batteryYes
StemRemoves endings by rulesautomation, automatic → automatNot necessarily

English and Chinese tokenization, and the dictionary

On the exam

English and Chinese mark word boundaries differently, so they need different tokenizers. In English, spaces and punctuation already show most boundaries. The course uses the Natural Language Toolkit (nltk), which splits "The battery died fast, honestly!" into [ The · battery · died · fast · , · honestly · ! ]. Punctuation and contractions still need rules, but spaces do most of the work.

Chinese usually has no spaces between words. The course uses jieba, which decides where one word ends and the next begins by using a dictionary and probabilities: it matches the sentence against dictionary entries and uses word frequencies to choose the most likely split. The course example is 续航很差，戴久了耳朵疼 ("Battery life is poor, and my ears hurt after wearing them for a long time"), which jieba splits into [续航 · 很差 · ， · 戴久 · 了 · 耳朵 · 疼]: battery life, very poor, punctuation, wear for a long time, a grammatical particle, ears, hurt. The tokenization methods differ, but after this step both languages go through the same counting and weighting steps. The lab runs "two tokenizers, one pipeline" on the same file.

Because jieba depends on its dictionary, a word that is not in the dictionary gets cut into the wrong pieces. This matters most for brand terms, which companies invent all the time.

Worked example: a coined brand term, before and after

The illustrative sentence is 声阔小金标的调音偏暖 ("Soundcore's 'Little Gold Label' has a warm tuning"). The product term is 小金标, an invented name that the default dictionary does not know.

Before the fix, jieba gives [ 声阔 · 小金 · 标的 · 调音 · 偏暖 ]. The product name is broken in two, and its last character 标 is glued to the particle 的 to form 标的, which is a different word. So two errors happen at once: the product is never counted as itself, and 的 joins the wrong token.

After adding 小金标 to the dictionary, jieba gives [ 声阔 · 小金标 · 的 · 调音 · 偏暖 ]. The product term stays one token, and 的 is back in its normal role of marking possession. The lesson: add brand terms to the dictionary before counting.

Framework to remember

Two dictionary lessons for Chinese text (2 lessons)

  1. Lesson 1, update the dictionary: add brand terms before counting, or 小金标 splits and 的 joins the wrong token.
  2. Lesson 2, language-specific stop lists: the English list has 179 words; Chinese lists are separate (的, 了, 在); 很 (very) depends on the task.

How to recall it: fix what counts as a word (lesson 1), then fix which words you throw away (lesson 2).

The course then turns this into a maintenance duty. Common words such as 入耳式 (in-ear) and 降噪 (noise cancellation) are already built in and are split correctly without any change. What you must add are coined brand terms, product codes and campaign hashtags, one entry per line. Every new campaign brings new terms, so an outdated dictionary causes segmentation errors, and those errors flow into every count, frequency and sentiment score built later. The lecture's picture of sixty years of Xinhua Dictionary editions makes the same point: a dictionary is something someone has to keep updating. In the SoundPeak lab, the same problem appears with 充电盒 (charging case), which the default dictionary splits into 充电 and 盒真 until one line is added.

Trap

"Tokenization is a technicality; the model sees the same text either way" is wrong. The tokenizer decides what the words are before any model runs. If a product name is split, its mentions scatter across wrong tokens and are undercounted, and every later number inherits the error.

Stop words: what to remove and what to keep

On the exam

Stop words are tokens you exclude for a task, usually very common grammatical words that carry little topic information. In English these include "the", "a", "and" and "of"; the standard English list contains 179 words. Chinese uses a separate list, with particles such as 的 (linking particle), 了 (aspect particle) and 在 (at/in). Stop lists are language-specific files: you cannot apply the English list to Chinese text.

Some words are a judgement call. 很 (very) can be noise for counting topics but useful for sentiment, so its relevance depends on the analytical task. The big exception is negation. The course says to retain negation terms: "not", "never", "no", 不 (not) and 没 (did not / have not). If you remove 不, then 不好 (not good) becomes 好 (good), and the meaning is reversed. The principle is to inspect the stop list before applying it and adapt it to your question.

Framework to remember

Stop words: remove vs retain (2 groups and 1 principle)

  • Terms removed: grammatical words such as the, a, and, of, 的, 了, 在 (the English list has 179).
  • Negation terms retained: not, never, no, 不, 没; removing them turns 不好 (not good) into 好 (good).
  • The principle: inspect the stop list before applying it, and adapt it to the analytical question.

How to recall it: throw away grammar, never throw away "not".

Even if you keep "not", a plain word count only records that a negator appeared somewhere in the review, not that it sat next to "good". Keeping negation is necessary, but word order is still lost, which is the problem the next section shows.

Bag of words: documents as numbers

On the exam

The simplest representation is the bag of words: each document becomes a row of token counts, with one column per word in the vocabulary and no information about word order. Imagine pouring the words of a review into a bag and shaking it. You know which words are inside and how many times, but not their order.

Reviewsoundgreatbatteryterrible
d1 · 'Great sound, terrible battery'1111
d2 · 'Terrible sound, great battery'1111

Read the table carefully. The first customer likes the sound and hates the battery. The second customer is the opposite. A product manager would send these two reviews to different teams. Yet their rows are identical, because both contain the same four words once each. This is the limitation: different meanings, identical rows, because word order is not preserved.

Why use counts at all, then? Because once text is numeric, you can feed it into the classifiers of Week 4, the regressions of Week 5 and the clustering methods of Week 6. Counts are cheap and easy to inspect. The course also points forward: n-grams preserve local order, and contextual embeddings can represent broader context. Those are the fixes for the problem in this table.

Exam tip

If a question shows two reviews with the same words in a different order and asks why a model treats them alike, the answer is that bag of words (and TF-IDF, which is built on it) ignores word order. The fix is n-grams for short phrases or embeddings for wider context.

TF-IDF: weighting words by frequency and rarity

On the exam

Raw counts reward words that appear everywhere. In earbud reviews, "earbuds" appears in nearly every review, so it tells you almost nothing about what makes one review different. TF-IDF fixes this by multiplying two factors. Term frequency (TF) is how often the word appears in this review. Inverse document frequency (IDF) measures how rare the word is across the whole collection, including this review. The lecture's summary is "TF-IDF = frequent here × rare elsewhere = informative".

Framework to remember

TF-IDF = frequent here × rare elsewhere (2 factors, multiplied)

  • TF (term frequency): how often the word appears in this review (repetition within a review).
  • IDF (inverse document frequency): how rare the word is across the whole collection; IDF = ln(N / df).

How to recall it: "earbuds" is everywhere, so low IDF; "firmware" is rare, so high IDF, but read its context before calling it a problem. A word in every document has IDF = ln(1) = 0.

TF-IDF(t, d) = TF(t, d) × IDF(t),   with   IDF(t) = ln(N / df(t))

TF(t, d) is the number of times term t appears in document d. N is the number of documents in the collection. df(t), the document frequency, is the number of documents that contain t at least once. ln is the natural logarithm. A word in every document has df = N, so IDF = ln(1) = 0 and its weight is zero however often it appears. A word in only one document gets the largest IDF, ln(N).

The course gives two words to compare. "earbuds", if it appears in nearly every review, has low IDF and distinguishes reviews weakly. "firmware" is rarer, so it has higher IDF. But there is a caution: interpret the context of a rare word before concluding that it indicates a business problem. A high weight tells you a word is distinctive, not that it is bad news. TF-IDF is a common baseline for document retrieval and text analysis.

Worked example: TF-IDF on three reviews

The collection has N = 3 reviews:

d1: "Great sound, perfect fit, lovely case". d2: "Terrible battery, battery died every hour". d3: "Great price, lovely case, perfect fit".

Step 1: document frequency. "fit" is in d1 and d3, so df = 2. "great" is in d1 and d3, so df = 2. "battery" is only in d2, so df = 1. "sound" is only in d1, so df = 1. "died" is only in d2, so df = 1.

Step 2: IDF with the natural log. For words in 2 of 3 reviews: ln(3/2) = ln(1.5) = 0.405, which rounds to 0.41. For words in 1 of 3 reviews: ln(3/1) = ln(3) = 1.099, which rounds to 1.10.

Step 3: weights in d2. "fit" and "great" do not appear in d2, so TF = 0 and their weight in d2 is 0 × 0.41 = 0. "battery" appears twice in d2, so its weight is 2 × 1.10 = 2.20. "died" appears once, so its weight is 1 × 1.10 = 1.10.

Step 4: the "your turn" cell. "sound" appears once in d1 and its IDF is ln(3) ≈ 1.10, so its weight in d1 is 1 × 1.10 = 1.10. Every other word in d1 ("great", "perfect", "fit", "lovely", "case") also appears in d3, so each has weight 1 × 0.41 = 0.41. "sound" is the highest weight in d1.

Business reading. In d2, "battery" has the highest weight, and reading the original review confirms that the battery is the subject of the complaint. In d1, the word that sets the review apart is "sound", because the rest of d1 repeats d3.

WordDocuments with termIDF = ln(3/df)Count in reviewWeight
fit2 of 30.410 in d20 in d2
great2 of 30.410 in d20 in d2
battery1 of 31.102 in d22 × 1.10 = 2.20 in d2
died1 of 31.101 in d21 × 1.10 = 1.10 in d2
sound1 of 31.101 in d11 × 1.10 = 1.10 in d1

The lesson is to "interpret both factors". "great" is common across reviews, so its IDF of 0.41 is lower than battery's 1.10; being said often everywhere does not make a word informative. "died" is rare but appears only once, so it is informative but modest at 1.10. "battery" wins in d2 because it has both: it is rare across reviews and repeated within this one. TF measures repetition within a review; IDF reflects rarity across reviews. They contribute jointly, and even one occurrence can rank highly when a term is rare.

Trap

A high TF-IDF weight does not mean "a problem". It means "distinctive in this document". "firmware" could be distinctive because people praise a firmware update. Always read the reviews behind a high-weight word before you name it a complaint.

Exam tip
