Use the natural log, ln, not log base 10. ln(3) = 1.10 and ln(1.5) = 0.41; with log base 10 you would get 0.48 and 0.18, which do not match the course's numbers. If a word appears in every document, its IDF is ln(1) = 0, so its weight is zero.

N-grams: keeping local word order

On the exam

An n-gram is a sequence of n consecutive words (tokens) that keeps their local order. A unigram is one word, such as 'not'. A bigram is two words, such as 'not good'. A trigram is three words, such as 'not good value'. If you add bigrams as features, "not good" becomes its own column, separate from "good", and the bag of words no longer confuses the two. In the bag-of-words example, the bigrams "great sound" and "terrible battery" would finally separate d1 from d2.

Framework to remember

N-grams: unigram, bigram, trigram (3 lengths, in increasing order)

  1. Unigram (n = 1): 'not'.
  2. Bigram (n = 2): 'not good'.
  3. Trigram (n = 3): 'not good value'.

How to recall it: longer n keeps more local order but gives a larger, sparser vocabulary; select the length for the task.

The course illustrates context with letters: among all letters an "h" might appear about 4% of the time, but right after a "t" about 20% of the time (both numbers illustrative). The preceding letter changes the probability of the next one, and words work the same way. N-grams record this local context.

The cost is size. Each extra word of length creates many more possible combinations, so longer n-grams give larger, sparser vocabularies: more columns, and most of them are zero for most reviews. Choose the length for the task; bigrams are often enough to capture negation. A closing note: a Transformer uses a mechanism called attention to represent context, instead of a fixed table of n-gram counts, and language prediction is one application of it. You only need to know that Transformers handle context more flexibly than n-grams.

Embeddings and cosine similarity

On the exam

An embedding is a vector representation that can encode patterns of word usage. Each word gets a list of coordinates, and training adjusts these coordinates using the contexts in which words appear. Words used in similar contexts end up close together. The number of dimensions depends on the model.

The famous illustration is king − man + woman ≈ queen. In the picture, the arrow from "man" to "king" is about the same as the arrow from "woman" to "queen": similar offsets encode a relation. Start at "king", subtract the "man" direction, add the "woman" direction, and you land near "queen". The lecture labels this "an analogy, not a rule". The relation is approximate, it depends on the training data, and the positions in the picture are only a sketch (schematic). For business use, remember that review search still needs the original text and task-specific validation.

Once texts are vectors, you need a way to say how close two of them are. The course uses cosine similarity, the cosine of the angle between two nonzero vectors.

cos θ = (a · b) / (‖a‖ ‖b‖)

a and b are the two vectors (for example two reviews). a · b is the dot product: multiply matching coordinates and add them up. ‖a‖ and ‖b‖ are the lengths of the vectors. Dividing by the lengths removes the effect of size, so only direction matters. A value of 1 means the same direction, 0 means perpendicular directions (unrelated), and −1 means opposite directions.

Framework to remember

Reading cosine similarity (3 reference values, from 1 down to −1)

  1. 1: same direction (most similar).
  2. 0: perpendicular directions (unrelated).
  3. −1: opposite directions.

How to recall it: smaller angle, larger cosine. Length does not matter; a zero vector has no direction, so its cosine is undefined.

Three facts about cosine are easy exam material. First, smaller angle means larger cosine. Second, vector length does not affect cosine: a long review and a short review can point the same way and get a high score. Third, a zero vector has no direction, so its cosine is undefined. This happens, for example, when none of the query's words are in the vocabulary.

Worked example: the battery query

The query is "The battery died within an hour". Against "Power drains faster than advertised", the illustrative cosine is about 0.7. Against "The case scratches easily", it is about 0.4. The first candidate shares no words with the query, yet it ranks higher, because in an embedding space "battery died" and "power drains" point in similar directions. The second shares "The" but is about a different problem.

The reading: a keyword search for "battery" would miss the power-drain review completely. A meaning-based search finds it first. That ranking is the value of embeddings. Remember the values are illustrative and precomputed; you are expected to read them, not calculate them.

Worked example: cosine by hand (my own illustration)

Suppose two reviews have count vectors a = (2, 0, 1) and b = (4, 0, 2) over the words (battery, case, drain). Then a · b = 2×4 + 0×0 + 1×2 = 10. ‖a‖ = √(4 + 0 + 1) = √5, and ‖b‖ = √(16 + 0 + 4) = √20. So cos θ = 10 / (√5 × √20) = 10 / √100 = 10 / 10 = 1. Review b is just review a written twice as long, and cosine says they point the same way. That is what "length does not matter" means. If a third review has the vector (0, 3, 0), its dot product with a is 0, so the cosine is 0: no shared direction.

Choosing a representation, and review search

On the exam

The course brings the four representations together in one table. Learn it as a set of trade-offs, because a multiple-choice question can give you a task and ask which representation fits.

Framework to remember

Choosing a text representation (4 representations × 4 criteria: preserves order, uses context, cost, suitable use)

RepresentationPreserves order?Uses context?CostSuitable uses
Bag of wordsNoNoLowFrequency analysis with a small vocabulary
TF-IDFNoNoLowIdentifying terms that distinguish documents
N-gramsShort spansLocalLowPreserving local phrases such as 'not good'
EmbeddingsModel-dependentDepends on modelComputation + dataMeaning-based search; multilingual models for cross-language tasks

How to recall it: the first three are all cheap; only embeddings cost real computation and data, and only embeddings capture meaning beyond the exact words.

The practical starting points are: use TF-IDF as a baseline, add n-grams when local order matters, and consider suitable pretrained embeddings when you need semantic relationships. In words: start cheap and transparent, and pay for more only when the question needs it.

Framework to remember

Practical starting points (3 steps, in order)

  1. Baseline: use TF-IDF.
  2. Add local order: add n-grams when phrases such as 'not good' matter.
  3. Add meaning: consider suitable pretrained embeddings for semantic relationships.

How to recall it: start cheap and transparent; pay for more only when the question needs it.

A review-search demonstration compares two representations on 2,000 synthetic English reviews. With the query "battery", TF-IDF works well because many reviews share that exact word. With the query "power endurance", no review uses those words, so the TF-IDF query vector is all zeros. Its cosine is undefined and no ranking is shown. The other side of the demo uses hand-assigned word vectors (coordinates chosen by the instructor, with no model trained), and it returns review C002079, "After the 2.1.1 update my Pro drains 50 percent overnight", with a cosine of 1.0000 (rounded). The query "waterproof" is there to show the behaviour with a zero query vector. Real word2vec (optional background) learns word vectors from neighbouring words in a corpus, while this demo uses manual coordinates, so the matches follow the assigned mapping, not learned relations.

The discussion question is: does high cosine similarity establish the same complaint? The answer is no. A high cosine says two vectors point in a similar direction, which means they use similar or related words. It does not prove the same meaning. "The battery lasts for hours" and "The battery dies after two hours" share almost all their words but say opposite things. In the demo, the cosine of 1.0 comes from coordinates a person chose. You still need to read the original reviews and validate on your task.

Trap

High cosine is similarity of wording or of vector direction, not proof of the same complaint, the same sentiment or the same cause. And a query with no known words gives a zero vector, so the cosine is undefined, not zero.

Topic models and latent Dirichlet allocation (LDA)

On the exam

Counting words tells you which words are common. A topic model goes one step further and asks what the reviews are about, by finding groups of words that tend to appear together. The words are observed; the topics are hidden and must be estimated.

The starting point is a documents × words count table. Four invented reviews are R1 "Comfortable fit, but the battery fades", R2 "Light on my ears; charging takes hours", R3 "Battery lasts hours after each charge" and R4 "Light fit, easy on my ears". Over six words they give this table:

Reviewfitearslightbatterychargehours
R1100100
R2011011
R3000111
R4111000

Here D = 4 reviews and V = 6 words, and you choose K = 2 topics. K is chosen by the analyst, not found by the model. A fitted model estimates two tables: topic-word probabilities, with K × V = 2 × 6 numbers, and review mixtures, with D × K = 4 × 2 numbers. You can already guess the pattern: fit, ears and light go together, and battery, charge and hours go together.

The model the course uses is latent Dirichlet allocation (LDA). "Latent" means hidden. LDA is built on two mixtures. First, a review is a mixture of topics: the review "Comfortable, but the battery fades" might be 60% Comfort and 40% Battery. One review can contain both topics, which is more realistic than forcing each review into one group as clustering does. Second, each topic assigns probabilities to words: the Comfort topic gives high probability to fit, ears and light; the Battery life topic gives high probability to battery, charge and hours. A word can have nonzero probability under several topics. "battery" has probability 0.06 under Comfort and 0.35 under Battery life.

Framework to remember

LDA: two mixtures (2 tables the model estimates)

  • Review mixture θ (D × K): each review is a mixture of topics, e.g. Comfort 60%, Battery 40%.
  • Topic-word probabilities φ (K × V): each topic assigns probabilities to words, e.g. P(battery | Battery life) = 0.35.

How to recall it: reviews mix topics, topics mix words. D = reviews, V = words, K = topics (chosen by the analyst). Combine them with P(word) = Σ θ × φ.

P(w = v | θd, Φ) = Σk=1K θdk φkv

This is the probability that a word drawn from review d is the word v. d is the review, v is the word, k is the topic. θdk is the share of topic k in review d (the review mixture). φkv is the probability of word v inside topic k (the topic-word probability). You multiply "how much of this topic is in the review" by "how likely this word is in the topic", then add over all topics.

Worked example: the probability of "battery"

The review mixture is Comfort 60% and Battery life 40%, so θ = 0.60 and 0.40. The topic-word probabilities for "battery" are P(battery | Comfort) = 0.06 and P(battery | Battery life) = 0.35.

Comfort contributes 0.60 × 0.06 = 0.036. Battery life contributes 0.40 × 0.35 = 0.140. The total is 0.036 + 0.140 = 0.176, or 17.6%.

The reading: a randomly chosen word from this review has a 17.6% chance of being "battery". Most of that chance comes from the Battery life topic, even though that topic is the smaller share of the review, because "battery" is much more likely inside that topic. All values are illustrative.

Read the output before naming a topic

The model does not name topics. It only gives numbered word distributions, and a person adds the label. The table below shows the illustrative word probabilities for the two candidate topics. Each topic is a probability distribution over the same six words, so each column sums to 1.

WordCandidate label: ComfortCandidate label: Battery life
fit0.320.05
ears0.280.05
light0.220.05
battery0.060.35
charge0.060.25
hours0.060.25
Sum1.001.00

Check the sums: 0.32 + 0.28 + 0.22 + 0.06 + 0.06 + 0.06 = 1.00, and 0.05 + 0.05 + 0.05 + 0.35 + 0.25 + 0.25 = 1.00. The first topic is dominated by fit, ears and light, so "Comfort" is a fair candidate label; the second by battery, charge and hours, so "Battery life" fits. The process is observed counts, then approximate inference, then topic distributions, and the label comes only after you inspect the top words and the original reviews.

Framework to remember

Read the output before naming a topic (4 steps, in order)

  1. Observed counts: the documents × words table.
  2. Approximate inference: the model estimates the hidden topics.
  3. Topic distributions: each topic is a probability distribution over the same words (each sums to 1).
  4. Human label: assign a name only after inspecting the top words and the original reviews.

How to recall it: the model gives numbers, a person gives names.

Evaluate topics before choosing an action

Two settings shape the result. K specifies the number of topics. α (alpha) controls prior expectations about review mixtures, roughly how many topics you expect one review to mix. Different K values, different preprocessing and even repeated fits of the same model can give different topics, so compare them. The course also warns that lower perplexity need not produce clearer topics. Perplexity is a statistical fit score where lower means the model predicts the words better, but a better fit score can still give topics that humans find harder to interpret.

The biggest trap is that one topic can hold different meanings. A "battery" topic can contain praise ("The battery lasts for hours. Excellent."), which is a product strength; a complaint ("The battery dies after two hours."), which is a report to investigate; and a question ("How many hours should the battery last?"), which is an information need. All three use battery and hours, so the topic model puts them together. A topic tells you what people talk about, not how they feel or what they want. Inspect the original comments and their sentiment before choosing an action.

Framework to remember

Same topic, three meanings (3 items)

MeaningExample in the battery topicWhat it is for the business
Praise"The battery lasts for hours. Excellent."A product strength
Complaint"The battery dies after two hours."A report to investigate
Question"How many hours should the battery last?"An information need

How to recall it: a topic says what people talk about, not how they feel or what they want. Check sentiment and read the comments before acting.

Trap

"The battery topic is large, so we have a battery problem" does not follow. A topic groups words, not attitudes. Praise, complaints and questions can share one topic. You need sentiment, and a look at the reviews, before you act.

Sentiment analysis at scale

On the exam

The second half of the week asks how customers feel. The business task is to estimate the sentiment expressed in each review, every day. The input is the review text plus a star rating where available. The output can be one of three forms: a class (positive, negative or neutral), a sentiment score, or sentiment per aspect. An aspect is an entity, attribute or component that the text evaluates, such as sound, battery or case.

Framework to remember

The business task: sentiment analysis at scale (3 parts: goal, input, output)

  • Goal: estimate the sentiment expressed in each review, every day.
  • Input: review text and an associated star rating, where available.
  • Output: positive / negative / neutral; or a sentiment score; or sentiment per aspect.

How to recall it: what you want, what you feed in, what comes out. Aspect-level output supports action.

The level of analysis matters. Document-level sentiment gives one label for the whole review. Sentence-level sentiment gives one per sentence. Aspect-level sentiment gives one per feature. The course example: 'Great sound, terrible battery' becomes sound + and battery −. A manager can act on that, because it points to the battery.

Worked example: three analyses of one review

The illustrative review has 3 stars out of 5: 'Sound is amazing and the case feels premium. Battery died within a week.'

A document-level analysis labels it 'mixed'. That is an honest overall summary, but the specific issue remains unclear: a manager does not know what to fix.

An aspect-level analysis gives sound +, case + and battery −. Now the action is clear: investigate the battery problem.

A rating check reads the text alongside the 3-star rating. Here they roughly agree (a middle rating for a mixed review), but in general you should review cases where the text and the rating disagree, because those are where errors and sarcasm hide.

The point: document-level sentiment summarizes the review; aspect-level sentiment identifies specific issues.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Three analyses of one review (3 levels: document, aspect, rating check)

Analysis levelResult for the 3-star reviewManagement action
Document-level'mixed': an overall summarySpecific issue remains unclear
Aspect-levelsound + · case + · battery −Investigate the battery problem
Rating checkRead the text alongside the 3-star ratingReview disagreements between text and rating

How to recall it: document summarizes, aspect identifies, rating check catches disagreements.

Exam tip

The mock exam's Case 2 tests exactly this. Given comments such as "the interviews are engaging, but the search results are useless", list one positive aspect and one negative aspect per comment, recommend one specific change tied to a named feature (for example, improve search relevance), and explain that a whole-comment "mixed" label merges praise for one feature with dissatisfaction about another, so it does not say what to fix. "Improve the app" is not a specific action.

The course compares three methods: lexicon scoring, supervised learning, and language-model analysis. The next three sections take them in turn.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Three sentiment-analysis methods (3 methods)

  1. Lexicon scoring: a fixed word list; score = positive hits − negative hits.
  2. Supervised learning: Week 4 classification with TF-IDF features and labels.
  3. LLM zero-shot (language-model analysis): ask a large language model for sentiment, aspects and a reason, with no labeled examples.

How to recall it: a word list, a trained model, a prompted model; from cheapest and most inspectable to most flexible.

Method 1: lexicon scoring, and its failures

On the exam

A lexicon is a word list with sentiment scores or categories. Lexicon scoring uses a fixed list and adds up the hits.

score = positive hits − negative hits

Count the words in the review that are on the positive list and the words on the negative list, and subtract. A score above zero is classed positive, below zero negative, and zero neutral (the rule the course notebook uses).

A well-known lexicon is the Valence Aware Dictionary and sEntiment Reasoner (VADER), which adds rules for English social text. The lab uses its own English word list, and for Chinese it uses SnowNLP, which returns a score between 0 and 1 (0.5 or more reads as positive). The advantages are fast scoring, low computational cost, and an inspectable word list: you can open the list and see exactly why a review got its score. There are three limitations. Slang: 'sick fit!' may be scored as negative, because "sick" is on the negative list even though here it is praise. Negation: 'not bad at all' needs a negation rule, otherwise "bad" counts as a negative hit. Domain-specific terms may be absent from a general list. The potential use is rapid sentiment summaries for standard language, subject to validation.

Framework to remember

Method 1, lexicon scoring: advantages, limitations, potential use (3 advantages, 3 limitations)

  • Advantages: fast scoring; low computational cost; an inspectable word list.
  • Limitation 1, slang: 'sick fit!' may be scored as negative.
  • Limitation 2, negation: 'not bad at all' requires a negation rule.
  • Limitation 3, domain terms: domain-specific words may be absent from the list.
  • Potential use: rapid sentiment summaries for standard language, subject to validation.

Worked example: the lexicon, word by word

Row C000301 of the course dataset, rated 1 star out of 5, says: 'Amazing battery, I charge them twice a day.'

Go word by word. "amazing" is a positive word: +1. "battery" is not in the list: 0. "charge" is not in the list: 0. No other word matches. The total is +1, so the review is classified POSITIVE.

That is wrong. Charging earbuds twice a day means the battery is bad, and the 1-star rating agrees. The positive word is used sarcastically. Word-level scoring cannot see sarcasm, because the meaning lives in the situation the sentence describes, not in any single word.

The course then tests the challenge cases on its own data. The 4,000 synthetic reviews include 15 examples of each of three challenge families, and not all are misclassified. Negation: 'Not bad at all for the price' needs a negation rule; the notebook's English scorer flips a hit when a negator appears within two places before it, so it handles this example correctly. Sarcasm: 'Amazing battery, I charge them twice a day', where positive wording expresses dissatisfaction. Mixed language: 这个bass真的顶 ("This bass is really excellent"), where 顶 is positive slang and the English word "bass" sits inside a Chinese sentence; SnowNLP classifies the full example correctly.

Framework to remember

Lexicon challenge cases (3 families, 15 reviews each in the course dataset)

  • Negation: 'Not bad at all for the price' needs a negation rule.
  • Sarcasm: 'Amazing battery, I charge them twice a day.' Positive wording expresses dissatisfaction.
  • Mixed language: 这个bass真的顶 (This bass is really excellent); 顶 is positive slang.

How to recall it: my own memory aid, not a course term: "not", "not really meant", "not one language". Not all of these cases are misclassified, so test them rather than assume.

The overall result on Chinese: SnowNLP matches the provided labels on 243 of 355 Chinese reviews. Check it: 243 / 355 = 0.6845, or 68.45%. So about one in three Chinese reviews gets the wrong label. The lab reports that the English course lexicon matches on 254 of 319, which is 79.62%.

Before reporting any sentiment figure, the course gives three validation steps. First, inspect 50 randomly selected cases: a human sees sarcasm in seconds. Second, examine positive wording beside a 1-star rating: that disagreement is a red flag, not a compliment. Third, evaluate lexicon rules and classifier thresholds separately before changing either, so you know which change fixed what.

Framework to remember

Validate before reporting sentiment (3 steps, in order)

  1. Inspect 50 random cases: read a random sample of scored reviews.
  2. Examine positive wording beside a 1-star rating: the sarcasm red flag.
  3. Evaluate rules and thresholds separately: test lexicon rules and classifier thresholds one at a time before changing either.

How to recall it: sample, compare with stars, change one thing at a time.

Trap

A sentiment score you have not checked is a number you cannot quote. A lexicon fails in predictable families (negation, sarcasm, slang, mixed language, missing domain words). Measure agreement with labels and read the errors before you report a dashboard figure.

Method 2: supervised learning on text

On the exam

The second method is Week 4's classification, with text features. It has four steps. Labels: you need a target. Star ratings can serve as noisy proxy labels, so validate the target definition. Features: the TF-IDF columns from the first half of the week. Model: logistic regression or a random forest, as in Week 4. Evaluate: on held-out reviews, unchanged from Week 4. Split, train, evaluate: the workflow is the same; only the feature columns are new.

Framework to remember

Method 2, the supervised pipeline for text (4 steps, in order)

  1. Labels: ratings can be noisy proxy labels; validate the target definition.
  2. Features: TF-IDF columns from the first half of the week.
  3. Model: logistic regression or a random forest, as in Week 4.
  4. Evaluate: on held-out reviews, unchanged from Week 4.

How to recall it: L-F-M-E (my own mnemonic, not a course term). Only step 2 is new; the rest is Week 4. Requirements: labeled data and continued evaluation as language changes.

Two cautions are specific to text. Domain vocabulary: a word such as "light" can describe comfort (light on the ears, good) or sound volume (light bass, possibly bad), so interpret learned associations in context. And requirements: you need labeled data, and you need to keep evaluating as language use changes, because new slang and new products shift the words.

Evaluating predictions on the course dataset

The course notebook compares sentiment predictions with the provided labels. The scorer agrees with the provided label on 243 of 355 Chinese rows (0.6845) and 254 of 319 English rows (0.7962). It then gives the Chinese confusion matrix, with rows for the provided label and columns for the prediction.

Provided labelPredicted negativePredicted positiveRow total
Negative8529114
Positive83158241

Read the agreement rate first, then the matrix. The diagonal is 85 + 158 = 243 correct out of 85 + 29 + 83 + 158 = 355. The matrix shows where the errors are: 29 negative reviews were called positive, and 83 positive reviews were called negative. The discussion question is which kinds of language you would inspect among reviews labeled negative but predicted positive. Those 29 are where you expect sarcasm (praise words describing a failure) and negation problems (a negative meaning built from a positive word plus "not"). The notebook also reports that among 48 Chinese rows labeled neutral, SnowNLP gave a score inside the band 0.35 to 0.65 for only 19.

Star ratings versus provided labels

It is tempting to skip labeling and just use stars: four or five stars means positive. The course dataset shows why that is a separate labeling rule, not the same thing.

RatingReviewsProvided sentiment labelReviews
1 star602Positive432
2 stars588Negative242
3 stars355Neutral95
4 stars1,051Total labeled769
5 stars1,404

All 4,000 reviews have a rating (602 + 588 + 355 + 1,051 + 1,404 = 4,000), but only 769 have a provided sentiment label (432 + 242 + 95 = 769). Among the 201 labeled 4-star reviews, 177 are positive and 24 are neutral. So a rule "4 stars = positive" would mislabel 24 of 201 of those reviews, about 12%. The conclusion: use the provided sentiment labels to evaluate text analysis. A rating threshold is a different definition of the target, and the sarcastic C000301 shows that text and rating can also point in different directions.

Reading logistic regression coefficients

Here is how to read a fitted logistic regression when positive sentiment is the target class. Each TF-IDF word column gets a coefficient. A positive coefficient means increasing that feature raises the predicted probability of positive sentiment. A negative coefficient lowers it. A zero coefficient does not change it. As in Week 4, you read each coefficient holding all other features fixed.

Framework to remember

Reading logistic regression coefficients, positive sentiment as target (3 signs)

  • Positive: increasing the feature raises the predicted probability of positive sentiment.
  • Negative: increasing the feature lowers the predicted probability of positive sentiment.
  • Zero: increasing the feature does not change the predicted probability.

How to recall it: the sign is the direction. Always hold all other features fixed, and remember unigrams omit word order ("good" vs "not good").

There is also a text-specific warning: unigram features omit word order. If "good" gets a positive coefficient, that does not tell you what happens in "not good". Read phrases such as "good" and "not good" in real reviews before you interpret a word association. The course reports no fitted numbers here; it is a conceptual explanation. As my own illustration: if "drain" had a negative coefficient, a review mentioning "drain" more would get a lower predicted probability of being positive, other words fixed. That says "drain" is associated with negative reviews in this data. It does not say that removing the word would make customers happier; the coefficient describes the model, not a lever.

Trap

A supervised model learns your labels, including their errors. If ratings are used as labels and some 5-star or 1-star reviews are sarcastic, the model learns the wrong lesson from them. Validate the target definition before training, and do not read a single-word coefficient as if it captured negation.

Method 3: zero-shot analysis with a large language model

On the exam

The third method asks an LLM directly. Zero-shot means you supply no labeled examples and do no task-specific training: you simply ask for sentiment, aspects and a reason. Because the model reads the whole sentence, context may help with sarcasm and slang. The costs are three: cost accumulates over millions of reviews, answers can vary from one run to the next, and the explanations need checking. External services also require approved handling of customer text. One practical workflow is to use an LLM through an application programming interface (API) to explore a sample, then use a validated lower-cost model at scale.

Worked example: LLM analysis of the same sarcastic review

The request sends the review 'Amazing battery, I charge them twice a day.' and asks for fixed output fields: sentiment (pos, neg or mixed), aspects (a sentiment per identified aspect), sarcasm (true or false), and a reason of at most 12 words.

The illustrative response is: sentiment 'neg'; aspects battery 'neg'; sarcasm true; reason 'Praise wording describes charging twice a day.'

Compare: the lexicon scored this same sentence +1, POSITIVE. The LLM reads the situation, not just the word "amazing", and gets it right in this example. The real lesson is about the structure, though: specify the analysis and the output fields, then check the response against the review. A fixed structure makes errors easier to inspect, because you can count, compare and audit fields in a way you cannot with free prose.

Framework to remember

Method 3, LLM zero-shot: the requested output fields (4 fields)

  • sentiment: pos | neg | mixed.
  • aspects: a sentiment per identified aspect (battery: neg).
  • sarcasm: true | false.
  • reason: 12 words max.

How to recall it: specify the analysis and the fields, then check the response against the review. Its three costs: cost grows with volume, answers vary, explanations need checking.

Comparing the three methods and routing reviews

On the exam

The course compares the three methods on five criteria. No column wins every row, which is the main point.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Comparison of three sentiment methods (3 methods × 5 criteria: cost, sarcasm handling, auditability, requirements, potential use)

LexiconSupervisedLLM zero-shot
Cost per reviewLow scoring costLow inference cost at scaleDepends on model and volume
Sarcasm handlingLimited without context rulesDepends on representative labeled examplesMay help; validate on your data
AuditableInspect word list and rulesModel documentation and error analysisRequires records and repeated evaluation
RequirementsA word listLabels and maintenanceModel access
Potential useRapid summaries of standard textStable tasks in a labeled domainTheme and aspect exploration

How to recall it: combined workflow: an LLM explores themes and aspects; a validated lower-cost model scores reviews at scale.

The lexicon is cheapest and easiest to audit, but weakest on sarcasm. The supervised model is cheap once trained and learns your domain's words, but needs labels and upkeep. The LLM may handle sarcasm and aspects best, but its cost depends on volume and its answers can vary. The combined workflow: an LLM explores themes and aspects, and a validated lower-cost model scores reviews at scale.

A discussion task makes this concrete. Thousands of reviews arrive after each product release, in two languages. Which reviews would you assign to a lexicon, a supervised model or an LLM? The task is to state one explicit decision rule and to evaluate it on both cost and auditability. The course then gives a practical decision guide for routing. For each situation it names a method to test and what to check before use.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Routing sentiment work: a practical decision guide (3 situations × method × check)

SituationMethod to testCheck before use
Clean, standard languageLexiconNegation and domain words
Stable task with labeled reviewsSupervised modelHeld-out errors and label quality
Aspects, sarcasm or mixed languageLLM-assisted analysisOutput consistency and cost

How to recall it: clean text → word list; labeled stable task → trained model; hard language → LLM. Then assess cost per thousand reviews, auditability and maintenance as language changes.

The guide also asks you to assess cost per thousand reviews, auditability, and maintenance as language changes. A good routing answer names the situation, the method and the check. For example: "a new market's reviews mix two languages and we do not know the aspects yet, so explore a sample with an LLM, check consistency and cost, then score at scale with a cheaper validated model". "Use the LLM for everything" is a weak answer because of cost and auditability.

Named entity recognition

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

Sentiment analysis estimates the attitude a text expresses. Named entity recognition (NER) identifies the entities a text mentions, sorted into categories such as person, organization, place, product, money and date. It answers "who and what is named?", not "how does the writer feel?". Its uses are to route a complaint to the team responsible for the named product or service, and to extract competitor mentions and store relevant entities as structured fields. The mock exam's version: from "The BrightNote app failed during my Paris trip", NER gives product: BrightNote and place: Paris. A sentiment score or a word count would not give those fields. If a question says "NER tells you how positive a review is", that is false.

Framework to remember

Named entity recognition: entity categories and applications (6 categories, 2 applications)

  • Entity categories: person · organization · place · product · money · date.
  • Application 1, routing: assign a complaint to the team responsible for the identified product or service.
  • Application 2, structured fields: extract competitor mentions and store relevant entities as fields.

How to recall it: sentiment says how they feel; NER says who and what they name.

The short version

Models need numbers, so text must be cleaned and represented first. Tokenizing splits text; lemmatizing maps forms to a dictionary word ("was, were, is" to "be"); stemming chops endings and may leave a non-word ("automat"). English uses spaces (nltk); Chinese uses a dictionary and probabilities (jieba), so brand terms must be added or they split wrongly. Remove stop words, but keep negation, or "not good" becomes "good". Bag of words loses order, so "great sound, terrible battery" and "terrible sound, great battery" get identical rows. TF-IDF is TF × ln(N/df): frequent here and rare elsewhere; in the three-review example battery scores 2 × 1.10 = 2.20 in d2, sound 1.10 in d1, while great and fit have IDF 0.41. N-grams keep local order at the cost of larger, sparser vocabularies. Embeddings put similar usage close together; king − man + woman ≈ queen is an analogy, not a rule. Cosine compares direction only (1 same, 0 perpendicular, −1 opposite), ignores length, is undefined for a zero vector, and a high cosine does not prove the same complaint. LDA treats each review as a mixture of topics and each topic as word probabilities (0.60 × 0.06 + 0.40 × 0.35 = 0.176); read the word bars and the reviews before naming a topic, and remember one topic can mix praise, complaints and questions. For sentiment, aspect level tells you what to fix. The lexicon (positive hits minus negative hits) is cheap and auditable but misses sarcasm (C000301 scores +1 at 1 star); SnowNLP agrees on only 243 of 355 Chinese reviews (68.45%), so validate before reporting. Supervised learning is Week 4 with TF-IDF columns and needs good labels, which star ratings are not. An LLM zero-shot with fixed output fields can catch sarcasm but costs more and varies. Route clean language to a lexicon, stable labeled tasks to a supervised model, and aspects, sarcasm or mixed language to LLM-assisted analysis.

Practice

P7.1 A text pipeline for a fictional bakery chain turns "baking", "baked" and "bakery" all into "bak". Which operation is this, and what gives it away? (a) Tokenization, because the words are split from the sentence. (b) Lemmatization, because each word is mapped to its dictionary form. (c) Stemming, because endings are removed by rules and the result need not be a dictionary word. (d) Stop-word removal, because common words are dropped.

Show the answer

(c) Stemming. "bak" is not an English word, which is the sign of rule-based chopping. Lemmatization would give real dictionary forms such as "bake" and "bakery". Tokenization only splits text and does not change the words, and stop-word removal deletes words rather than shortening them.

P7.2 A search tool for a fictional hotel app represents reviews with TF-IDF over its review vocabulary. A guest searches "elevator out of order", but none of those words appear in any review. A colleague also notes that review X ("Room was spotless, staff were lovely") and review Y ("Room was spotless, staff were lovely, I would return, the room was spotless") have a cosine similarity of about 0.84 when you use raw word counts. Which statement is correct? (a) The query's cosine with every review is 0, so all reviews tie. (b) The query vector is zero, so its cosine is undefined and no ranking can be made; X and Y score high mainly because they point in a similar direction, even though Y is longer. (c) Y's cosine with X is low because Y is longer. (d) A cosine of 0.84 proves that X and Y report the same complaint.

Show the answer

(b). A query with no known words gives a zero vector, which has no direction, so cosine is undefined (see the "waterproof" query in the review-search demo). Cosine divides by the vector lengths, so length alone does not lower the score; X and Y share most of their words. With raw counts, the dot product is 9 (Y repeats "room", "was" and "spotless"), ‖X‖ = √6 and ‖Y‖ = √19, so cos θ = 9 / (2.45 × 4.36) ≈ 0.84. The score is below 1 only because Y adds new words and repeats some words more than others, not because it is longer. And a high cosine shows similar wording, not proof of the same complaint, so (d) is wrong; here neither review is a complaint at all.

P7.3 A fictional tea shop has N = 4 reviews. The word "tea" appears in all 4 reviews, "lid" in 2 of them and "leak" in 1. Review r3 contains "tea" 3 times, "lid" once and "leak" twice. Using IDF = ln(N/df), compute the TF-IDF weight of each word in r3, say which word best describes what r3 is about, and explain in one sentence why "tea" scores as it does.

Show the answer

IDF: tea = ln(4/4) = ln(1) = 0; lid = ln(4/2) = ln(2) = 0.69; leak = ln(4/1) = ln(4) = 1.39. Weights in r3: tea = 3 × 0 = 0; lid = 1 × 0.69 = 0.69; leak = 2 × 1.39 = 2.77 (from 2 × 1.386). "leak" has the highest weight, so r3 is most distinctively about a leak, which you should confirm by reading the review. "tea" appears in every review, so it does not distinguish any review from the others, and its IDF and weight are zero even though it is the most repeated word in r3.

P7.4 A topic model on fictional food-delivery reviews gives one review a mixture of 70% "Delivery" and 30% "Taste". The topic-word probabilities for the word "late" are P(late | Delivery) = 0.20 and P(late | Taste) = 0.02. (a) What is the probability that a word drawn from this review is "late"? (b) The analyst wants to call a third topic "Complaints about packaging" after seeing only its topic number. In two or three sentences, say what she should do first and why.

Show the answer

(a) P(late) = 0.70 × 0.20 + 0.30 × 0.02 = 0.140 + 0.006 = 0.146, or 14.6%. Almost all of it comes from the Delivery topic. (b) The model only produces word probabilities; it does not name topics. She should inspect the topic's highest-probability words and read original reviews that load on it. Even if the words are about packaging, the topic may mix praise, complaints and questions, so she should also check sentiment before calling it "complaints" and choosing an action.

P7.5 PedalGo is a fictional bike-sharing app. Its analyst scores reviews with a small lexicon. Positive words: love, smooth, great, fast. Negative words: slow, broken, bad. Score = positive hits − negative hits. Three reviews arrive:

ReviewStarsText
A3"Love the smooth ride, but the app is slow."
B1"Great, the lock jams every single morning."
C4"Not bad, and payment is fast."

(a) Compute each score without any negation rule, then for C with a rule that flips a hit when "not" appears just before it. (b) Which review does the lexicon get wrong even with the negation rule, and why? (c) Give an aspect-level reading of A and B and one specific action. (d) Which method from the routing guide would you test for reviews like B, and what would you check?

Show the answer

(a) A: love +1, smooth +1, slow −1, total +1, positive. B: great +1; "lock" and "jams" are not in the list; total +1, positive. C without a rule: bad −1, fast +1, total 0, neutral. C with the negation rule: "not bad" flips to +1, fast +1, total +2, positive, which fits the 4 stars.

(b) B. "Great" is sarcastic: a lock that jams every morning is a failure, and the 1-star rating agrees. The lexicon only sees the word "great" and has no entry for "jams", so it scores +1. This is the same pattern as the course's 'Amazing battery, I charge them twice a day.' Positive wording beside a 1-star rating is the flag the validation steps tell you to check.

(c) A: ride +, app speed −. B: lock −. A document-level label would call A "mixed" and B "positive", and neither tells the team what to fix. Specific action: send a hardware team to inspect and repair the locks that jam (supported by B), or have the app team investigate the slow app (supported by A). These three reviews justify investigation, not a claim about how common the problem is.

(d) Sarcasm points to LLM-assisted analysis, asking for fixed fields such as sentiment, aspects, sarcasm and a short reason. Before using it, check output consistency across repeated runs and the cost per thousand reviews, and compare its answers with the review text and the rating. Clean, standard reviews like C could stay with the cheaper lexicon once negation is handled.

Week 8 Social network analysis

Nodes, edges and components

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

Last week, text became numbers. This week, relationships become a graph: accounts are nodes, collaborations are ties. The computer never walks the city; it measures positions in the network. A spreadsheet of 240 accounts shows nothing about who sits at the centre. Draw the connections, and structure appears: groups, bridges, hubs. The lecture's line: "a table of accounts hides structure; the map shows it". You already manage networks without calling them that: the org chart is not the organisation (the work runs on who actually calls whom), supply chains are graphs (risk pools at a single-source hub), and customers arrive along edges (referrals, invitations, shared rides).

A network has two parts. A node (also called a vertex) is the thing being connected: a person, a company, an account. An edge (also called a tie or a link) is a relationship between two nodes: collaborates with, reposts, mentions. The lecture uses five words for everything else. An edge is undirected when the relationship is mutual by nature, such as a collaboration where both accounts appeared together. An edge is directed when it has a direction: account X reposted account Y, which does not mean Y reposted X. A component is an island of connected nodes: inside it, every node can reach every other node by following edges, and no edge leaves the island. The largest component is where most of the action is.

Framework to remember

Five words carry the session (5 items)

  • Node (vertex): a person, a company, an account.
  • Edge (tie, link): a relationship: collaborates with, reposts, mentions.
  • Directed: the tie has a direction, for example who reposted whom.
  • Undirected: the tie is mutual, for example both accounts showed up together.
  • Component: an island of connected nodes; the largest one is where the action is.

How to recall it: two things (node, edge), two kinds of edge (directed, undirected), one island (component). "Everything else this session is built from these five words."

Before any measure is computed, you can already read four positions off a map. A hub has many ties. A bridge is the only path across between two parts. The periphery is loosely attached. A cluster is a dense neighbourhood. Same accounts, same scene: different position, different strategy. The measures in the next sections make these positions precise.

The session then asks four questions, in this order: who matters (centrality), where the groups are (communities), how things travel (diffusion), and why it is worth money (network effects). The first two are the examined core of this chapter. The lecture also separates two terms that sound alike: network analysis is the method (it reads the network's shape), while a network effect is the economics (more users, more value; it cares about size, not shape). "Effect: the network's size is worth money. Analysis: the network's shape tells you what to do."

Reachability follows directly from the edges you are given. If two components have no edge between them, a message that moves only along edges cannot cross from one to the other, however close they look on the drawing. The picture is just a layout; only the edge list is data. With directed edges you must also respect the arrow: an arrow from U to P lets a message go from U to P, never from P back to U. Social network analysis (SNA) is the general method of network analysis applied to people, and it adds social ideas such as brokers and weak ties.

The whole examined section uses one small example from the course network of 240 creator and brand accounts and 1,162 ties. It is a corner of eight accounts with eight undirected ties, where a tie means repeated collaboration. On the left, A003, A005 and A038 form a triangle, and A173 hangs off A038. In the middle, A012 links A003 to A126. On the right, A126 has two tail accounts, A073 and A089. Every account can reach every other, so the corner is one component. Write this edge list on your note sheet; every number in the next sections comes from it.

EdgeWhere it sits
A003 – A005left triangle
A003 – A038left triangle
A005 – A038left triangle
A038 – A173left tail
A003 – A012the crossing, left half
A012 – A126the crossing, right half
A126 – A073right tail
A126 – A089right tail

Which account matters most? Three instincts

On the exam

Look at the corner and ask a simple question: which account matters most here? The class usually gives three different answers, and each one is a reasonable instinct. The first instinct points to the most ties, the popular one. The second points to the crossing, the broker who sits where the two sides meet. The third points to the fastest reach, the insider who can get a message to everyone in the fewest steps. The point is that "matters most" is not one question. It is three questions, and each one has its own measure. The lecture's line: "three answers, three instincts".

The three instincts become three named measures. Degree asks "how many ties?" and finds the popular account. Betweenness asks "how often are you the bridge?" and finds the broker. Closeness asks "how fast can you reach everyone?" and finds the insider. Together they are called centrality measures, because each one gives a different meaning to being central.

A fourth idea is added, for concept only: eigenvector centrality and PageRank, the prestige account. Here an account is important if its contacts are important. Ten ties to well-connected accounts count for more than ten ties to isolated ones. PageRank, which Google introduced in 1998 to rank web pages, uses the same trick: a page matters if pages that matter link to it. You do not need to compute it; you need to recognise it as "connected to the well-connected". The managerial line tells you why any of this matters: the measures tell you whom to seed, whom to retain, and whom to watch.

Core framework: learn this by heart

Three ways to measure position, plus the prestige account (3 measures + 1 concept)

  • Degree: "How many ties?" The popular account.
  • Betweenness: "How often are you the bridge?" The broker.
  • Closeness: "How fast can you reach everyone?" The insider.
  • Eigenvector and PageRank: connected to the well-connected; important if your contacts are. The prestige account. PageRank (Google, 1998) is the same trick. Concept only, no calculation.

How to recall it: count, crossing, reach, prestige.

The managerial use is itself a three-part list worth remembering. It turns the measures into three decisions a manager actually takes.

Framework to remember

Managerial use of centrality: whom to seed, whom to retain, whom to watch (3 decisions)

  • Whom to seed: where to start a message or campaign (reach, crossing, speed).
  • Whom to retain: whom you cannot afford to lose (the broker whose exit splits groups).
  • Whom to watch: whose position carries risk or influence you should monitor.

How to recall it: seed, retain, watch. The three words are the course's; the examples after each colon are my own reading of how the later sections use them.

Degree: counting ties

On the exam

Degree is the simplest measure: count the ties an account has, nothing else. The lecture calls it local popularity, because it only looks at an account's immediate neighbours. You do not need any algorithm, only the edge list.

Worked example: degree in the corner

Go through the edge list and count how often each account appears. A003 appears in three edges (with A005, A038 and A012), so its degree is 3. A038 appears with A003, A005 and A173: degree 3. A126 appears with A012, A073 and A089: degree 3. A005 has A003 and A038: degree 2. A012 has A003 and A126: degree 2. A073, A089 and A173 each have one tie: degree 1. As a check, the degrees add up to 3 + 3 + 3 + 2 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 16, which is twice the 8 edges, because every edge has two ends.

The business reading: three accounts tie at the top with three ties each. The lecture's bottom line says it plainly: "a tie at the top: the count alone cannot separate the three leaders". Degree gives you a shortlist, not a winner.

The lecture then lists what degree buys you. First, reach: degree is a measure of audience size, so it is the first filter when a brand shortlists accounts. Second, seeding deals: the count prices the placement, which is why influencer fees follow follower numbers. Third, it is fast to read: no algorithm needed.

It also lists what degree misses, and this is the part exams like. Degree misses position. Three ties say nothing about where those ties lead. A003 and A038 both hold three ties, but A003's ties lead across to the other side of the corner, while A038's ties mostly stay inside the left triangle plus one tail. Degree also cannot tell real ties from fake ones: bought followers raise the count without raising real reach. And the broker in this corner, A012, holds only two ties, so a ranking by degree would put it in the lower half. The summary line: "a high count answers how many; never where".

Framework to remember

What degree buys, and what it misses (3 buys + 3 misses)

What it buys: reachWhat it misses: position
Audience size: the first filter when a brand shortlists accounts.Where ties lead: three ties say nothing about where those ties lead.
Seeding deals: the count prices the placement.Bought followers: they raise the count without raising real reach.
Fast to read: no algorithm needed.The broker: the broker in this corner holds only two ties.

How to recall it: degree buys reach and misses position. "A high count answers how many; never where."

Trap

"The account with the most ties is the most important" is the classic wrong answer. Degree measures popularity. It does not tell you whether the account holds the network together, and it can be inflated with bought followers. If the question is about connecting groups or about what breaks when someone leaves, degree is the wrong measure.

Betweenness: counting the paths through you

On the exam

Betweenness asks how often an account lies on the shortest path between two other accounts. Take every pair of accounts. Find the shortest route between them. If that route passes through account v (and v is not one of the two ends), v gets a point. Add up the points over all pairs, and you have v's betweenness. An account with high betweenness is a broker: many conversations have to pass through it. In the corner every pair has exactly one shortest path, because the only loop is the left triangle, so you can count whole pairs. In bigger networks, when a pair has several equally short paths, each path carries a share of the point.

Worked example: betweenness by hand

Start with A003. Ask which pairs must go through it. A005, A038 and A173 are on the left. A012, A126, A073 and A089 are on the right. Every route from the left group to the right group crosses A003, because A003 is the only left account tied to A012. That gives 3 left accounts × 4 right accounts = 12 pairs. Pairs inside the left group do not use A003: A005 and A038 are tied directly, and A005 reaches A173 through A038. So A003's betweenness is 12.

A012 is the mirror image. The four accounts on its left (A003, A005, A038, A173) can reach the three on its right (A126, A073, A089) only through A012: 4 × 3 = 12 pairs. Betweenness 12.

A126: the five accounts on the far side (A003, A005, A012, A038, A173) reach A073 only through A126, and the same five reach A089 only through A126. That is 5 + 5 = 10 pairs. A073 and A089 are not tied to each other, so their own pair also goes through A126: one more. Total 5 + 5 + 1 = 11.

A038: A173's only tie is to A038, so A173 reaches each of the other six accounts through A038. That is 6 pairs. Betweenness 6.

Everyone else, A005, A073, A089 and A173, scores 0. They lie on no shortest path between two other accounts. A005 is the telling case: it has the same degree as A012 (two ties each), yet A012 carries 12 paths and A005 carries none. The bottom line: "position, not popularity".

Next, what betweenness buys. The first use is crossing communities: if you want a message to travel from one group into another, seed it at the broker, because the broker is where the groups touch. The second use is finding change agents. The lecture cites Battilana and Casciaro (2012), who found that people who drive organisational change tend to sit in high-betweenness positions: they bridge groups that do not otherwise talk, so they see more information and can carry ideas across. The lecture's image is a photo of a single bridge: "one bridge: every crossing pays it a visit."

The third point is the risk side. A broker can be a single point of failure. Where no second road exists, removing the broker splits the map. So high betweenness is good news when you want to spread something, and a warning when you depend on one account to hold two groups together. The lecture closes with a neat contrast: "degree never saw the crossing; betweenness sees nothing else."

Framework to remember

What betweenness buys (2 uses + 1 risk)

  • Crossing communities: seed at the broker to carry a message from one group into another.
  • Change agents: Battilana and Casciaro: change agents sit on betweenness.
  • The risk side: a broker can be a single point of failure; where no second road exists, removing it splits the map.

How to recall it: cross, change, collapse (my own mnemonic, not a course term). "One bridge: every crossing pays it a visit."

Exam tip

For a betweenness count by hand, do not list all 21 pairs blindly. For each account, ask: "if I delete this account, which accounts end up on different sides?" Multiply the group sizes on each side, and add pairs on the same side that still had to pass through it (like A073 and A089 for A126). In the corner this gives 12, 12, 11 and 6 quickly.

Closeness: how fast you reach everyone

On the exam

Closeness measures how near an account is to everyone else. For each account, find the shortest distance in hops (number of edges) to every other account, and add those distances up. A small distance sum means the account can get a message to everyone quickly. To turn the sum into a score where bigger means better, divide the number of other accounts by the sum.

Closeness = (n − 1) / distance sum

Here n is the number of accounts in the network, so n − 1 is the number of other accounts (7 in the corner). The distance sum is the total of the shortest hop distances from this account to every other account. A lower sum gives a higher closeness. The highest possible score is 1, for an account tied directly to everyone.

Framework to remember

Closeness = (n − 1) / distance sum, by hand (3 steps, in order)

  1. Distances: for one account, write the shortest hop count to each of the other n − 1 accounts.
  2. Distance sum: add them up. Lower sum, faster reach.
  3. Divide: closeness = (n − 1) / distance sum. Higher closeness, faster reach.

How to recall it: "others over sum". In the corner: A003 and A012 = 7/13 = .538, A126 = 7/15 = .467. An exact tie at the top means two centres.

Worked example: closeness in the corner

A003: its neighbours A005, A038 and A012 are 1 hop away. A126 and A173 are 2 hops away (through A012 and A038). A073 and A089 are 3 hops away (A003 to A012 to A126 to the tail). Sum: 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 + 3 = 13. Closeness = 7 / 13 = 0.538.

A012: A003 and A126 are 1 hop; A005, A038, A073 and A089 are 2 hops; A173 is 3 hops. Sum: 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 3 = 13. Closeness = 7 / 13 = 0.538, exactly the same as A003.

A126: A012, A073 and A089 are 1 hop; A003 is 2; A005 and A038 are 3; A173 is 4. Sum: 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 3 + 4 = 15. Closeness = 7 / 15 = 0.467.

The remaining distance sums, which you can check the same way: A038 has 16, A005 has 17, A073 and A089 have 21 each, and A173 has 22. A173 sits at the far end of the longest chain: it is 5 hops from A073 and A089.

The business reading: there is an exact tie at the top, so the corner has two centres, A003 and A012. Both sit on the crossing. The bottom line: "lower sum, faster reach: both centres sit on the crossing".

AccountDegreeBetweennessDistance sumCloseness
A003312137/13 = 0.538
A012212137/13 = 0.538
A126311157/15 = 0.467
A03836167/16 = 0.438
A00520177/17 = 0.412
A07310217/21 = 0.333
A08910217/21 = 0.333
A17310227/22 = 0.318

Read the table across a row and you see why the week insists on three measures. A038 ties for the top on degree but is only fourth on closeness and fourth on betweenness. A012 is in the lower half on degree but joint first on both betweenness and closeness. The lecture works out the closeness values only for A003, A012 and A126; the others in the table are the same formula applied to the remaining distance sums.

Trap

Do not mix up the distance sum and the closeness score. A low distance sum is good, a high closeness is good: they move in opposite directions. Also, closeness divides n − 1 by the sum, not the sum by n − 1. If you get 13/7 = 1.86, you have flipped it.

What each measure buys you, and the full graph's two leaders

On the exam

The measures can be gathered into a job table. The idea is to choose the measure after you know the job, because no measure does every job.

Core framework: learn this by heart

What each measure buys you (4 measures, one job each)

  • Degree · reach: influencer deals and follower counts; bought followers inflate it.
  • Betweenness · brokers: seed here to cross communities; change agents sit here.
  • Closeness · speed: who hears it first: launch day, crisis communication.
  • Eigenvector · prestige: advocates the well-connected follow; PageRank, Google 1998.

How to recall it: reach, brokers, speed, prestige. "Pick the measure to match the job; no measure does every job."

MeasureWhat it capturesBusiness jobWeak spot
DegreeReachInfluencer deals and follower countsBought followers inflate it
BetweennessBrokersSeed here to cross communities; change agents sit hereThe broker can be a single point of failure
ClosenessSpeedWho hears it first: launch day, crisis communicationSays nothing about who holds groups together
EigenvectorPrestigeAdvocates the well-connected follow; PageRank, Google 1998Concept only this week

The last column's entries for closeness and eigenvector are my own summary; the lecture names only the bought-followers weakness for degree and the single-point-of-failure risk for betweenness. A good exam answer links the measure to the decision. For a launch day or a crisis message that must reach everyone quickly, use closeness. For a campaign that has to jump from one community into another, use betweenness. For a pure audience buy, degree is the first filter, but check the followers are real.

Now move from the corner to the full course network of 240 accounts, and the result is the headline of the section: the full graph crowns two leaders. A003 (foodie_lane_01) has degree rank 1 of 240, with 34 ties: the count's leader. A126 (city_pace_02) has betweenness rank 1 of 240, but its degree rank is only 10, with 15 ties: the crossing's leader. "Two leaders, two definitions of mattering." If you gave a retention bonus to "the most important account" by follower count, you would pick A003 and might miss the account that actually holds the scenes together.

Exam tip

When a question gives you two accounts, one with many ties and one with high betweenness, the expected answer is almost always "it depends on the job". Say what you lose if each one leaves: a hub's departure thins reach, a broker's departure can cut the short roads between groups.

Which removal breaks the corner?

On the exam

This is a pair exercise. Remove one account so that the corner falls into pieces. Name it, then find a second account that also does it. The hint: "the cut you are looking for is a position, not a count." In other words, look at betweenness, not degree.

Worked example: the removal debrief

Remove A003. Its three edges disappear. A005, A038 and A173 still connect to each other, but nothing links them to A012. The corner splits into left {A005, A038, A173} and right {A012, A126, A073, A089}.

Remove A012. Now A003's group {A003, A005, A038, A173} and A126's group {A126, A073, A089} have no edge between them. Again a left and a right piece.

Remove A038. A173's only tie is gone, so A173 is cut off, and the other six stay connected. Two pieces.

Remove A126. A073 and A089 each lose their only tie, so the result is three pieces: the five accounts on the left and middle, A073 alone, and A089 alone.

Remove anyone else, for example A005, and nothing splits: A003 and A038 are still tied directly.

Removed accountBetweennessResult
A00312Two pieces: left and right
A01212Two pieces: left and right
A12611Three pieces: A073 and A089 each stand alone
A0386Two pieces: A173 cut off
A005, A073, A089 or A1730No split

The lesson is that "every clean cut passes through a broker". The accounts with betweenness above zero are exactly the ones whose removal breaks the corner, and betweenness saw the cut before anyone drew it. Notice that A038, with three ties, only cuts off one tail account, while A012, with only two ties, splits the corner in half.

Why should a manager care? The lecture gives two readings. First, the broker is a retention risk and a succession question: if it leaves, two groups may lose their only connection, so you need a plan for who would take that role. Second, the broker is the account you want on the change programme, which links back to the change agents in what betweenness buys. The lecture calls these "directions, not answers": any pair that names a broker has understood the measure.

Framework to remember

What the removals show (3 results + 3 management readings)

  • Remove A003 or A012: the corner splits into left and right.
  • Remove A038: A173 is cut off.
  • Remove A126: three pieces; A073 and A089 each stand alone.
  • Retention risk: if the broker leaves, groups lose their connection.
  • Succession question: who would take the broker's role?
  • Change programme: the broker is the account you want on it.

How to recall it: "Every clean cut passes through a broker. Betweenness saw the cut before anyone drew it."

Trap

The neat match between betweenness and "removal splits the map" holds in the corner because there are almost no second roads. In a large network, other paths usually exist. The practice solutions note that on the 240-account map, removing A126 does not split anything new, even though it is the betweenness leader; the short roads just get longer. So say "a broker can be a single point of failure, where no second road exists", as the lecture does, not "removing the top broker always splits the network".

What a community is

On the exam

The second examined part of the week moves from single accounts to groups. A community is a set of nodes that is denser inside than outside: the members have many ties to each other and few ties to the rest of the network. The lecture says this is the whole definition. Picture two dense blobs with one thin crossing between them: "dense inside, one thin crossing". The lecture's phrase "markets within the market" is the business meaning: a large network is often several smaller networks loosely joined.

What does a community buy you? The lecture lists natural segments (groups of customers or creators who actually interact), regional pockets (a city or district where ties cluster), and fraud rings: accounts that trade only with each other. A fraud ring shows up as a community because its members are very densely tied inside and barely tied to anyone outside, which is unusual for honest buyers and sellers. The key line: "nobody declares the groups; the algorithm finds them in the ties."

Core framework: learn this by heart

What a community is, and what it buys (1 definition + 3 uses)

  • Definition: denser inside than outside. That is the whole definition. Markets within the market.
  • Natural segments: groups who actually interact.
  • Regional pockets: places where ties cluster.
  • Fraud rings: accounts that trade only with each other.

How to recall it: segments, pockets, rings. Nobody declares the groups; the algorithm finds them in the ties.

Modularity and the Louvain search

On the exam

To find communities, a computer needs two things: a score that says how good a split is, and a search method that looks for a split with a high score. The lecture adds a third part that you must always mention: the method's honest limit.

Framework to remember

How the algorithm finds communities: score, search, honest limit (3 parts)

  • The score: modularity Q: edges inside the groups, minus what chance alone would give. Higher is tighter.
  • The search: Louvain (Blondel et al., 2008): start alone, move to raise Q, repeat. Alternative: label propagation.
  • The honest limit: small groups get merged into big ones; the score cannot see a group below a certain size.

How to recall it: score it, search it, know where it fails.

The score is modularity, written Q. Every way of splitting the network into groups gets a Q value. The lecture defines it in words: the edges inside the groups, minus what chance alone would give. Higher is tighter. "What chance alone would give" matters because big, well-connected groups would hold many inside ties even if ties were placed at random. Modularity only rewards inside ties beyond that random expectation. A Q near zero means the split is no better than chance.

Q = (share of edges inside the groups) − (share expected inside the groups by chance)

The first part is the actual fraction of all edges that fall inside groups. The second part is the fraction you would expect inside those same groups if the ties were scattered at random, keeping each account's number of ties. A good split has many more inside edges than chance would predict, so Q is clearly positive. The week notes write the full version as Q = Σ over groups of [ lc/m − (dc/2m)2 ], where m is the number of edges, lc the edges inside group c, and dc the sum of degrees in group c. The exam material only asks you to read Q, not to compute it.

The search is the Louvain method (Blondel et al., 2008). It works in three plain steps. Every account starts alone, in a group of one. Then the algorithm moves each account to the neighbouring group that raises Q the most. It repeats until nothing moves, because no single move can raise Q any further. The week notes add that Louvain then merges each group into a single super-node and runs the moves again, but the three steps are what you need. The important feature for the exam is that you never tell Louvain how many groups to find; the number of groups comes out of the search. Platforms run this nightly on millions of nodes; the lab ran it on 240 accounts and found seven communities at Q = 0.808.

Core framework: learn this by heart

The Louvain search (3 steps, in order)

  1. Start alone: every account starts in a group of its own.
  2. Move: move each account to the group that raises Q most.
  3. Repeat: repeat until nothing moves.

How to recall it: alone, move, stop. You never tell Louvain how many groups to find.

The lecture also mentions a second method, label propagation: every account starts with its own label, and at each step an account takes the label most common among its neighbours. Labels spread through dense areas until they stabilise, and each final label is a community.

Then comes the honest limit. Modularity has a blind spot for size: small groups get merged into big ones, because the score cannot see a group below a certain size. If you are looking for a tiny fraud ring of four accounts inside a network of millions, a modularity search may swallow it into a larger community.

Trap

A high Q says the split is tight. It does not say the groups mean anything in business terms, and it does not promise that every small real group was found. To check meaning, you compare the groups with an outside variable, as the lab did with the account handles.

Where does A012 belong? The corner, split

On the exam

Now add the account handles to the corner. A003, A005, A038 and A012 all have food handles (foodie_lane_01, foodie_lane_03, foodie_lane_36, foodie_lane_10). A173 is stroller_day_09, A126 is city_pace_02, A073 is bean_alley_33 and A089 is gallery_step_07. The question: A012 carries a food handle but sits on the crossing. Does it land with the family on the left or the city side on the right? The hint: "the algorithm reads ties, not handles." In class this is a show of hands: hold your answer before reading the computed split.

Worked example: the computed split

The algorithm returns two groups. Left: A003, A005, A038, A173. Right: A012, A126, A073, A089. A012 lands with the city side: its ties to the right decide. The split scores Q = 0.367, which the lecture reads as "denser inside than outside, comfortably". The week notes give 0.3 as a usual rule of thumb for real structure.

Here is where the number comes from (from the week notes, for understanding only). There are 8 edges. The left group holds 4 inside edges (the triangle plus A038–A173) and its degrees add to 3 + 2 + 3 + 1 = 9. The right group holds 3 inside edges (A012–A126, A126–A073, A126–A089) and its degrees add to 2 + 3 + 1 + 1 = 7. Only one edge, A003–A012, crosses. Q = [4/8 − (9/16)2] + [3/8 − (7/16)2] = [0.500 − 0.316] + [0.375 − 0.191] = 0.367.

Why does A012 go right? It has one tie on each side, so a simple count is a draw. The score breaks the draw. If you move A012 to the left, the left group gets 5 inside edges with degree sum 11, the right gets 2 inside edges with degree sum 5, and Q = [5/8 − (11/16)2] + [2/8 − (5/16)2] = 0.152 + 0.152 = 0.305. That is lower than 0.367. Adding A012 to the already large left group makes chance expect many more inside ties there, so the move costs more than it gains. The balanced four-and-four split holds its ties better.

The business reading, in the lecture's words: "the handle says food; the ties say city. The ties win." A012's label would put it in the food scene, but the people it actually works with pull it to the city side.

Exam tip

If you are asked why A012 joined the right group, the safe answer is: community detection uses only ties, not names or content labels, and the split with A012 on the right has the higher modularity (0.367). Saying "because its handle is food it should be left" is exactly the mistake this example is designed to catch.

Clustering versus communities, and segments you did not declare

On the exam

This part is a recap from Week 6, and it makes a good comparison question. Both methods answer the same segmentation question, "which customers or accounts belong together?", but they use different data.

Week 6: clusteringWeek 8: communities
Groups byFeatures: what customers boughtTies: whom accounts work with
Number of groupsK-means needs k declared in advanceLouvain finds k on its own
Meaning of distanceFeature similarityPath length

Read the last row carefully. In K-means, two customers are "close" when their numbers look alike, for example similar spending and visit frequency, even if they have never met. In a network, two accounts are "close" when few hops separate them, even if their profiles look nothing alike. Two creators can make very different content and still sit in the same community because they collaborate. The lecture's line: "same segmentation question; this time the relationship is the data."

Core framework: learn this by heart

Two ways to find groups: clustering (Week 6) versus communities (Week 8) (3 dimensions)

DimensionWeek 6: clusteringWeek 8: communities
Groups byFeatures: what customers boughtTies: whom accounts work with
Number of groupsK-means needs k declared in advanceLouvain finds k on its own
Distance meansFeature similarityPath length

How to recall it: what you group by, who picks k, what "close" means. Same segmentation question; this time the relationship is the data.

Communities also link to marketing theory. A detected community is Kotler's measurable, reachable segment (Kotler and Keller, 2016), found in the data instead of declared in a plan. It is measurable because you can count its members and ties, and reachable because the ties themselves are the channels a message can travel along. In the course network, the algorithm never saw the accounts' content categories, and in the lab you compare its groups with the real scene. The practice solutions report that six large communities each matched one handle family, and a seventh held the twelve city_pace accounts. The lecture repeats the fraud-ring use: accounts that trade only with each other light up as abnormal communities. Its image is a photo of a city: "one city, many neighbourhoods". The closing line: "nobody declared these groups; the ties declared them."

Framework to remember

Segments you did not have to declare (3 points)

  • Discovered, not declared: a detected community is Kotler's measurable, reachable segment, found in the data instead of declared in a plan (Kotler and Keller, 2016).
  • This network: the algorithm never saw the accounts' content categories; you compare its groups with the real scene to check meaning.
  • Fraud rings: accounts that trade only with each other light up as abnormal communities.

How to recall it: "Nobody declared these groups; the ties declared them."

The wrap of the examined material fits in two lines. The measures: degree is the count, betweenness is the crossing, closeness is the reach. The groups: a community is denser inside than outside, scored by modularity Q and found by Louvain. "Position says where to start; groups say where the segments are."

How things travel: cascades and seeds

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

The rest of the lecture asks how things spread through the map. Information, adoption and rumours move along ties, not through the air. Four ideas describe how spread works.

Framework to remember

How things spread (4 ideas)

  • Travel along edges: information, adoption and rumours move along ties, not through the air.
  • Weak ties: Granovetter (1973): acquaintances carry the new; close friends mostly know what you know.
  • Thresholds: people adopt when enough of their neighbours adopt, often after two or more exposures.
  • Virality: engineering the spread through invites, referrals and shares.

How to recall it: the managerial line is "seed the bridges; measure the cascade".

A cascade is a spread that starts from one or more seeds and moves outward, round by round. The lecture's warm-up uses the corner with the simplest rule: every newly active account activates all its neighbours in the next round. Seed A003 (round 0). Round 1: A005, A012 and A038. Round 2: A126 and A173. Round 3: A073 and A089, and the corner is covered. Seed A012 and the corner is also covered in three rounds. Seed A126 and it takes four, because the spread has to drain one hop per round down the left side. Same map, different clock: the seed sets the speed.

The mock exam's Case 2 uses this kind of round-by-round rule on a small directed network, so learn the rules well. An account activates only once. A newly activated account sends in the next round, not immediately. If two arrows reach the same account in the same round, or a later arrow reaches an account that is already active, it does not count as extra reach: you count distinct accounts. And you can only move in the direction of the arrow, so an account whose only arrow points into the active set can never be activated. Do not forget to state whether your total includes the seed.

Framework to remember

Round-by-round cascade rules (5 rules, apply every round)

  1. Round 0: only the seed is active.
  2. Send next round: an account activated in round r sends along all its outgoing ties in round r + 1, not immediately.
  3. Follow the arrow: a message moves X → Y only; never against the arrow.
  4. Activate once: repeated arrivals, or arrivals at an already active account, add no reach. Count distinct accounts.
  5. Stop and count: stop at the stated round and say whether the total includes the seed.

How to recall it: these five rules are my own summary of the mock exam's Case 2 rules, not a named course list. The mock's answer: P, then Q and R, then S; 4 distinct accounts including P.
