Real spread is random: a message passes along a tie only with some probability. The lab's independent cascade model gives each newly active account one chance to activate each neighbour, with probability 0.10. Because the outcome changes from run to run, one run proves nothing. The lecture compares seed choices fairly: the same 240-account network, the same model and probability, equal budgets (one head account costs what three waist accounts cost), and 300 runs each, reporting the average reach. One head account, A003, reached 11.6 accounts on average. Three waist accounts with 12 ties each and barely overlapping neighbourhoods reached 24.2, which is 2.09 times the head. One bridge account, A126, reached 10.5, so it still lost to the head. Portfolios beat heroes; one bridge alone is not a strategy. The lecture also mentions thresholds (people often adopt only after two or more exposures), weak ties (Granovetter, 1973: acquaintances carry new information across groups) and the K-factor, K = invites sent × conversion, where K above 1 means the spread sustains itself.

A classroom simulation where every transmission succeeds is useful for tracing paths, but it cannot rank seeds for a real campaign. Three limitations are worth naming: certain transmission exaggerates real spread; only one seed is tested, so there is nothing to rank against; and a small simplified network may not represent the campaign's real audience. The lecture asks the same question in its seed experiment: "Does one successful cascade establish the better portfolio?" No. Compare portfolios on the same network and model, over repeated trials. The K-factor reads the same way: K above 1, the spread sustains itself; K below 1, "you pay for every user, forever". Referral loops (Vinted, Dropbox) are K-factor engineering: seed the bridges, reward both sides, and measure the cascade, not the clicks. The lecture also mentions link prediction, which predicts the missing or next tie (people you may know, the brand a creator should hear from) from neighbour overlap, because of triadic closure: your friends become friends.

Framework to remember

A fair seed comparison (keep the same, repeat, report: 3 parts)

  • Keep the same: the network, the transmission model and probability, the budget, and the number of rounds.
  • Repeat: run each seed many times (the lecture uses 300), because chance decides whether each transmission succeeds.
  • Report: the average number of distinct accounts reached, counting the seed the same way, plus the variation.

How to recall it: my own summary, not a course term: "same, repeat, average". The lecture's result: three waist accounts 24.2, one head 11.6, one bridge 10.5. "Portfolios beat heroes; one bridge alone does not."

Exam tip

For a "is seed X the best?" question, the marks are for fairness: keep the network, transmission rule, probability, budget and number of rounds the same; run each seed many times because transmissions are random; report the average number of distinct accounts reached (with the same convention about counting the seed), and ideally the spread of results too. One successful cascade, or a simulation where every transmission succeeds, cannot rank seeds.

Network effects: registrations versus active ties

Background: not on the exam list, but the mock exam tested it

The lecture separates two ideas. Network analysis is the method: it reads the shape of the network. A network effect is the economics: each additional user makes the platform more valuable to every other user. It cares about size, not shape. Metcalfe's law prices a network at about n2; Odlyzko and Tilly's correction is n × log n, because most ties are weak or dormant. The manager's version is the part to remember: value tracks active, weighted ties, not registrations. Count the ties that carried something this month. "A platform with a million registrations and ten thousand monthly transactions is worth the ten thousand."

Framework to remember

Network effects, priced honestly (3 points)

  • Direct network effect: each additional user makes the platform more valuable to every other user; marketplaces tip because of it, and cold start is existential because of it.
  • Metcalfe, honestly: Metcalfe prices a network at about n2; Odlyzko and Tilly's correction is n × log n, because most ties are weak or dormant.
  • The manager's version: value tracks active, weighted ties, not registrations.

How to recall it: size is not value; activity is value, and the shape of the active ties tells you what to do.

Two-sided marketplaces add a flywheel: more sellers pull more buyers, and more buyers pull more sellers. Below critical mass the same loop runs backwards: an empty marketplace has no value, users leave, and it gets emptier still. The lecture names three escape moves: subsidise one side, win one dense niche first, and manufacture trust. "Chicken-and-egg is structural, not a marketing failure."

The case section shows this in marketplaces. Vinted kept listing free for sellers and charged buyers a protection fee; BlaBlaCar was free in France for about six years before charging; Xianyu launched into Taobao's existing graph and never had a cold start. The mock exam's B5 asks the same idea from the other side: if registrations double while transactions stay flat, value has not quadrupled. A good answer names an activity measure, such as completed monthly transactions or distinct buyer-seller pairs that traded this month, and adds that mapping those ties answers a structural question a user count cannot: are there disconnected components, is trade concentrated on a few hubs, are some groups cut off?

The short version

"Which account matters most" has three answers. Degree counts ties and measures reach, but it ignores position and can be inflated with bought followers. Betweenness counts the shortest paths that pass through an account; in the corner A003 and A012 score 12, A126 scores 11, A038 scores 6 and the rest score 0, and these brokers are exactly the accounts whose removal breaks the map, which makes them both change agents and single points of failure. Closeness is (n − 1) divided by the distance sum; A003 and A012 tie at 7/13 = 0.538 and A126 has 7/15 = 0.467. On the full 240-account map, A003 leads degree with 34 ties while A126 leads betweenness with only 15 ties, so pick the measure to match the job. A community is denser inside than outside; modularity Q scores a split as inside ties minus what chance would give, Louvain searches for a high Q without being told how many groups to find, and it can merge small groups. In the corner, the ties put A012 with the city side at Q = 0.367 even though its handle says food. Clustering groups by features with k chosen in advance; communities group by ties and find k themselves.

Practice

P8.1 (Multiple choice) A fictional tea brand maps six partner accounts with undirected ties: G–H, G–J, H–J, J–K, K–L, K–M. Which statement is correct? (a) J has the highest betweenness because it is the only account with three ties. (b) Removing K leaves three pieces. (c) G and H have positive betweenness because they sit in a triangle. (d) Removing any account with two ties splits the network.

Show the answer

(b). Removing K deletes the ties K–J, K–L and K–M. L and M each lose their only tie, so the pieces are {G, H, J}, {L} and {M}: three pieces, just like removing A126 in the corner. (a) is wrong twice: K also has three ties, and degree does not decide betweenness. (c) is wrong because G and H are tied directly and every path that leaves the triangle goes through J, so G and H lie on no shortest path between others. (d) is wrong because G and H have two ties each, and removing either one splits nothing.

P8.2 (Calculation) Use the same six accounts as P8.1. Compute the betweenness of J and K by hand, and the closeness of J, G and L using closeness = (n − 1) / distance sum. Which account would you choose to announce a product recall fastest, and which would you worry about losing?

Show the answer

Betweenness of J: the left group {G, H} reaches the right group {K, L, M} only through J, so 2 × 3 = 6. Betweenness of K: the left group {G, H, J} reaches L and M only through K, which is 3 × 2 = 6, and L and M reach each other through K, one more pair, so 7. Closeness, with n − 1 = 5: J's distances are G 1, H 1, K 1, L 2, M 2, sum 7, so 5/7 = 0.714. K's sum is also 7 (J, L, M at 1; G, H at 2), so K ties J at 0.714. G's distances are H 1, J 1, K 2, L 3, M 3, sum 10, so 5/10 = 0.500. L's distances are K 1, J 2, M 2, G 3, H 3, sum 11, so 5/11 = 0.455. For a fast recall message, closeness is the job, and J and K tie as two centres, so either works (or both). The one to worry about losing is K: it has the highest betweenness, and removing it leaves three pieces, so it is the single point of failure for L and M.

P8.3 (Case reading) A fictional bakery app maps seven accounts. An arrow X → Y means a message can travel from X to Y only. The complete list of arrows is: T1 → T2, T1 → T3, T2 → T4, T3 → T4, T3 → T5, T5 → T3, T4 → T6, T7 → T1. Only T1 is active at round 0. Each account activated in the previous round sends along all its outgoing arrows; every transmission succeeds; an account activates only once; the team stops after round 2. (i) List the newly activated accounts in rounds 1 and 2 and give the total number of distinct active accounts after round 2, including T1. (ii) Explain how you treated the arrivals at T4 and why T6 and T7 are not active. (iii) A manager says T1 is "the best seed" based on this run. Give one limitation and describe a fair comparison.

Show the answer

(i) Round 1: T2 and T3 (T1's two outgoing arrows). Round 2: T4 (from T2 and from T3) and T5 (from T3). Total after round 2: T1, T2, T3, T4 and T5, which is 5 distinct accounts. (ii) T4 receives the message twice in round 2, but it activates once and counts once; the second arrival adds no reach. In round 3 the arrow T5 → T3 would arrive at an account already active, which also adds nothing. T6 would only activate in round 3, after T4 sends, which is past the stopping point. T7 can never be reached from T1, because its only arrow points from T7 into T1, and a message cannot travel against an arrow. (iii) Limitation: every transmission is assumed to succeed, which overstates real spread, and only one seed was tested, so there is nothing to rank against. A fair comparison runs T1 and another seed on the same network, with the same transmission probability, the same budget and the same number of rounds, repeats each many times because transmissions are random, and reports the average number of distinct accounts reached (counting the seed the same way for both), plus a measure of variation.

P8.4 (Short answer) A fictional second-hand book platform finds that its Louvain communities split one large "fiction readers" segment from its marketing plan into three groups, one of them mostly accounts in a single university district. The marketing head says, "The algorithm is wrong; we decided there is one fiction segment." In three to five sentences, respond using this week's ideas.

Show the answer

Louvain groups accounts by their ties, not by the categories in a plan: a community is a set of accounts denser inside than outside, and the search keeps the split with the highest modularity Q. The marketing segment was declared from features or assumptions, while the three communities were discovered from who actually trades with whom, so they can differ without either being a mistake. The university-district group looks like a regional pocket, which the course frames as Kotler's measurable, reachable segment: you can count it, and its ties are the channels to reach it. Before acting, check the Q value and compare the groups with an outside variable such as location, because a high Q alone does not prove the groups mean something, and modularity can also merge small groups. If the district group holds up, it deserves its own campaign rather than being treated as part of one fiction segment.

P8.5 (Multiple choice) Which statement correctly contrasts Week 6 clustering with Week 8 community detection? (a) Both need the number of groups chosen in advance. (b) In community detection, two accounts are close when their features are similar. (c) K-means groups by features with k declared in advance; Louvain groups by ties and finds the number of groups itself. (d) Louvain uses account handles to decide where borderline accounts belong.

Show the answer

(c). K-means needs k declared and measures distance as feature similarity; Louvain uses only ties, finds the number of groups as part of its search, and measures distance as path length. (a) is wrong for Louvain. (b) describes clustering, not communities. (d) is the A012 trap: the algorithm reads ties, not handles, which is why the food-handled A012 landed with the city side.

Revision Framework checklist

The lecturer teaches much of this course as structures: lists, ordered steps, grids and decision tables. Every one of them sits in its chapter in a green box. Boxes labelled Core framework are the 53 big named structures that carry the course; know each of them by heart, with its items in the course's own words and in the right order. The other 93 boxes are smaller structures that are still worth recalling as lists.

To revise, read a framework's name, cover the line beneath it, and recite the items. Click any name to jump to the full explanation.

Last chapter How to write answers that earn the marks

The pattern behind almost every mark

Look at any Part B question in the mock guide and the 6 marks split the same way: two points of two marks each, plus a third point of two marks, and each two-mark point splits again into "state it" and "connect it to the scenario". When the question involves data, the points almost always come out as four moves. First, the number, worked out from the exhibit. Second, what that number means in plain words. Third, the decision it supports. Fourth, one honest limit on that decision. If your answer makes all four moves in three to five sentences, you have usually covered the marking scheme without seeing it.

Take mock question B2 as an example. The review queue holds 16 + 20 = 36 messages, which is 12 more than the team can check (the number). Raising the lower boundary to 0.50 sends the 16 messages in that band straight to "keep", so the queue falls to 20 and fits the capacity (what it means and the decision). But some of those 16 may be spam, so missed spam could rise, and the table cannot say by how much because it has no true labels (the limit). That is four sentences and all 6 marks. Notice what the guide refuses to reward: calling all 16 messages spam. You only say what the evidence supports.

Exam tip

Before you write, underline every number in the question and every limit it states, such as "at most four predictors" or "a team of three can review 24 messages". The marking almost always checks that you used each of them. A limit stated in the question is a hint that the "obvious" answer is not allowed.

The limits the examiners keep rewarding

The fourth move, the limit, is the one students forget, and it is often worth a whole mark. Over and over, the mock rewards the same small set of honest cautions. A model that settled is not the best possible model: a converged K-means run is the best among the starts you tried, not proof that no start could do better. A model that fits the past closely may predict the future badly: lower training error is not lower prediction error, and you compare models on the same new weeks. Errors that sum to zero are not zero errors: a miss of +10 and a miss of −10 cancel in the sum but not in the squared total of 200. A small sample is limited evidence: three validation weeks cannot settle the question for good. Counting users is not counting activity: registrations can double while active ties stay flat. And a comparison is only fair if everything else is held equal: the same network, the same probabilities, the same budget, the same number of rounds, repeated many times.

Learn these as sentences you can drop into almost any answer. "This is the best result among the runs we tried, not proof of the best possible result." "This compares fit on the data used for training, not prediction on new data." "With only three weeks this is limited evidence, so I would validate further." "These comments support investigation, not a claim about how often the problem occurs among all users."

Trap

The guide says that "all models are imperfect" earns nothing. A limit only scores when it is specific to the case and explains its consequence. "Only three validation weeks, so a different season could favour model Q" is a limit. "More data is always better" is not.

Using the frameworks in a written answer

The frameworks in this book are not only lists to recognise in multiple choice. In Parts B and C they give your answer its skeleton, and markers find a structured answer easy to credit. When a question touches a framework, name it in your first sentence, walk through its items in order, and apply each item to the scenario's facts. "Using the six factors: firm strength is limited, product and market homogeneity are low, rivals are many, so concentrated targeting fits" scores better than "they should target one segment".

Three habits help. First, use the course's own words for the items (measurable, substantial, reachable, actionable; not your synonyms), because the marking guide lists them in those words. Second, when a framework has an order, keep it: STP comes before the 4Ps; in cleaning, duplicates come before missing values and units come before outliers; on the analytics ladder, you describe before you diagnose. Third, when the facts point in different directions, say which item decides and why, as in Lumen Beauty's targeting choice, where limited firm strength outweighs the other factors.

The framework checklist lists every framework in one place. In the last days before the exam, cover the item lines and try to recite each framework from its name alone.

Part A: how to avoid losing easy marks

Multiple-choice questions in this course are rarely about obscure facts. They test whether you can apply a definition exactly to a tiny example. A 1/0 basket table records presence, not quantity, so three print packs are still just a 1. "Ten days" is not "less than seven days", so you follow the "no" branch of a tree. Eighty shares are twice forty shares because a count has a true zero, which makes it a ratio scale. A component is a group with no path to the rest, and moving the drawing around adds no edge. When two options look right, the wrong one usually confuses two neighbouring ideas: support with confidence, precision with recall, Ridge with LASSO, tokenizing with lemmatizing, degree with betweenness, a cluster with a community. Read every option against the exact words of the definition.

Remember the mechanical rule too. You circle your choice and write its letter in the answer grid, and if they differ, the grid is what gets marked. Leave two minutes at the end to compare them. Answer every question in English and label each written answer with its question number.

Part C: working through a case

A case gives you a scenario, one or two exhibits, and three sub-questions with their own marks, usually 8, 6 and 6. Treat the marks as a guide to how many distinct points each part needs. An 8-mark question wants about eight scored elements. In the mock's Apriori question, that meant the minimum count, then each candidate kept or discarded with its reason, then the warning that surviving the pruning is not the same as being frequent.

Show your working the way the guide marks it: set up the formula with the numbers in it, then give the result. If you set it up correctly but slip on the arithmetic, you still get the method mark, and a later step that correctly follows your earlier wrong number is not penalised twice. So never skip the setup line, even when you can do the sum in your head. Put units on everything: orders, squared orders, viewers, percent.

When a sub-question asks you to choose, say your choice in the first sentence and then justify it with the comparison: "P, because its squared prediction error on the same three later weeks is 200 against Q's 650." Then add the limit. If you think an alternative is defensible, the guide often accepts it, as long as it meets the stated conditions and the evidence supports it.
